PREFACE AND ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
I was first alerted to a way of thinking quite
different from the liberal, middle-class, Quakerly
tradition in which I had been raised, when I was a
student of Social and Public Administration in Oxford
(1967-1969). My "conversion" was the result of reading
Herbert Marcuse 1 s One-Dimension·a1 Man, for what I found
this book was a secular analysis of contemporary social
conditions, whose results seemed to converge completely
with the religidus concerns that had hitherto shaped
my life. Soon after my entry to Syracuse University in
1969 tc study for an M.A. in sociology, I read for the
first time Marx's 1844 manuscripts, and discovered the
source of Marcuse I s inspiration. However it wa_s ·not
until I began preparing for my dissertation proposal
at the University of California at Berkeley, that I
first appreciated, thanks to the guidance and encourage-
ment of Professor Gertrude Lenz er (Who was visiting
professor at Berkeley, 1973-1974) to what extent Marx
had drawn on Adam Smith. However, almost as soon as I
had submitted my dissertation proposal in May 1974, I
was beset by doubts. I dutifully began making a biblio-
graphy of the secondary literature: how could I possibly
master all the discussion that had already been devoted
to interpreting Marx's 1844 manuscripts? How could I
submit a dissertation without doing this? I attended
lectures and seminars on Hegel: how could I possibly


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
ix
grasp Hegel• s complex philosophical system? How could I
possibly begin my analysis of Marx without such a grasp?
In August, under a grant from the University of Cali-
fomia, I travelled to Amsterdam to visit the Institute
where these manuscripts are kept. There I learned of
the eccentric pagination, the page division and other
physical aspects of Marx's manuscripts that gave me
the idea of approaching Marx's text by exploring these
unknown features of the original manuscripts.
On my return to Berkeley, however, in the fall of
197 4, I found myself unable to piece together the
information about the physical features of Marx's ori-
ginal manuscripts into a coherent approach for under-
standing the text. I went into complete despair and
like so many students gave up the dissertation I had
undertaken and searched around for another.
A year later, I had the opportunity of team-teaching
an introductory course on Marx and Socialism entitled
"Who was Marx? What is Socialism? n at the East Bay
Socialist School in Oakland, California. This was a
most rewarding experience which 1I1ade r!l.e realize that my
academic concerns could be put to political practice
through the medium of exposing paople to Marx's thought
as clearly and as undogmatically as possible. Further-
more, my consistent tendency to go back to Adam Smith
to clarify Marx served the function of faStening Marx's
thought on to concepts and categories that students


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
X
were already familiar with, ideas that they had already
through their education and the liberal American press.
I was sufficiently encouraged to turn my thoughts once
again to my original dissertation proposal. In May 19 76,
I presented a paper at the Adam Smith Bicentennial, a
very crude and shortened version of the main evidence
and argument presented in this dissertation, and did
not find the academic response at all encouraging ..
In the summer 1976, I left Berkeley to take up a
job in Germany with the primary intention of developing
the linguistic skills necessary to read Marx in the
original. I enrolled as a student in the Philosophy
Department of Munich University and explained my knowl-
edge of Hegel and Feuerbach by attending university
seminars and lectures. Early in 1977, I received an
offer from Science and Society to publish the paper
that I had delivered at the Adam Smith Bicentennial.
My rewriting of this paper for publication brought in-
creasing doubts about my hypothesis and in the end I
did not submit it for publication.
The dissertation that I am now submitting expresses
these doubts. The earlier chapters present new infor-
mation about the physical aspects of Marx's original
manuscripts and express the conviction that any inter-
pretation of Marx 1 s text must take into account these
aspects. The later chapters offer my own interpretation
that ci.oes take into account the physical aspects of


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
Marx's First Manuscript, but I myself find this
interpretation unsatisfactory. After six years of
struggle I realize, however, that I, as a single
individual, can go no further with the new evidence.
I am submitting my dissertation at a time when my
doubts are greater than my convictions.
To the many who helped me and participated in my
struggle, I offer my thanks.
xi
First and foremost, my thanks go to Dr. Gertrud
Lenzer, who suggested ~is topic to me way back in
1972, who, during her year as vi.siting professor in
the Department of Sociology at the University of
California, in Berkeley (1972-1973), devoted an enour-
mous amount of her time to prepare me to undertake
this project, and from whom I learnt how to appropriate
for myself the insights of others. She was the first
teacher I ever had who put me through a systematic
reading of the corpus of Marx's writings.
To members of my dissertation committee Profs.
Philippe Nonet (Chairperson 1974-1976), Barbara Heyns
(committee :member 1976-79), Neil Smelser (committee
member 1974-76 and Chairperson 1976-1979) and Martin
Jay (committee member 1974-79) for their encouragement
t.'lroughout these five years and especially for their
continuing faith in my ability to carry out this pro-
ject during my own periods of despair and overwhelming
sense of inner inadequacy and outer visible stagnation.

xii

xiv
verbalized ideas, her careful monitoring of the ideas
I wrote up, her continual insistence that I try to
work to an outline and organize these incoherent

Last but not least to those who before I ever began

to whom I owe the quakerly upbringing that made it

the bitter end whatever one untertakes, and who gave
me every encouragement in that undertaking, especially

To my grandfather, Professor E-:ne.:-itus of Economic
History at Cambridge, who died before I ever had a

from whom I inherited the most importari c element in
rey understanding of Marx.
To my classics teachers and tutors at Cambridge,
where I first learnt the basic issues in philosophy and


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
xv
politics, through a thorough study of Greece and Rome,
and the importance of textual criticism for appreciating
what great men and women have written. And in particular
to my tutors at Newnham College, Joyce Reynolds and
Pat Easterling, whose continues enthusiasm for my work,
long after I had (ostensibly) left all my classics trai-
ning behind me and launched into the enterprise of
social science, encouraged me to apply to Marx the same
scholarly respect, thoroughness and consistency that
the academic establishment had always accorded to Plato
and Aristotle.
To those who were able to do what is normally re-
garded as impossible in today's society, to teach a
female mind to think not only logically but also dia-
lectically: These include my Greek philosophy tutors
at Cambridge (1964-66); George o 'Brien, of Gonville &
Caius College and John Easterling of Trinity College,
whose patient clarification of my errors in trying to
write about Plato and Aristotle exposed me to the im-
portance of asking the questions which would lead
somewhere, and taught me how to ask those questions.
To Larry Blum, my colleague at Oxford, who inspired
me by sharing his political thoughts, convictions and
activities with me.
To Herbert Marcuse and T. B. Bottomore, neither of
whom I ever met: One-Dimensional Man (1968) by the one
and the very readable translation of Marx I s Early


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
xvi
Writings (1970) by the other were the earliest conscious
landmarks in the chain of events that drove me to devote
five years of my life to this project, something my
par:-ents have never been able to understand.
This long list of acknowledgements, which represents
only the tip of the iceberg of those who made this
dissertation possible, is simply one way of saying that
the work I have undertaken and here present is less
the result of a well-planned and consciously executed
research design than a unique cluster of social rela-
tions which neither I nor any other individual is in
a position to determine through anticipatory planning
or conscious control. I leave my readers to decide
for themselves about the validity of this evolutionary
product of combined efforts, both purposeful and
random.

CHAPTER I
Introduction:
Does the World Really Need yet
Another Interpretation of Ma:::::x' s
Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts
of 1844?
1. 1
Who?: The Smi thian Mar~
Imagine a young political journalist in his mid-twen-
ties, forced to flee his mother country because of harsh
state censorship, settling down in his new home in a
foreign city to a course of self-education in economics.
He had already distinguished himself as a brilliant stu-
dent in the formal education system of his own country,
starting off, in accordance with his father's wishes,
as a student of law in Bonn, and finishing up in Berlin
with a doctoral dissertation on Greek philosophy. He was
well-trained in the methods of independent study, master-
ing the contents of the books he read - carefully copy-
ing-out, paraphrasing, and sunnnarizing their major argu-
ments and interspersing them with his own critical com-
ments and reactions. After taking his Ph.D. in 1842 in
Berlin, where his original and systematic thinking had
corrnnanded the respect of many of his professors and


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
2
colleagues, he became editor of a _journal which had been
founded in Cologne by a group of weal thy liberals to
serve as a platform for the critical discussion of cur-
rent social, political. and economic issues. As a jour-
nalist he did not abandon the skills he had developed
as a student, and continued copying out notes from works
on history, politics and social philosophy to help him
in his new vocation,
namely to analyse as accurately as
possible the historical and social significance of the
events on which he was reporting.
Now - the year was 1844 - in his new home, Paris, his
study of English political economy together with his ex-
posure to French socialism and the revolutionary political
history of France inspired a new understanding of con-
temporary European society and a radical reassesSID.ent of
all that he had studied, especially his training in phi-
losophy. This new understanding allowed him to develop a
revolutionary connection between the new industrial pro-
duction system of England• s thriving free uarket economy
and the philosophical products of Germany's speculative
thinkers. In scratchy handwriting, he carefully penned
his new line of thought in a couple of notebooks. Though
at one time he had an interested publisher, these note-
books lay on his shelf and were never published in his
own lifetime. Almost a century later they were published
in Germany, and in 1959, they were translated for the
first time into English. The impact on social scientists


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
and philosophers, struggling to understand the basic
problems of the twentieth century and the cOndition of
modern technological society, was overwhelming - one
such philosopher has written:
The number of books and articles written about,
or referring to the Manuscripts of 1844 are
countless. They are unquestionably the most
talked about philosophical work in this century.
(Meszaros 1972, 11)
The main theme developed in these manuscripts - alien-
ation, or: more precisely alienated labour - quickly
passed into the vocabulary of the social sciences, psy-
chology, philosophy, even the ordinary household, as a
basic conceptual tool for making sense of life in the
twentieth century.
Who was this young journalist? lmd secondly, how did
he come to distil and develop an insight, which, a
century later,instead of being dismissed as obsolete,
irrelevant, and of antiquarian interest, should have
triggered off such excitement among people all over the
western world and stirred up so much debate among to-
day's intellectuals? The answer to the first question
is Karl Marx. 1}
The answer to the second question is t..11.e subject
of this dissertation.
At this point, the sophisticated reader, who has
long been exposed to a battery of di verse and conflict-
ing interpretations of Marx 1 s theory of alienated labour,
may be tempted to close this book with a groan: "My God,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
4
do we really need yet another interpretation of Marx's
early writings? Hasn't the debate over the young Hegel-
ian Marx long since been delared dead?" Now, it is cer-
tainly true that I am not the first to claim to have come
up with a statement of what the 26-year old Marx really
meant when he P'?'Ililed his "Economic and Philosophic
Manuscripts" of 1844. It is also true that the debate
over these manuscripts has gone into a lull, with the
different parties to the debate continuing to insist on
their own positions and dismissing their critics' ob-
jections as invalid or irrelevant. And it is certainly
true that the secondary literature is strewn with so
many remnants of this inconclusive debate that no one
in his/her right mind would dream of devoting their
energies to the unending, if not impossible, task of
fashioning this extant multitude of diverse and conflict-
ing contributions into a consistent and unified under-
standing of "what Ma:cx really meant".
But this is not the task that I am going to under-
take. I am not going to drag the reader through the oft-
repeated pros and cons of issues that have been sub-
jected to protracted debate and through sheer repetition

detail the hitherto ignored or unknown evidence about
the group of documents written by Marx in 1844, whose
contents have been published under the title Marx's
"Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts of 1844 11 , inform-

ation which is only available to those who have the

Some of this information may be gleaned from the
standard editions of Marx's Economic and Philosophic
Manuscripts (EPM), but the brief descriptions offered
there of some of the features of Marx 1 s original manu-

why I subject my
readers throughout this book to so much det.ail about
the immediately visible features of Marx's original

not been documented anywhere else. But more importantly
- and this is the reason why I plunge my readers straight-

it is precisely this ignored evidence that undermines the
whole debate on the "Hegelian Marx", for this debate has
so far been conducted on the basis of the misleading
published versions of these manuscripts. The evidence
that I document in section 2, subsection (iii) directly
contradicts - or at least calls into question - the
shared assumptions underlying even the most di verse and
conflicting positions that have so far been adopted on


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
the hotly debated issue of the meaning and significance

As I shall demonstrate in the chapters that follow,
the debate over the "Hegelian Marx" is not the appro-
priate approach to appreciating the significance of
'Marx's Economic and Philosophic .:1anuscripts of 1844".
It is true that by 1844, Marx had so thoroughly mastered
Hegel's philosophy that he
won the public admiration
of his professors and other older and more senior con-
temporaries. But the key to understanding the 1844
manuscripts and their significance does not lie so much
in the 26-year-old Marx's firm and long-established
grasp of Hegelian philosophy, but rather in his then
very recent exposure to the writings of English poli-
tical economy, and in particular in his expcsure to
the founding work of classical liberal economics, Adam
Smith's Wealth of Nations. To the sophisticated reader
who poses the question: "What new is there to say about
Marx's 1844 Manuscripts?'~ my come-back would be, "Tell me
what you know about the Smi thian Marx of 1844 ?" If my
sophisticated readers' knowledge of Marx's "Economic
and Philosophic Manuscripts of 1844"
rests only on
what they have read in the secondary literature, or
even on a careful reading of the available published
texts of these manuscripts, t.'len I doubt if they will
have much to say. What I have to say on the subject
is contained in my present work, which not surprisingly

bears the title The Influence of Adam Smith on Marx I s

unity of Marx 1s theory of alienation, namely Adam Smith's

in 1776 as the Wealth of Nations.
I offer my readers the "Smithian Marx 11 , a Marx that
rests on far more solid, objective evidence than any
of the justifications for the various "Hegelian Marxes"

And - if I may plagiarize the Marx who wrote Capital I -

1.2 What?: Marx's Paris Writings of 1844
The title "Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts of
1844° was given to the contents of two of Marx's note-
books and two sets of four pages that accompanied these

notebooks, when they were made public for the first time
in 19 32.
I will henceforth refer to these four manuscripts
by the acronymic abbreviation EPM, rather than by the
more popular short titles such as Marx• s 1844 Manuscripts,
the Paris Manuscripts, the Early Writings, all of which
misleadingly suggest on the one hand that this is all
that has survived from Marx's studies during his 15-
month exile in Paris from November 1843 to the beginning
of February 1845, and on the other hand that Marx com-
pleted all four manuscripts during this period. (The
evidence suggesting that Marx 1 s thinking and writing

are presented in Appendix 4). Most of Marx's work from
the Paris period 0£ his life has come down to us in
the form of handwri t":.en notebooks containing excerpts
from other writers. The contents of these excerpt~note-

same volmne as the EPM (i.e. the MEGA edition of 1932),
but unlike the· ~, they have rarely been republished
and there is no edition of them in the English language.
Hence I shall devote the first part of TD.Y own intro-

Section 1.2.1 will briefly describe Marx's excerpt-note-
books on political economy compiled in 1844 and indicate
their significance for understanding the contents of
the EPM. Section 1. 2. 2 will present a summary of the
contents of the EPM, while section 1. 2. 3 is devoted to
a description of the manuscripts as physical objects.

1.2.1 Marx's Excerpt Notebooks of 1844 on Political
Economy
That Marx's excerpt-notebooks of 1844 l':ave received
so little attention is scarcely surprising. For they
are like many similar notebooks which Marx compiled
throughout his life and which are equally little known.
For the most part, all that they contain are summaries,
paraphrases, and (most frequently of all) word-for-word
quotations from books that Marx was studying and digest-
ing at the time. Apart from an occasional comment in
which Marx expresses his own insights or criticisms,
these notebooks contain little of Marx's own original
thinking. For Marx himself, however, they must have
been extremely important in the process of developing
his own ideas. They not only provided him with a record
of the most important writings that had influenced him,
a record that could again and again be referred to, but
more importantly, they enabled him to penetrate the un-
spoken assumptions of other theorists. For the method
of identifying and copying out key passages, paraphras-
ing their context, and summarizing the less relevant
links in the argument, is an excellent way of laying
bare the fundamental logic, structure, and premises
underlying the work of others.
· From this point of view, the excerpt notebooks of
1844 are particularly important, because they contain
Marx's earliest studies of political economy, and


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
10
therefore record his first intellectual exposure to the
social reality which was to preoccupy him for the rest
of his life, namely the operation of the capitalist
economy. These studies of 1844 begin with two works by
French economists (J.B. Say and F. Skarbek), and con-
tinue with a French translation of Adam Smith's Wealth
of Nations. (At that time, Marx had not yet mastered
English.) Other works on political economy, that Marx
studied during this period, as recorded in these excerpt-
notebooks, include a French edition of Ricardo's
Principles of Political Economy and Taxation with an
elaborate and critical coI!!Inentary by J. B. Say and the
French translation of the Elements of Political Economy
by James Mill (father of the more famous J. S. Mill}.
Marx's notes on Ricardo at this time, together with his
rather scornful comments on Say I s connnentary, provide
fascinating reading in view of the later influence of
Ricardo's labour the:,ry of value on Marx. Marx• s excerpt-
notes on James Mill's Elements contain two extended
comments, expressing Marx's own original ideas. These
comments, the first 0ver six pages long, the other three
pages long, have been translated into English and in-
cluded in a collection of the writings entitled Writings
of the Yotmg M:a·rx On Ph"ilo·sophy· ·and Society {Easton and
Guddhat 1967), where they were given the titles "Money
and Alienated Man" and
11Free Human Production" respec-
tively. Both of these cormnents provide a valuable com-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
11
plement to the concept of alienated labour, developed
in Marx's ~, especially the second comment which con-
cludes with an analytical sketch of unalienated labour.
I will refer again to the excerpt-notebooks on Smith,
for many of the passages that ?A.arx copied out from the
Weal th of Nations in those notebooks reappear in the
EPM.
Al though Marx' s excerpt-notebooks are most s igni fi-
can t for the record that they provide of Marx's first
confrontation with political economy, they also record
his continued study of history, politics and philosophy,
mainly with reference to France and the French Revolution.
Many of these notebooks are even more inaccessible than
the political economy excerpt notebooks. They have not
been published and the interested reader has to rely on
photocopies of the original manuscripts, which, because
of Marx's notoriously illegible hand, require years of
practice before one can decipher them, let alone read
them with ease.
All of the surviving notebooks, recording Marx's
studies during his 15 months in Paris -
and which re-
mained unpublished during his own lifetime and for half
a century after his death - are now in the keeping of
the International Institute for Social History in Amster-
dam. However at the time that the EPM and the excerpt-
notes recording Marx's earliest studies of political
economy were made available to the public for the first
time in 1932, the original manuscripts were not in Am-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
12
sterdam but in the Berlin archives of the German Social
Democratic Party, which had gradually ever since Engels 1
death in 1895 acquired many of Marx's literary remains.
This party was in the process of publishing a complete
collection of Marx' and Engels' writings, a collection
which is now known as the MEGA edition (acronymic ab-
breviation of Marx/Engels Ge·samtausgabe). The volume
in which the EPM and Marx's excerpt notes of 1844 on po-
litical economy appeared also contained Marx and Engels'
first joint publication, The Holy Family (fi.rst pub-
lished in February 1845} and was subtitled nThe Holy
Family and Writings of Marx from the beginning of 1844
to the beginning of 1845". It formed volume 3 of Part I
of the MEGJl. series and was brought out by the Marx.-
Engels publishing house in Berlin shortly before Hitler's
rise to power in Germany. Soon afterwards the German
Social Democratic Party was banned, the publishing house
closed, and the archives containing Marx's literary
remains removed for safe keeping to Amsterdam. They have
remained there ever since. The MEGA edition of 1932,
which made public for the first time many of Marx's
previously unpublished writings from his 15-month exile
in Paris, has never been reprinted.
I myself have not come across any other edition of
Marx I s collected writings that carry the contents of
his excerpt-notebooks of 1844, nor am I aware that they
have ever been translated out of the languages, French
and German, that Marx himself used - with the exception

of the excerpt-notes on James Mill (mentioned above) ,

cessibility, the contents of Marx's excerpt notebooks

not been exploited by scholars researching the much
more famous and accessible ~
- all that appears in
most of the secondary literature is the bare mention of

From my point of view, however, Marx's excerpt note-
books of 1844 provide a valuable tool for tackling the
problem of how to approach and understand the much-de-
bated contents of the ~· While the former undoubtedly

they contain for the most part only copied-over passages

It is hardl.y necessary to assure the reader
who is familiar with political economy that
m'I conclusions are the fruit of an entirely
empirical analysis, based upon a careful cri-

63)
Thus, Marx himself indicates that any reader who is

interested in how Marx arrived at his theory of alien-

The Content of Marx I s Economic and· Philosophic
MazfU:Scri.pts· o·f 1844
In contrast to the rel.a ti ve inaccessibility of Marx's
excerpt notes of 1844, the EPM, which date from the same
period of Marx• s life, have been translated into many
languages and there are at least four English versions
currently in print. 2 ) These manuscripts, in t.'1eir origi-
nal foJ:In as penned by Marx, consist of two notebooks
(the First Manuscript and the Third Manuscript), two
separate sheets which form a continuous text of four
pages (Second Manuscript) and a double-folio, which like-
wise forms a continuous text of four pages (Fourth Manu-
script} .. All in all, then, what is designated by the
title Marx 1 s nEconomic and Philosophic Manuscripts of
'.1844n is a total of four manuscripts, which in their
original fo:an constituted approximately 76 pages of hand-
written text. (See Appendix 5) This title does not
appear anywhere in Marx's own manuscripts but was in-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
15
vented by the editors of the MEGA, who published their
written text for the first time in 19 32, to characterize
the topics that Marx deals with on these pages. Like-
wise the division of the written text into chapters and
the chapter headings were also the invention of the MEGA
editors. Almost every publication of the EPM (the main
exception being Landshut's edition of 1953) has followed
the MEGA chapter division~ and reproduced the chapter
headings (with slight variations). This division of the
written text into chapters has obscured the underlying
unity and careful structure of Marx's own argument. I
will therefore ignore it in rrrf own summary of this ar-
gUlllent, and elsewhere in my book -
e:v::cept inasfar as it
serves the function of readily referring the reader to
which part of the text I am discussing.
The overall title invented for the written contents
of Marx I s 76 pages does indeed indicate in a very general
way what the text deals with. But we can be much more
specific: the task that Marx undertakes on these pages
is on the one hand a critical examination of the politi-
cal economy and the phil.osophy of his time and on the
other hand a pioneering breakthrough to a radicall.y new
perspective £or understanding the hidden links between
these two areas of the intellectual. di vision of labour.
Marx's critical examination reveals on the one hand the
limits, the inadequacies, and the internal contradictions
of the cl.assical liberal approach to the study of eco-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
16
nomic reality, and on the other hand, the failure of
idealist philosophy to make good its claim that the
self-conscious exercise of reason, reasoning that ?:"e-
flects on its own processes of reasoning can provide
a satisfying explanation of the contemporary social
reality. The new perspective that Marx develops in the
process of this critical examination is radical in the
literal sense of the Latin word, radix {meaning root)
from which this word is derived, for it goes to the roots
of both the study of political economy and the philoso-
phical approach to understanding the human condition.
Thus Marx's first notebook is ~
in that it
is primarily occupied with an examination of the ~
category - the commodity and its components wages, pro-
fit, and rent - that structures the founding work of
classical liberal economics, Adam Smith's ~
~
and on which the whole tradition of political
economy has been based ever since the publication of
this work in 1776. In the other notebook, Marx devotes
over ten pages to a
thorough examination of a short
4-sentence passage from Hegel I s Pheno!!lenology of Mind,
first published in 1809, a worJc in which Hegel lays out
the historical proof for the accuracy of his philosophi-
cal approach to the world. The four sentences that Marx
examines contain the distilled essence of Hegel's answer
to the question posed by Kant's Critique of Pure Reason:
"How is it possible to have true and accurate knowledge
of anything outside our own minds'? How is it even possible


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
17
to have true and accurate knowledge of ourselves?" This
question, as Hegel was fully aware, is the fundamental
question that must be answered in order to justify any
scientific activity.
Thus Marx is not only radical; he is also specific.
He concentrates his critique of the inadequacy of poli-
tical economy to grasp contemporary social reality on
specific passages of Adam Smith's Wealth of Nations,
treating these as the crucial premises on which the whole
of political economy rests or falls. Likewise his cri-
tique of the philosophical justification of all scienti-
fic activity is concentrated on the last chapter of
Hegel's Phenomenology of Mind, from which the 4-sentence
passage is taken. The very title of Hegel I s chapter
"Absolute Knowledge" indicates the assurance and cer-
tainty with which Hegel felt he had decisively refuted
Kant 1 s argument that the scientific and philosophizing
mind cannot have true and accurate knowledge about
anything. Marx's decisive refutation of Hegel in turn
does not go back to Kant's position of despairing at the
incapacity of the human mind to understand its histori-
cal world, but moves beyond the whole philosophical
assumption that understanding is primarily an intellec-
tual activity.
The rest of the notebook in which Marx develops his
critique of Hegel's approach to understanding the world
(the Third Manuscript) is devoted to sketching in Marx's
own answer to the questions: "How can we understand our


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
18
present s.ocial reality and our present state of knowl-
edge? Under what conditions can we arrive at a true and
accurate knowledge of ourselves and the world about us?"
For Marx, the basis of human understanding does not
1.ie in the intellectual pursuit of truth, in the endeavor
of human beings, both as individuals and as cultural
collectivities, to satisfy their intellectual curiositi.es,
but in the practical activity through which human beings
shape not only their cultural and social environment, but
al.so their physical environment. The endeavours of human
beings to satisfy their physical needs, an activity which
involves manual as well as mental labour, not only provide
the basis for answering the questions of the intellect;
these endeavours are also the source which generates and
formulates the intellect's questions. For Marx, the only
adequate answer to Kant I s dilemma - that the external
world {in Kant's terminology, the thing-in-itself) can
never be grasped by the i.nternal operations of the reason-
ing self-consciousness {in Kant's terminology, the "I-
think") - is the abolition of thinking as an independent
and self-contained activity, the abolition of the type
of activity that in and of itself effects no change in
the external world and has no impact on. it. And the pre-
condition for abolishing thinking as an independent
specialized activity is the abolition of the whole system
of the division of labour in its present form. In other
words, the separation of mental from -manual labour must
be removed so that the activity of thinking can be re-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
19
integrated with the manual activity that sets in motion
the society's processes of production and reproduction.
For these processes are just as much necessitated by
the condition of being human as is the activity of
thinking ..
Marx's answer to the most fundamental question under-
lying all scientific activity, namely "How do we know
what we know?" draws upon the criticism that he has
levied against the answers to this question given im-
plicitly by political economy and explicitly by philoso-
phy - or, more precisely, the answers to be found in
Adam Smith's weal th of Nations and in Hegel's Phe-
nomenology of r-il.nd. But it does more than this~ for it
integrates tl':ese criticisms under one and the same con-
cept, alienation.
The concept of alienation forms one of the most cen-
tral and basic categories in Hegel 's description and
analysis of the historical development of human knowl-
edge. The whole of the Phenomenology of Mind is based
on a consistent application of the dialectical process
of alienation to the insightful analysis of an enormous
variety of human experiences, bot}-, individual and collec-
tive, through which human beings come to know themselves
and their world. But Heg:el treats the succession of
philosophical systems, which the history of the western
world has produced., as the developmental process under-
lying and fundamental to the history of the world it-
s elf. Marx's argument, on the other hand, is that the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
20
intellectual expression of the pursuit of truth cannot
be treated as independent of the historical reality in
which the intellectualizing philosopl".er lives and moves
and has his physical being. Hence it is impossible for
the philosopher to arrive ac the happy unalienated state
of "Absolute Knowledge" as long as the society which
produces the philosopher's food, clothing and lodging
imposes a life of alienated labour on the vast majority
of his contemporaries. To discover wl':.ether or not it is
really possible for human beings to overcome and trans-
form their alienated relationships to one another to
the outside p'h.ysical world
and to their own individual
selves
we must examine the nature of the society·' s
production processes. For these are the processes by
which the collective endeavours of human beings produce,
perpetuate and recreate their physical environment, their
social, political, and cultural institutions, and their
own individual personalities.
Hence
r!'.arx does not take Hegel 's account of histori-
cal reality - an account which traces the deveiopment
of human knowledge as the progressive alienation and
de-nlienation of self-consciousness - as his starting
point. Instead ~!arx' s First !·!anuscript - the only one
of the four EPM manuscripts to which Marx himself gave
a number - records w},at has been documented about the
historical reality of the society's production processes
by scholars who have systematically examined the capi-
talist economy. From this record, r1arx develops an


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
21
entirely different interpretation of what the political
econorr~sts have documented~
By ruthlessly uncovering the contradictory ways in which
Adam Smith and those who followed him had used the same
categories to describe very different phenomena, Marx
calls into question the basic categories that Sm.i th de-
veloped to analyse the free play of market forces~ These
basic categories are the exchangeable value of the
commodity and the three components of that value, wages,
profits and rent, which in turn are based on the three
"factors of production" - labour, capital, and land -
required to create a commodity~
From the contradictory accounts given by Smith of
the way in which the value of the individual commodity
11resolves itself" into wages, profits, and rent, and
of the way in which the wealth of the whole society is
likewise divided among labourers, capitalists, and
landlords, Marx concludes that there is nothing inherent
in the physi·ca1
aspects of labour, capital, and land
that can account for this di vision. Rather, according
to Marx, the only way we can only understand the nature
and composition of the value of the individual commodity
is by refusing to take for granted the commodity-form
of the product. Instead we must examine the ~
organization of the production process in order to dis-
cover the real (i.e. social, not physical) determinants
of the division of the production-process into labour,
capital, and land, which at the same time are the real


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
22
determinants behind the redistribution of the wealth
of nations as wages, profits, and rent. This is what
Marx attempts to do by introducing a new category into
the study of political economy" - alienation -
and by
reexamining, with t11e aid of this new concept, the so-
cial reality which the science of political economy has
documented, albeit with inadequate and misleading
scientific categories. This social reality is the capi-
talist mode of production, still in its early stages
and still bearing traces of the social context in which
it was born,namely the feudal society of Europe.
What Marx is able to show, by applying the concept
of alienation to the phenomenon that the political eco-
nomists have analysed as the exchangeable value of the
commodity is this
As long as the social organization
of production is based upon the exchange of labour and
money - the exchange between the wage-labourer and the
employing capitalist - then the labourers are forced to
give up control of their own productive activity and
put it at the disposal of their non-labouring employers.
In this situation, the activity of production will
necessarily remain an activity beyond the control of
the agents themselves, a.."'l activity "belonging to another"
(the original meaning of the Latin "alienus"), in short
an activity of alienated labour. Marx develops his con-
cept of alienated labour to demonstrate how this re-
lationship between wage-earning employees and the em-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
23
players who buy and b.'1.ereby control their labour struc-
tures all the processes by which the society as a whole
perpetuates, reproduces, and c.;.anges itself. And, as a
result of the social organization of the production-pro-
cesses,
the very personalities of the individuals who
participate in these processes, both the individual
labourers and the individual. capitalists, are likewise
alienated, one-dimensional, and misshaped. Having ex-
plored, in his first notebook, the nature of alienation
in the real world where human beings have to produce
in order to guarantee the survival of themselves and their
species, Marx turns in his other notebook to assessing
the inadequacies - and also the strengths - of Hegel I s
account of alienation.
My characterization of the contents of the EPM, em-.
phasizing the central role of Marx's concept of alien--
ated labour as the unifying concept between Marx's
critique of the political economy and the philosophy
of his day, should now make it clear that the question
we posed at the beginning of this chapter: "How did
Marx come to develop, distil, and apply his insight in-
to the nature of labour in the capitalist world to-day,
the concept of alienated labour?" is the same as the
question: "What was Marx's line of thought as he was
in the process of pa:.."1.ing the whole of the EPM? What
were the steps in the argument that moved Marx from his
'careful critical study of political economy' to his

devastating critique of the basis on which all scienti-
tic activity had hitherto been conducted?"
The vital clues lie in the manuscripts themselves,
especially their least known parts. These are the be-
ginning {the first part of the first manuscript) and
the end (the forth manuscript) • The first two-thirds of
the first manuscript were characterised in the MEGA
edition of 1932 as having little or nothing to do with
the last third of this same notebook, where Marx intro-
duces and develops his concept of alienated labour. In-
stead these first 21 pages were to be regarded as be-
longing to an abandoned project, which just by coinci-
dence happered to have been penned in the same notebook
as the theory of alienated labour. As a result, there
are many editions of the EPM which omit the contents
of these pages, and it is particularly unfortunate that
the most popular and widely-read version of the EPM in
the English-speaking world, published as Marx's Concept
~
with a commentary by Erich Fromm, belongs to
this category of incomplete editions. However, even the
so-called complete English editions are not complete,
for they all omit Marx• s 4-page fourth manuscript. (See
Appendix 6) This is a double-folio sheet that was sewn
into the notebook known as the Third Manuscript, in be-
ween its centre pages, and I
know of no available
English translation of its contents. The original German
edition (MEGA 1932), which first published the contents
of the
EPM, included the fourth manuscript as an appen-

cannot be regarded as complete, for it om.its the first
21 pages of the first notebook!
These two parts of Marx's EPM, the beginning and
the end, bear the greatest similarity to Marx's similarly
ignored excerpt notes of 1844. About 80% of the written
content of Marx's first sixteen pages of his first
notebook are paraphrases, summaries, and - most fre-
quently of all - word-for-word quotations from other
writings, in particular from Adam Smith's Wealth of
~.
Similarly the four pages which comprise the
fourth notebook contain extensive extracts, most of
them carefully copied out word-for-word, and heavily
underlined, of one of the most important statements of
Hegel's philosophy - the last chapter of the ~
nplogy of Mind entitled "Absolute Knowledge".
However, despite the surface similarity of the first
16 pages of Marx's first notebook to both the fourth
manuscript and the excerpt notebooks, there are impor-
tant differences. These differences provide us with
a unique opportunity for understanding how Marx arrived
at his concept of alienated labour. They will be dis-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
26
cussed in detail in the next chapter. Here it is suffi-
cient to say that what is important about the first 16
pages of Marx's first notebook is not what he wrote, but
how he arranged what he wrote, for in the arrangement
of his excerpts from Adam Smith's Weal th of Nations
lies the structure of Marx's own critique of the classi-
cal political economy of his time. The words themselves
are Smith's (mainly), but their arrangement is distinc-
tively Marx's and reveals devastating inconsistencies
and self-contradictions in Smith's basic categories.
I
shall show in greater detail in the next chapters
how the first 21 pages of Marx's first notebook not
only precede, but generate Marx's famous concept of
alienated labour, laid out in the last 6 pages of this
same notebook. In other words, the first two thirds
of Marx's starting point, "a careful critical study
of political economy" first begun in his excerpt note-
books, and his most important and original contribution
to understanding the human condition in capitalist
society, namely the concept of alienated labour, pre-
sented in the last third of I-lc.n: • s first notebook.
To anyone familiar with other interpretations of
Marx's EPM, my characterization of the contents of
this manuscript will surely sound idiosyncratic, for
it presupposes a far greater unity, consistency
and
logical structure than any other intepretation currently
available in the secondary literature. Most corrunentators


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
27
have accepted the characterization of the EPM appended
to the- first publication of their written contents by
the editors of the 1932 MEGA edition. 3 ) The following
paragraph well expresses the taken-for-granted assump-
tions underlying the interpretations of the EPM offered
by
contemporary scholars since these manuscripts were
first made public:
Fragmentariness. As is well known, this work is
incomplete. The Manuscripts of 1844 range from
extracts from books, with brief comments on them,
through loosel.y connected notes and reflections
on various topics, to a more or less comprehen-
sive assessment of the Hegelian philosophy. While
it is relatively easy to understand the particu-
lar texts and passages, it is by no means easy
to see the guiding line of the work as a whole.
(Meszaros 1970, 12)
Thus it is universally accepted in the secondary lite-
rature that the BM are fragmentary and incomplete, de-
ficient of any overall structure. (Meszaros in fact
goes so far as to elaborate this last point, already
subsumed in his discussion of the "Fragmentariness"
of the EPM, as two further independent points, "Inade-
quacy of conceptual framework" and "Structure of Paris
Manuscripts".}
My own reason for rejecting these taken-for-granted
assumptions on which other scholars have based their
interpretations of the written content of the EPM is
quite simply that they are not born out by the manu-
scripts themselves, the fallacy of these assumptions
become clear, once we take into account not only the
written text, but also the physical features of the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
28
pages and notebooks in which Marx originally penned this
~- These physical features, whose significance has so far
been ignored by editors, commentators and interpreters
alike, provide the indispensable evidence for my own
interpretation of the written content;. In our: next sub-
section {iii) I will introduce the most significant phy-
sical features of the notebooks and pages which Marx
himself, with deliberate intent, mentally designed
and manually created.
1. 2. 3
Marx• s Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts of
1844 as Physical Objects
After reading the unsatisfactory and often conflict-
ing characterizations of Marx's original manuscripts
in the secondary literature, I made tne decision in
1974 to go to the International Institute for Social
History in F.rnsterdam and take a look at the manuscripts
myself. There I met and talked with the Director of
the Institute, Dr. Jurgen Rojahn, who was already
in the process of preparing a more accurate and detailed
description of the physical features of Marx's original
manuscripts. This description will be included in a
forthcoming edition of the EPM to be published in
Moscow in 1979. Because of the fragility of the manu-
scripts and the danger of further deterioration, I did
not insist on seeing or touching the original manu-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
29
scripts penned by Marx. Instead I found that the infor-
mation provided by Dr. Royalen, together with photo-
copies of all the pages of Marx's written text, was
sufficient to appreciate the significance of the unknown
physical features of Marx's notebooks and to call into
question the conventional wisdom. Throughout my research
I have worked with photocopies of the original EPM and
relied on Dr. Royalen I s help.
The following pages will require my readers to excer-
cise not only their attention but also their imagination
and to work up an enthusiasm for some dry and (at first
sight) pedantically obscure facts about tatty old docu-
ments, whose written contents are far more exciting.
However, it is precisely in order to appreciate how very
exciting the contents are, that I urge my readers to
work up this enthusiasm and bear with me through the
detailed description of the physical aspects of Marx's
EPM. For by paying careful attention to the details of
the physical objects that resulted from Marx's efforts
to externalize his internal processes of thought, we
get a far more complete and accurate picture of how
Marx' s mind was working at the time when he penned his
theory of alienated labour. This theory was by no means
the result of a stroke of inspiration that only geniuses
can hope to entertain. Rather it was I as Marx himself
stated, the results of a careful and critical study,
which any reader can undertake for him/herself. This


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
30
will be demonstrated in my later chapters. Here my main in-
tention is to get rid of the taken-for-granted, but un-
proven, assumptions of XXth. century editors and commen-
tators which have caused most readers of the EPM to give
up the attempt to reach a full understanding and appre-
ciation of what Marx was up to in these rnar.uscripts.
1. 2. 3 .1
TWO Notebooks and Two Sets of Four Paaes
The group of writings known as Marx's EPM of 1844
consists of two notebooks and two sets of four pages.
(See the following diagrams and Appendix 2.)
One of the notebooks has a "title" page, in the
sense that the outermost page bears the word ~
(notebook I) and nothing else. This notebook is known
as the first manuscript. The other notebook has no such
"title" page : the written text begins immediately on
the outermost page, which is numbered I. This notebook
is known as the Third Manuscript. In both notebooks,
the pages which carry a written text are numbered with
a Roman numeral in Marx's hand. In the published version
of the ~, these Roman numerals are inserted into the
printed text - though not always correctly - to indi-
cate Marx's pagination of his original manuscripts.
I, too, will use Roman numerals to refer to the pages in
Marx's two notebooks. There are 27 nurebered pages in
the first notebook (I through XXVII) and nine unnumbered
pages; all of these nine unnumbered pages are blank


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
31
except for the outermost title page and the third un-
numbered page, which carries a booklist. {See Appendix
3.) On this third unnumbered page, Marx listed and
numbered 22 bibliographic i terns. In the other notebook
there are 41 numbered pages, all of which carry a written
text, and twenty-three unnumbered pages, which are
totally void of any writing.
The lines of writing on the pages of Marx's notebook
run parallel to the "spine" of the notebook, so that in
order to read the text we have to turn the notebook
with the spine furthest away from us, and "flip over"
the pages, rather than the more conventional operation
of "turning over" the pages that we are accustomed to
in reading most books today.

35
One of the sets of four pages is a single double
folio sheet folded in two and sewn into the middle of
the notebook known as the Third Manuscript. Though the
four sides of this double-folio are not numbered (un-
like the surrounding pages of the notebook proper) , the
written text on these four pages forms a continuous and
self-contained summary of Hegel's final chapter of his
Phenomenology of Mind, "Absolute Knowledge". This 4-page
"Hegel-excerpt" is the little-known Fourth Manuscript.
It was not included in the 1932 MEGA edition as part
of the main text of the _fil:M, but postponed to an appen-
dix (see p. 21 above) . Landshut' s 1953 edition of the
EPM is the only one I know of that has treated the
Fourth Manuscript as an integral part of the EPM. The
English translations of the §fil1_, with the exception of
Bottomore' s Karl Marx Early Writings, make no mention
of this fourth manuscript. And even Bottomore only re-
fers to it briefly in his translator's preface: he does
not include the written text in his translation of the
rest of the EPM, on the grounds that "it adds nothing
to the discussion of Hegel 1 s philosophy in the third
manuscript" (Bottomore 1964, (xviii)}. Since this is
a view I do not share, a translation of this Fourth
Manuscript appears in an appendix to this book.
The other set of four pages was not sewn into either
of Marx's notebooks. It consists of two sheets of paper 1
both sides of which are written on and numbered (unlike
the four written but unnumbered pages of the Fourth


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
36
Manuscript). However, the corners of the two sheets are
very worn and the page numbers are now scarcely de-
cipherable. These two separate sheets, carrying four
pages of continuous written text, are known as the
Second Manuscript. It is universally assumed (following
the commentary given in the 1932 MEGA edition (Bottomore,
xvii)) that these two sheets formed the last pages of
a notebook which has been lost.
This much information about the physical aspects of
Marx's original manuscripts can be found in the editors 1
prefaces to most editions of the ~
(see for example,
Bottomore (1964), xvii-xviii and Struik {1964), 3-4).
And to a certain extent these physical features of
Marx's manuscripts are reproduced in the published texts.
Thus for example the published texts follow the.physi-
cal di vision of Marx's manuscripts by presenting the
written text as First Manuscript, Second Manuscript,
and Third Manuscript. {The chapter division of each
manuscript, however, like the chapter headings, which
appear in almost all the printed versions of the EPM
(Landshut's 1953 edition being the only exception I
know of) , is not to be found in Marx's original note-
books, but was the invention of the editors of the
MEGA edition of 1932.) And secondly, the Roman numerals
of Marx's own pagination of his text are inserted in
parentheses in the printed texts.
On the basis of the tri-colurnnar format ( the
commodity structure directly


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
37
appropriated from .2!,..darn Smith's analysis of the commodity
in the Wealth of Nations) of all the pages of Marx's
First Manuscript, together with the many passages and
paraphrases from Adam Smith's work that fill the pages
I -
XVI of this manuscript, I propose to call the First
Manuscript
Marx's Smithian notebook, in contrast to
the Third Manuscript, which carries Marx's critique of
Eegel. Hegel is nowhere explicitly mentioned in the
Smi thian notebook, the notebook which Marx himself in-
dicated on its outermost page ("Notebook I") was to
to be taken as his point of departure. Since, however,
the other notebook (the Third Manuscript) not only con-
tinues Marx's critique of Adam Smith and the political
economy that Smith's work gave rise to, but also
contains Marx's most important and explicit critique
of the ''birthplace of Hegel's philosophy" (Hegel's
Phenomenology of Mind, in particular the last chapter
"Absolute Knowledge") I shall call the Third IY'ianuscript,
Marx's Heaelian notebook.
1.2.3.2
What we read in the nrinted text is in many
places not what Marx himself wrote or intended.
Marx's two notebooks and two sets of four pages -
making a total of approximately 76 pages of written
text - are penned in black ink on pages that have now
turned yellow with age. Only a few of these pages have


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
38
deteriorated so that the original written text is im-
possible to read. However, Marx's handwriting is ex-
tremely difficult to read and the deciphering of indi-
vidual words has to rely in part on the ingenuity of
the reader, guided by the surrounding context. The German
editions of the EPM occasionally list alternative rea-
dings for particular words, but this by no means covers
all the ambiguities of Marx 's script.
Moreover, in many places Marx clearly had second
thoughts and crossed out words, inserted phrases - even
whole sentences and paragraphs - and added editorial
notes or symbols in the margin, some of which clearly
indicate that he wanted to rearrange his work in a way
that differed from how he had written it. Not all of
Marx's editorial symbols and notes in the margin have
been followed by the transcribers of the published text,
so that the sequence intended by Marx has in places
been lost. It has generally been taken for granted
that there is no self-evident structure or sequential
argument that binds together the contents of the four
manuscripts. This taken-for-granted assumption springs
to a large extent from the failure of the printed texts
to reproduce accurately the sequence that Marx himself
intended.
Since my own interest is in making Marx's own struc-
ture self-evident, I will be more concerned with the problem
of the precise sequence that Marx himself intended


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
39
than with the other problem, the ambiguity of individual
word.3. (Indeed the problem of deciphering individual
words can only be carried out if one has access to the
original manuscripts and not merely to photocopies of
those originals.) 41
1.2.3.3
surnmary of the Four Manuscriots as Physical
In summary then, the EPM in their original form as
written by Marx consist of two notebooks (the first
and third manuscript T and two sets of four pages ( the
second and fourth manuscript ) . The following are the
most significant things to remember about their physi-
cal aspects - for it is precisely these that have been
ignored, dismissed, or forgotten in the current editions
and interpretations of the contents of these manuscripts:
1) The First Manuscript was clearly intended by Marx
to be taken as his point of departure (for its outermost
page is labelled "~", i.e. "Notebook I"), and all
the pages of this manuscript are characterized by the
"commodity" structure. The notebook itself comprises
two self-contained, but mutually related parts: pages
I -
XVI and pages XVII -
XXVII.
The key to the complementarity of these two parts
lies in the commodity structure of the pages of both
parts.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
40
2) The Second Manuscript consisting of only 2 sheets
of paper written on both sides to form four pages, does
not constitute any evidence for the previous existence
of a notebook, which allegedly formed a bridge between
the two surviving notebooks (the first and third manu-
scripts).
3) The Third Manuscript was probably not written in the
same order that we now read it. If this is so, then the
notes at the beginning of this manuscript refer to -
and were probably intended to be inserted in - pages
XXXVI and XXXIX of this same manuscript.
4) The Fourth Manuscript exists and is an integral part
of Marx's work in 1844 that has come to be known as the
EPM.
1. 3.
How? From Adam Smith's Labour Theory of Value to
Marx's Theory of Alienation.
In Part I, we will present our evidence and lay out
our hypothesis. In chapters 2 and 3, we examine the
physical format of Marx's first notebook. This leads
us to hypothesize that the contents of this notebook
should be treated as a structural unity and that the
clue to this structure lies in Hegel's dialectical
method (chapter 4) and in Adam Smith's labour theory
of value (chapter 5).


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
41
The purpose of Part II (chapters 6-10) is to substan-
tiate this hypothesis by examining the text of Marx's
notebook. In chapters 6 and 7, we document firstly, the
extent to which the contents of the first p::t.rt of tJl.arx' s
notebook have their origins in Adam Smit.'1 1 s Wealth ol.
Nations, and secondly we try to demonstrate the way in
which Marx selected and reorganized different proposi-
tions from Smith in order to reveal the self-contra-
dictions in Smith's categories. In this way we hope to
substantiate our hypothesis that pages I -
XVI of the
notebook represent the second stage of the dialectical
method, i.e. the dialectical stage of negative reasoning
or the moment of immanent critique, and that the mate-
rial with which he was working, i.e. Adam Smith's Wealth
of Nations, represents the first stage, or the Level
of Understanding. Chapter 6 will be devoted to Marx's
analysis of wage-labour, a category that Marx notes on
the very first page of his notebook is uniquely affec-
ted by the division of the product of labour into three
separate components. Chapter 7 will be devoted to Marx's
analysis of the two other components, both rent and
profit, for, as we shall show, one and the same struc-
ture underlies Marx's discussion of these two categories.
Chapters 8-10 will examine the contents of pages
XVII-XXVII. According to our hypothesis, the contents
of these sheets represent the third step of the dialec-
tical method, the speculative stage of positive reason-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
42
ing.
According to Hegel, the task at this stage is to
identify the underlying principle, which Hegel calls
"the unity of opposites", which allows us to grasp the
contradictions inherent in the categories of the Under-
standing, contradictions which have been brought to
the surface by the dialectical stage of negative reason-
ing. In chapter 8 we argue that the new organizing prin-
ciple that Marx introduces for understanding the nature
of commodity-producing society is the "movement of pri-
vate property", and that private property conceived as
a movement, i.e. as the simultaneous acts of privation
and appropriation, is itself a "unity of opposites". We
show how Marx C.evelops this unity of opposites by locating
the historical origins of private property in the estab-
lishment of feudal society, a society which was based
on the alienation of land from those who worked the land
(chapter 9) • In this same chapter, we show how Marx uses
the concept of the movement of private property to show
the necessary continuity between the alienation of land
(private property in its feudal form) and the alienation
of labour (private property in its capitalist form). The
interpretation of alienated labour offered in chapter 10
takes as its starting point the contradictions that Marx
has uncoverd in Srni th' s concept of labour, and shows how
Marx reconceptualizes these contradictions to demonstrate
a further "unity of opposites", namly that what Ada..,1
Smith and other political economists had identified as


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
43
private property is merely the result and outward mani-
festation of the alienation of labour.
There is a concluding chapter (11} and a short epilogue.
Chapter 11 documents the results of our research into
the physical features of manuscripts II, III, and IV,
and offers some first impressions of how these may
corporated into an understanding of the contents of
these manuscripts. The epilogue ventures a few thoughts
on the significance of our interpretation of Marx's
First Manuscript for appreciating the continuity between
Marx's 1844 manuscripts and Capital volume I.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
44
NOTES CHAPTER I :
1)
2)
(Footnotes 1) and 2) are missing in
the original ms. )
3) The MEGA editors prefaced the first publication in
1932 of Marx's Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts of
.. l.~.!i .. JEPM) with the following general comment on the
fragmentary nature of both the written text and the
outward appearance of the four manuscripts. As mentioned
in t.CJ.is comment, they also prefaced the transcribed con-
tents of each of the manuscripts with further comments
on the specific details in the individual manuscripts
which allegedly corroborate the general impression of
fragmentariness (MEGA 1932, 30: trans.
MAF):
Apart from the excerpt notebooks of Marx's
Paris period (November 1843 to February 1845 -
see pages 410-412 in this volume) - other manu-
script fragments have survived from the studies
that Marx undertook and are most fully reported
by Marx himself in a short text t."1.at he entitled
"Preface". What is presented here are certainly
only fragments of more comprehensive studies on
political economy, law, moral philosophy, poli-
tics etc. which Marx drafted in Paris and then
made partial use of in the Holy Family. The out-
ward appearance of the four surviving manuscripts
confirm their fragmentary character. On the
following pages we reproduce Manuscripts I-III
(p. 39ff., 97ff., 107ff.) and include Manuscript
IV, a Hegel excerpt, in an appendix (p. 592ff.).
We introduce each of these manuscripts with
a short description of its outward appearance.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
45
NOTES CHAPTER I (con.) :
The sequence in which these manuscripts appear,
from I through III, probably corresponds to the
chronological sequence in which the manuscripts
were first written. In the case of the "Preface",
however, which cannot have been written before
August 1844, and was therefore certainly later
than Manuscripts I, II and most of III, we made
an exception and placed it at the beginninq- because
of its contents (p. 33ff.; cf. pp. 589ff.). In
addition we have placed the critical excursus
on the Hegelian philosophy, which is to be found
inserted in Manuscript III as three separate
sections and which undoubtedly originated at
the same time as the expositions of political
economy contained in the same notebook, at the
end, because it is clear from the "Preface"
that Marx planned to make this three-part ex-
cursus his final chapter.
In the correspondence between Marx and the
publisher, C. w. Leske of Darmstadt, there is
a contract dated 1. February 1845 and signed
by both parties, which provides for the pub li-
cation of a work entitled "Critique of Politics
and Poli ti cal Economy". The manuscripts that
have survived, however, present (apart from
the critique of Hegel) what are almost exclu-
sively attempts at a critique of political
economy. Moreover, even the "Preface" is
obviously related only to a critique of political
economy, and to all appearances refers precisely
to those studies, of which the manuscripts, pub-
lished here, have survived as fragments.
We have inserted all superscriptions or
restorations that we ourselves have made in
square brackets, and we have inserted the pagi-
nation of Marx's manuscripts, enclosed between
diagonal lines /
/.
My detailed examination of each of Marx's manuscripts
in Parts I (First Manuscript), II (Second and Third
Manuscripts, except for Marx 1 s Critique of Hegel), and
III (Second and Third Manuscript, i.e. Marx's Critique
of Hegel), will cite and criticise the MEGA editors'
characterization of the "fra<: _ntary outward appearance"
of the individual manuscripts.

CHAPTER II
The Suppression of the Commodity
Structure of Marx's First Parisian
Manuscript.
2 .1
The Alleaed Irrelevance of ~.dam Smith in P..11
Editions of the EPM
Marx I s concept of alienated labour is introduced
47
on the last six pages of his first Parisian notebook
(pages XXII-XXVII) • The most striking feature of these
pages is their division into three columns. On every
page, these three columns carry the underlined
headings "Wages of Labour", "Profit of Capital", and
"Rent of Land". Similarly - with the exception of four
pages which are divided into two columns only (pages
XIII-XVI
inclusive) - the first twenty-one pages
of this notebook are also divided into three columns
and given the same three column headings
Wages of Labour
Profit of CaEital
Rent of Land
Illustration of the format of most of the pages of
Marx's First Parisian Manuscript


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
48
The source of these column headings is Adam Smith's ana-
lysis of the exchangeable value of a comrnodi ty, an ana-
ly:::ls that he presented in his Wealth of Nations.
"In every society", wrote Smith in the sixth chapter
of his first volume, entitled "Of the Component Parts
of the Price of Cornmodi ties",
the price of every commodity finally resolves
itself into some one or other, or all of those
three parts; and in every improved society, all
the three enter more or less, as component
parts, into the price of the far greater part
of commodities ••• the whole price •.. resolved
itself either immediately or ultimately into
the same three parts of rent, labour, and pro-
fit. (Srni th, 50)
Smith extended this analysis of the commodity to identi-
fy and explain the basic income structure cf a commodi-
ty-producing society.
As the price or exchangeable value of every
particular commodity, taken separately, resolves
itself into some one or other, or all of those
three parts; so that of all the commodities which
compose the whole annual produce of the labour
of every country, taken complexly, must resolve
itself into the same three parts, and be par-
celled out among different inhabitants of the
country, either as the wages of their labour,
the profits of their stock, or the rent of their
land .•• wages, profit and rent, are the three
original sources of all revenue as well as of
all exchangeable value. {Smith, 52)
Not only did Marx divide all the pages of his notebook
into columns replicating Smiths analysis of the commo-
dity, he also drew heavily on Smith's Wealth of Nations
for the written material presented in the first sixteen
pages of his notebook. Although Marx frequently acknowl-
edges this source, there are many unreferenced passages


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
49
which can be traced back directly to Smith's explanation
of the tripartite structure of the cornmodi ty in Chapter
Six of the Wealth of Nations or to the three chapters
in which Smith examines each component separately: Chap-
ter 9 "Of the Wages of Labour", Chapter 10, "Of the Pro-
fits of Stock",and Chapter 11, "Of the Rent of La!1d."
Thus the columnar design of every page in Marx's
original notebook, giving each page the visible struc-
ture of Adam Smith's analysis of the exchangeable value
of the commodity, together with the written contents of
the first sixteen pages, strongly suggest
that the
roots of Marx 1 s concept of alienated labour lie in Jl..dam
Smith's Wealth of Nations. This indeed is my contention
and my major hypothesis: that the answer to how Marx
arrived at this concept and what he intended with it
lies in his analysis of Smith's account of comrnodi ty
production and that Marx I s concept of alienated labour
represents and embodies the conclusions of this analy-
sis.
From the time that the 1844 manuscripts were first
transcribed into publishable form, however, the evi-
dence for such a hypothesis was ignored. The editors
of the 1932 MEGA edition - which was the first complete
edition of Marx's 1844 manuscripts and which became the
basis of all subsequent editions and translations -
prefaced the published text of this first notebook with
the following description and interpretation of its ori-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
50
ginal form: 1 )
Manuscript no. 1 consists of a bundle of 9
double-folios (18 sheets, 36 pages), which Marx
compiled into a notebook. Before the text was
written down, the pages as a whole were divided
into three columns by two vertical strokes. These
columns by and large bear the following headings
(from left to right}: Wages of Labour, Profit of
Capital, Rent of Land. These headings were like-
wise penned before the writing of the text and
they are mostly the same on every page. For this
reason it can be assumed that Marx had intended
to devote a more of less equally long detailed
treatment to the three themes. Nevertheless the
planned parallelism appears to have suffered
in many ways from later deviations and inter-
ruptions. From page XXII on, the division of the
pages into th!:'ee col1.lrl1!'..s and the colur.cn-11eadir.gs can-
pletely lose every meaning. The text goes con-
tinuously across all three columns and we have
given it the title, in accordance with its con-
tent, "Alienated Labour". On page XXVII the
manuscript breaks off. {~!EGA I: 3, p. 38, trans.
MAF)
Thus, according to the MEGA editors.
1) The content of the first 21 pages is ordered by its
columnar format and is consistent with the column
headings. But these first 21 pages add up to nothing
more than an abandoned project that suffered "inter-
ruptions and deviations." The contents of these pages
h.:::.ve been typically regarded in the secondary lite-
rature as loosely-connected fragments from the writings
of political economists.
2) There is no relationship between the form and the
content of the last six pages.
3) The last six pages bear no relationship to the
first 21 pages.
All subsequent editions of Marx's~ have implicitly

51
or explicitly accepted and endorsed these assrnumptions.
In the introduction to his English translation, T. B.
Bottomore, for example argues that:
On page XXII+ of the manuscript, however, Marx
began to write on a different subject, igno-
ring the division of the pages into three
columns. (Bottomore xvii, emphasis mine)
The contents of the manuscript have invariably been
published as four separate, unrelated chapters. The
first three 1 given the titles of Marx's column
headings -
"Wages of Labour", "Profit of Capital", and
"Rent of Land" - contain the text of r-~arx' s first 21
pages. The text of the last six pages appears as a
fourth chapter which has been given by all editors the
title invented by the MEGA editors, "Alienated Labour".
In many collections of Marx's early writings, the first
three chapters are omitted altogether and the chapter
on "Alienated Labour" appears on its own, as an inde-
pendent, self-contained fragment. The first American
publication of Marx's ~
- and still the most popular
and widely-read edition in the English language -
Erich Fromm's Marx's Concept of Han (1959) makes this
same unfortunate omission.
Since it has been the published form, and not the
original format of Marx's ~, which has shaped the
endeavours of scholars to understand and interpret
both Marx's' ~
as a whole and his concept of alienated
labour in particular, and since this published form
is based on the assumptions of the MEGA editors, ~we

shall not deal directly with debates appearing in the
