MEGA editors.
2. 2
The Flaw in the MEGA Edi tors' Rationale
The assertion that the columnar di vision and the
column headings of the last six pages of Marx's First
Manuscript have nothing to do with the analysis of
alienated labour which appears on these pages, rests
or falls on the following statements in the MEGA edi-
tors ' description of Marx's manuscript:
Before the text was written down, the pages
as a whole were divided into three columns
by two vertical strokes • • •
(The column)
headings were likewise penned before the wri-
ting of the text .
"Before the text was written down, the pages as a
whole were divided . . . These headings were likewise
penned before the writing of the text". The "before"
in this description is ambiguous; what does it mean?
It is of course obvious that since the written text is
organized into three columns, ~.iarx must have divided
each page into three before he began to write on it.
But the editors' account of the irrelevance of the
columns on the last six pages means that Marx must
have formed all twenty-seven of his pages into columns
and labelled ill these columns'~ he made any start


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
53
on the written text. In other words, for the hypothesis
of the editors to hold true ( "From page XXII on, the
division of the pages into three and the column-headings
completely lose every meaning"), we must assume that
Marx mechanically drew his vertica~ lines on 27 blank
pages and mechanically labelled the resulting columns
of all 27 pages, before launching into his written text.
Only this presupposition can explain the existence of
six pages, divided into three headed columns, whose
content allegedly has nothing to do with the di vision
of these pages into three labelled columns. 2 )
What is the evidence that Marx mechanically divided
27 blank sides of his none-double folios into three
columns and gave each of these columns a heading
before he penned anything else on these pages? There is
no evidence other than the extant notebook itself. And
this evidence points to precisely the opposite conclu-
sion: namely that Marx did not divide and label twenty
seven blank pages, before filling them in with a written
text, but that the writing of the text and the dividing
of the pages into headed columns occurred at the same
time.
The evidence provided by Marx's original notebook
is as follows:
Firstly, Marx did not consistently use the same
phrasing to expre£S the heading of the "Profit of Capi-
tal" column. Four different synonyms are used in the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
54
titles of the "Profit" columns. Marx starts out by
using "Profit des Kapitals" as the title of this column
(pages I and II), but he then changes to "Kapitalgewinn"
on page III and to "Gewinn des Kapi tals" on page IV.
"Profit des Kapi tals" appears only once more in the
whole notebook as the title of a column, namely on page
XXII. The other two synonyms are much more frequently
used: "Kapi talgewinn II on pages V through XI and again
on page XXVI, and "Gewinn des Kapitals" on pages XII
and XIII, XVI through XXV (except for page XXII), and
once again on the last page, XXVII. On page XIV, we find
yet a fourth synonym, "Gewinn der Kapitalien". (All of
this may be checked by reference to the reproduced
pages of Marx's original notebook {First Manuscript) in
Appendix I) .
The fact that Marx used four different synonyms for
the titles of his "Profit" columns strongly suggests
that he did not draw and label the columns of his pages
ahead of time. For if it is true that the pages on which
he designed these columns were all blank, then it would
seem that the labelling of those columns must have been
a fairly mechanical process. If so, what motivated Marx
to vary the titles that he gave to the "Profit" columns
on 27 empty pages? If, on the other hand, we adopt the
opposite hypothesis - that Marx drew and titled the
columns at the same time that he was working out and
writing down his text - then the answer is easy: the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
55
title that he gave the "Profit" column on each page was
influenced by the material with which he had just been
working. This hypothesis finds further support in the
following facts: (1) The compact single word "Kapital-
gewinn", which appears as the title of the "Profit"
column for the first time on page V, is used for the
first time in the text of the previous page IV, where
is appears twice. (2) The relatively infrequent use
of the Frerndwort "Profit" in the column headings (where
it appears only three times) in comparison to its in-
digenous German synonym, "Gewinn" {which is used in all
the other column headings) corresponds to its relatively
infrequent appearance in the text ("Profit" and its
derivative "profitieren" appear 22 times, 3) and "Gewinn"
and its derivatives appear 57 times). That Marx chose
the less common non-German word "Profit" as the column
heading for his first two pages can be attributed to
the fact that most of the works on political economy
which he had studied in preparation for this written
analysis of the tripartite structure of the commodity
were in French. The only other page on which Marx used
"Profit des Kapitals" as the column heading was page
XXII. The word "Gewinn" does not appear in the text
of this page at all - nor does it appear in the text
of any of the foregoing five pages (where the column
headed "Profit of capital", like the column headed
"Wages of Labour", has been left blank) • But the word


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
56
"Profit" does appear - twice on page XXII itself, and
once on the immediately preceding page XXI. {3) The
fourth synonym, "Gewinn der Kapitalien", which appears
only once as a colwnn heading, differs from all the
other three synonyms in that capital is here given the
plural, not the singular, form. The page on which this
plural form is used as the column heading is page XIV.
Although Marx's text most frequently refers to capital
in the singular, on page XIV, the one page where the
plural from appears as the column heading, the plural
"Kapitalien 11 appears twice in the text (in quotations
taken from Adam Smith). "Kapitalien" also appears in
the text of the immediatel.y preceding page XIII, again
in a quotation taken from Adam Srni th. 4)
Neither the MEGA edition, nor any other editions of
the EPM, make reference to the four different synonyms
that Marx used to head his column on "Profit" - nor is
this fact, to my knowledge, mentioned anywhere in the
secondary literature. And indeed, Marx's variations in
his column headings make little difference to our
understanding of the specific content of those columns.
The reason why they are significant is that they provide
evidence for calling a hitherto unchallenged assumption
into question: namely that the only way to explain the
columnar structure of the last six pages of Marx's note-
book is to assume that the act of dividing the pages of
this notebook into columns and the labelling of those


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
columns occurred prior to and independently of the
filling in of those columns with a written text.
57
Secondly, if the reader skims through the reproduced
pages of Marx's notebook given in Appendix I, he/she will
note that whereas most of the pages are divided into
three columns, there are four that are divided into two
columns only. These are pages XIII-XVI. The ~-1EGA editors
made no mention of these four 2-column pages in their
description of the physical appearance of the notebook.
The existence of these four pages presents another serious
challenge to the assumption that Marx drew and titled
his columns on pages that were otherwise blank. For if
the pages were blank, what rnaC.e Marx decide, after di-
viding twelve blank pages into three columns, that the
next- four blank pages were somehow different and de-
served to be divided into two columns only? If, on the
other hand, the twelve 3-column pages lying before him
were not blank, but already contained a written text,
then we nay hypothesize (a hypothesis which we will
later substantiate) that the argument he had already
developed and written out had reached a stage which
demanded that the next pages be divided into two, rather
than three, of Smith's components of the commodity. Why:
Smith himself recognized that under certain conditions
of production and market competition, the exchangeable
value of a cornmodi ty was not composed of all three com-
ponents, but only of two components. In both of the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
58
passages already quoted, Smith speaks of the value of
every commodity resolving itself into "some one or- other,
or all of those three parts) {emphasis added).
Thus a thorough examination of Smith's analysis of
the exchangeable value of the commodity would include
an exa..~ination of values composed of only two components.
It is scarcely surprising that in order to carry out
his examination of Smith's two-component values, Marx
divides his pages into only two columns.
-Thirdly, if the reader continues skimming through
the reproduced pages of Marx's original notebook, he/she
will note that the pages immediately following these
four 2-column pages are divided into three again - but
with this difference: whereas in all the previous three-
column pages, the "Profit of Capital" appeared as the
centre colunm, on pages XVII-XXI the "Rent of Land"
now appears in the centre and is written in, while the
other two columns remain blank, apart from their colurnn-
headings. Why did r.:arx, having returned to his original
design of three columns per page, now decide that it
would be better to switch the column headings, so that
the. "Rent of Land" now occupies the center of the page
- and why should he have left the other two columns
blank? The hypothesis that Marx divided all his pages
ahead of time yields no satisfactory answer to this
switch of column headings. The counter-hypothesis -
that Marx's division of his pages into columns was


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
59
carried out at the same time as he was writing the con-
tents of these pages - is strikingly confirmed by the
structure of these pages. These five pages are the only
place in the first notebook where Marx discusses feudal
society, a society where land ownership is central and
where capital and wage-labour have not yet emerged or
only play a peripheral role. In other words, the written
contents of these five pages correspond to the page
format, a correspondence that will be documented in
detail in a later chapter.
'-Fourthly, on the last but one page of the notebook
(page XXVI) , Marx originally headed the third column
"Private Property". Later he crossed this heading out
and replaced it with "Profit of Capital". Why should
he have bothered to do this, if, as the MEGA editors
assert, "From page XXII on, the division of the pages
into three and the column headings completely lose
every meaning"? If the headings were in any case mea-
ningless, why bother to change them?
--....Fi~,.hb:1 the seven pages of Marx I s First Manuscript
that have been left blank constitute, by their very
emptiness, evidence against the MEGA editors' hypothesis.
These pages contain no text, nor are they divided into
columns. If, as the MEGA editors assert, there is no
relationship between the commodity structure of the
last six pages of the notebook and their content ( the
analysis of alienated labour), why did Marx decide not


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
60
to make use of the pages that were entirely blank and
contained no misleading traces of an earlier, abandoned
project? Why did he ignore these available blank pages?
This question has never been raised and has never re-
ceived an answer.
JYly answer is quite simple: that at
the time Marx was in a position to write down his ana ....
lysis of alienated labour the pages on which he did so
were just as blank as the seven unused pages at the
front of the notebook. Again it seems that Marx must
have divided and labelled his columns at the same time
as - and not at some point prior to - the process of
filling those columns with a written content.
In sum, none of the pages of Marx's original manu-
script give any indication that the columns were pre-
pared ahead of time. Moreover, these columns were drawn
freehand in the same i.l"lk as the text itself was written
in.
Thus all the evidence points to the conclusion that
the formation of the columns and the writing of the
text occurred at the same time. This means that when
Marx drew and labelled the columns of his last six
pages, he did so in the full knowledge of what he was
going to write in those columns.
On the basis of the evidence documented in this
section, it is no longer possible to accept the reason-
ing behind the MEGA editors' inference that the commo-
dity-structure of the pages on which Marx introduced


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
61
and developed his concept of alienated labour has
nothing to do with their content. The question posed
at the beginning of this chapter must now be regarded
as a legitimate question: "Why did Marx choose to pre-
sent his own original concept of alienated labour on
pages that were divided into F.dam Srni th' s components
of the exchangeable value of the commodity?"
2.3
A Note on the MEGA Editors' Lack of Familiarity
With Adam Smith's wealth of Nations
The MEGA editors' reorganization of the text of
Marx's First Manuscript meant that the physical struc-
ture of Marx's presentation was lost. Thereby much of
the evidence of Adam Smith's persistent influence on
Marx's thinking-processes was absent from all published
versions of the manuscripts. However I do not want to
imply that the MEGA editors consciously suppressed the
evidence of Adam Smith's influence. On the contrary:
there are several indications that the MEGF. editors were
themselves unfamiliar with Adam Smith's work and con-
sequently failed to appreciate the great extent to which
Marx relied on Adam Smith for his own analysis of poli-
tical economy.
The task confronting the TY1.EGA editors was the task
of deciphering Marx's handwritten script. This was a
task demanding a high degree of calligraphic skills


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
62
and enormous mental concentration. Marx I s handwriting
throughout his life was almost unreadable to the un-
practised eye. After Marx's death, Engels devoted the
rest of his own life to preparing Marx• s work for publi-
cation by copying over the text of the later volumes
of Capital, and by training other trusted colleagues,
for example Karl Kautsky, to carry out the same task.
At that time, Engels felt it was far more important to
ensure that Marx's later works be published, than to
spend time working over his earlier notebooks. Thus the
only surviving version of Marx's jRM is entirely written
in Marx I s hand. The most pressing and most important
task confronting the ?JI-EGA editors - and one that must
have taken up an enormous amount of time, energy and
concentration - was the very difficult task of faith-
fully transcribing into legible form what Marx himself
had written. The conscientiousness with which the MEGA
editors carried out this task is to be applauded, and
it is unfortunate that the fruit of their labour -
the 1932 MEGA edition of the ~
- was never reprinted,
for it contains far more information about Marx• s ori-
ginal text than any other edition I have come across.
There a.re many places where the MEGA editors were able
to decipher words or sentences that Marx wrote and sub-
sequently crossed out. These are reproduced in the foot-
notes of the MEGA edition, but they have been omitted


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
63
by subsequent editors of the EPM despite their reliance
on the MEGA editors' transcription.
Nevertheless, despite the high degree of transcribing
skills required to decipher Marx's text and despite the
MEGA editors' conscientious application of these skills,
there is one transcription mistake that reveals their
lack of farniliari ty with Adam Smith's work. On the last
page of their "Profit of Capital" chapter, the MEGA
editors deciphered a phrase from page XVI of Marx's
original notebook as "Swift I s twenty lottery tickets"
( "Die 20 Lotterielose von Swift", MEGA, p. 66) . In later
editions of the EPM (see Bottomore p. 102, footnotes
2 and 3}, this has been changed to the correct refe-
rence "Smith's 20 lottery tickets", which was what
Marx himself had written (see Appendi:;:c I, page XVI of
the First Manuscript, second column, last word of the
second from the last line). Admittedly, Marx's writing
of this word is ambiguous and it could equally well
be deciphered as "Swift" and as "Smith". The correct
deciphering does not depend on transcribing skills, but
on acquaintance with Smith's work: on at least three
occasions, Smith uses the analogy of the way a lottery
works for the way economic forces determine the fates
of individuals. 5}
In the places in the First Manuscript where Marx
not only cites Smith but also gives his page reference,
the MEGA editors have accordingly transcribed Marx's


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
64
reference to Srni th. But there are also many places where
Marx quotes, paraphrases, or swnmarizes passages from
Smith (the vast majority of which Marx had already co-
pied out in full in his excerpt-notebook) , without
giving his source. In these cases the MEGA editors
failed to give the original source; nor did they refer the
readers to the contents of Marx's excerpt-notebook
published in the same volume of the MEGA, where these
passages could be found. This lack of reference to
passages from the Weal th of Nations stands in sharp
contrast to the MEGA editors' treatment of another au-
thor, Wilhelm Schulz (one of the first German socialist
writers), from whom Marx took some of his material.
In the latter case, the MEGA editors cited the ori-
ginal passages that Marx had paraphrased from Schulz
and footnoted the divergences in Marx 's reproduction
of these passages (see for example the extensive foot-
notes to pages 46-48 of the MEGA text). Thus, although
the MEGA editors' major task was to prepare Marx's
script so that the reading public could have access to
it, they also recognized that it was helpful to the
readers to provide additional information about Marx's
sources. That they provided this information in the
case of Schulz but not in the case of Srni th I suggests
that they were able to carry out this secondary task
only to the extent that they were already familiar
with Marx's sources. It is not surprising that the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
editors of the 1932 MEGA, who were themselves German
socialists, were acquainted with the work of Schulz
65
but not with the work of English liberal political
economists. What is surprising is that the MEGA editors
did not make the connection between Marx I s earlier
excerpt notes on the Wealth of Nations and his reliance
on Adam Smith's work in the First Manuscript I since
both of these early studies by Marx were published in
one and the same volume.
In summary I the change that Marx's First Manuscript
underwent in the process of being prepared for publi-
cation was by no means a deliberate attempt to suppress
evidence that Marx's concept of alienated labour had
its roots in Adam Smith's Weal th of Nations. Rather,
those who transformed Marx's almost illegible script
into a legible, publishable text were preoccupied with
a task that first and foremost demanded transcribing
skills. Moreover, they seem to have been unfamiliar
with Adam Smith's work. The failure of later scholars
to question the MEGA editors' arrangement of the material
can be explained by the difficulty involved in going
back to Marx's original script and comparing it v;-ith
the published text. In addition, the rise of Hitler and
the subsequent censorship of Marxist literature pre-
vented the reprinting of the 1932 MEGA volume. This
meant that Marx's earlier excerpt notes on Adam Smith,
which provide the readiest evidence for Marx's reliance


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
66
on Srni th' s work in his First Manuscript, remained re-
latively inaccessible to later scholars.
2. 4
The Purpose of the Commodity Structure of Marx's
Pages
The fact that Marx divided all the pages of his
First Manuscript into columns bearing the underlined
titles, "Wages of Labour", "Profit of Capital" and "Rent
of Land", was interpreted by the MEGA editors as evi-
dence that Marx "intended to devote to each of the three
themes a detailed exposition of more or less the same
length". In this section we shall point to
evidence
that contradicts this hypothesis of the purpose behind
the commodity structure of Marx I s pages and suggest
an alternative hypothesis.
We shall begin by drawing attention to Marx I s asser-
tion in his preface to the EPM on the valuable contri-
bution that Engels
had made to the study of political
economy. In this preface, Marx speaks of Engel's "Out-
line of a Critique of Political Economy" as one of
"the original and important German works on this subject"
(Marx 64) and as a work on which he himself relied.
Engels' essay was published in the DeutschfranzOsische
Jahrbticher in 1844, when Marx was editor of that jour-
nal, and Marx's excerpt notes contain a summary of
Engel's argument. Engels' critique was directed pri-
marily against the categories that political economists


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
67
had developes to conduct a scientific study and analysis
of contemporary social economic reality. These scienti-
fic categories, argued Engels, served to obscure the
relationships and forces that were really at work in
contemporary society. Thus, in the case of capital, for
example, the political economists' own description of
this phenomenon revealed that it was not an independent
factor of production and should therefore not be treated
as a separate category.
It becomes immediately evident, that capital
and labor are identical, since the economists
themselves confess that capital is "stored-up
labour". We are therefore left with only two
sides - the natural objective side, land; and
the human, subjective side, labor. (Engels in
Struik 207-208)
Thus, Engels concluded, "We have, then, two elements
of production in operation - nature and man" (Struik
208).
The analysis of capital as "stored-up labour", on
which Engels based his conclusion, was taken from Adam
Smith (p. 262) and reappears on the very first page of
Marx's notebook as the answer to the question "What is
capital'?" (Marx 85). This indicates that right from
the first page of his notebook, Marx was clearly aware
that the categories which Adam Smith had introduced to
divide up and document the empirical phenomena of commo-
dity-producing society were themselves questionable be-
cause they misinterpreted and obscured certain aspects
of empirical. reality.. Thus, if Marx's intention was to


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
68
carry out "an entirely empirical analysis, based upon
a careful study of political economy", as his preface
states (Marx 63) , he would have failed in his goal had
he consistently adhered to the three-component theore-
tical approach that had dominated political economy
since the publication of the Weal th of Nations. This
means that is is most unlikely that Marx would have
planned a detailed exposition of each of the three
categories that serve as the headings of his columns
without calling into question the categorical division
itself.
Both Marx and Engels had received a thorough training
in Hegelian philosophy, a basic methodological principle
of which was that an understanding of the parts must
be derived from an understanding of the whole. 6 ) Accor-
ding to Hegel, it is the whole that structures not only
the status and interrelations of the parts to one an-
other, but also determines the very form and content
of the individual parts. Hence before one can undertake
a detailed exposition of the parts, one must understand
the nature of the whole. Thus instead of viewing the
whole as the sum of its parts, as Adam Smith and his
successors had tended to do, the Hegelian approach re-
quires that the parts be understood as specific mani-
festations of the whole. The difference between the two
approaches may be clarified as follows: for Adam Smith,
the process of production consists of bringing together


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
69
three independent and elementary factors of production,
labour, capital, and land. For Marx, on the contrary,
the division of the production process into these three
factors is something that can only be explained by the
nature of the production-process itself. The three com-
ponents of the commodity are not to be taken as given,
but as manifestations of a specific structure of pro-
duction as a whole. If this specific structure did not
exist, then we ba:ve no grounds to presuppose that the
components of the production process would exist in
the form of labour, capital, and land.?)
Thus, the commodity-structure of Marx's pages should
not be interpreted as an abandoned project in which Marx
originally intended to arrange his material in such a
way that he could carry out a systematic comparison of
wages, profit, and rent. Rather, Marx's intention was
to bring together on the same page a comprehensive
picture of the whole. Initially he presents this pic-
ture from three different standpoints, standpoints do-
cumented by the political economists themselves: the
standpoint of the labouring class, the standpoint of
the capitalist class, and the standpoint of the land-
owning class. The word "standpoint" should no be mis-
interpreted to imply that Marx was interested in the
ideological interpretation that each of these classes
offered of themselves and their society. Rather, as
the titles of the columns indicate -
"Wages of Labour",


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
70
"Profit of Capital" and "Rent of Land" -
Marx was inter-
ested in the material relationship between each class
and the production process, i.e. the material circum-
stances imposed on each class by the social organization
of commodity production. What Marx documents on his pages
is not the respective ideologies of the working class,
the capitalist class, and the landowning class, but on
the one hand the information hitherto gathered by
scientific studies on the differential access that these
three classes have to material wealth, and on the other
hand, an analysis of the respective contributions of
the classes to the production of that wealth.
The object of Marx's investigation then was to un-
cover the hidden structure of the whole and to do so
by penetrating the hidden relationships between the
partial manifestations of that whole. In the course of
his investigation, he was led,like Engels, to call into
question and eventually abandon the categories that
political economy had used.
Thus, although Marx continues to divide the pages
of his notebook into columns bearing as their headings
the concepts used by political economy to identify the
structure of commodity production, Marx's own analysis
increasingly diverges from that of political economy.
Not surprisingly the page content increasingly diverges
from the page format, so that on the last six pages
we find the same columnar format as on the first six


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
71
pages, but no longer the same division of the content
into three separate texts. In the straightforward
cornmodi ty structure of the first six pages, the relation-
ship between the three column titles and the three se-
parate texts that fill the columns is self-evident. This
is not so for the commodity structure of the last six
pages, where the text to "Wages of Labour" fills all
three columns. However the last six pages are not the
only pages that diverge from the straightforward comma-
di ty structure found in the first six pages. Most of
the intervening pages also present variations on the
straightforward commodity structure, although only one
page (page VII) repeats precisely the same variation
as the last six pages. 8 )
Before we attempt an explanation of the significance
of these variations in the columnar format of individual
pages and of their relationship to the straightforward
commodity structure and to one another, we must first
take into account a hitherto unknown datum of Marx's
original notebook: the divergence between the sequence
of the page numbers and the order in which the pages
follow one another. This datum, as we shall see in the
next chapter, is an important clue to the overall struc-
ture of Marx's investigation into the value of the
commodity and the nature of commodity-production.

CHAPTER III
The Division of Marx I s Smithian Notebook into a Core-
Notebook and Outer Sheets: Toward an Understanding of
of the Commodity Structure of the Pages
We have so far presented evidence that challenges the
hitherto taken-for-granted assumption that the columnar
format of the last six pages of Marx's notebook, on
which he introduces
and elaborates his concept of alien-
ated labour, belongs to an abandoned project. We have
suggested, on the contrary, that the concept of alien-
ated labour was a continuation of the project that Marx
undertook in the rest of his notebook, namely a critical
investigation into the nature of commodity-production.
Now, in this chapter we shall show that the structure
of this project is revealed by the way in which H.arx com-
piled his notebook. The compilation procedure is in turn
revealed in the divergence between the numbered sequence
of the pages. First we shall document the compilation
procedure of Marx's Hegelian notebook (Third and Fourth
Manuscripts), a notebook in which there is no divergence
between the numbered sequence and the physical sequence
of the pages. This will enable us to appreciate the ir-
regularities in the pagination of the First Manuscript.
Second, we shall argue that this pagination can only be
explained by assuming that this manuscript originally


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
76
existed as two distinct pieces. We will call these two
pieces the core notebook and the outer sheets. (Instruc-
tions for a do-it-yourself guide to the compilation of
Marx's First Notebook and its division into a core note-
book and outer sheets is given in Appendix II, which can
be treated either as a supplement to, or a substitute for,
sections
and 2 of this chapter.) The core-notebook con-
sists of pages I-XVI and the outer sheets consist of
the nine unnumbered pages at the beginning of the First
Manuscript and the last eleven munbered pages, XVII-XXVII.
After demonstrating the division of Marx's notebook into
a core notebook and outer sheets (sections 1-3), we will
devote the second half of this chapter {section 4) to
documenting the different ways in which Marx structured
the individual pages of both his core notebook and . his
outer sheets.
3 .1
The Compilation of Marx's Hegelian Notebook
The Hegelian notebook, as we explained in our first
chapter, consists of manuscripts III and IV. Like the
first notebook, it is evidently hare-made: and is made up
of double folio sheets. A double folio sheet is a sheet
of papter folded in two so that it forms a total of four
pages.
DIAGRAM OF A DOUBLE-FOLIO SHEET


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
77
From Marx 1 s Hegelian notebook, all the double-fol.io
sheets with numbered pages have been published as the
Third manuscript and the 23 unwritten and unnmnbered
pages at the end of the notebook are also regarded as
part of the Third Manuscript. The centermost double-
folio consists of four written pages, but these pages
are unnumberd. They are not included in the published
versions of the Third Manuscript. The MEGA edition of
1932 published the contents of these four pages as an
appendix and referred to them as Manuscript IV. Most
edit.ions of the~, however, omit them altogether and
thus there is little mention of their existence in the
secondary literature. The significance of this little-
known FOurth
Manuscript for our present purposes is
that its existence gives us a clue to how the sequence
in which Marx numbered the pages of his First Manus-
cript could diverge from the sequence in which the pages
of this manuscript follow one another.
The Fourth Manuscript is a self-contained text, in
which Marx copied out selections from the final chapter
of Hegel's Phenomenology. Since these pages were not
numbered, there would be no interruption in either the
text nor the numbered sequence of the Third Manuscript.
Nevertheless, the relationship between the Fourth and
the Third Manuscript is clear, for the four unnumbered
pages contain the text from Hegel, on which Marx based
a major part of his Third Manuscript, namely his critique


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
of Hegel's cialectic and philosophy in general. It is
most unlikely that Marx's reference notes {i.e. the
Fourth Manuscript) for this critique would have been
buried in the middle of the notebook at the time that
Marx used them to write up his critique. Yet this is
78
where they stand in the notebook that has survived to-day
-
sewn in with the same sturdy white thread that holds
together all the pages of the Third Manuscript. Thus, in
order to explain how this four-page self-contained text
appears in the middle of another independent text, we must
assume that both texts had been written on loose sheets
and that none of these sheets were attached to one an-
other until the written text had been completed. In other
words, what Marx wrote on were loose sheets that had not
yet been stitched together to form the notebook as it
exists to-day.
Nevertheless, it is clear that Marx, at the time of
writing intended to compile these loose sheets into a
notebook, for the numbered pages of the Third Manuscript
follow one another in consecutive sequence. All the
double-folios of Marx's Hegelian notebook are stitched to-
gether through the center fold, so that one half of the
double folio appears as two pages in the first half of the
notebook and the other half of the same double-folio ap-
pears as two pages in the latter half of the notebook.
Since the pages of the written text follow consecutively,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
79
this means that Marx initially left one half of all the
double-folio sheets blank. Only when the end of his work
was in sight could he start filling in the other half
of the folded sheet he was working on.
We may thus visualize Marx• s working-process as fol-
lows. On the one side of his desk there is a pile of
blank sheets. He takes one, folds it in two to form four
pages, and writes only on two sides of the one fold,
numbering these two sides I and II. He opens this folded
sheet and lays it out flat on the other side of his desk.
He takes another blank sheet, folds it in two and again
only writes on the two sides of one half, numbering
these sides III and IV. Thus a pile of half-written
sheets rises on the one side of his desk, with the one
half of these sheets forming a continuous written text
and a consecutive number sequence and the other half
left blank. When he has made the decision to fill in all
four sides of the double-folio sheet he is working on,
he then draws for his next sheet of paper, not from the
pile of blank double-folios, but from the pile of double-
folios that have already been filled in.
Needless to say, the end rarely came in sight before
Marx was well past the midpoint of his completed work.
Thus it is not surprising that when Marx had completed
his written text, some of the doUble -folios were still
only half used. For what Marx still had to say after the
end of his argument came in sight took up less pages than


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
80
the number of blank pages left in the half-fil.led double-
folios.
The sequence of pages in the Third Manuscript allows
us to reconstruct the fairly straightforward procedure
behind the compilation of this notebook and thereby ap-
preciate the eccentricities of the pagination of Marx's
First Manuscript. The outermost page of the Third Manus-
cript is numbered I and as we turn the pages of the
notebook, the numbers continue to rise consecutively
(with the accidental ornroissions of numbers XXII and XXV),
until we reach page XLIII. Then both the writing and the
numbers stop and the last 23 pages are blank.
These last blank pages form the unused halves of the
outermost double-folio sheets of this notebook. The used
halves appear as the first twenty-three pages at the be-
ginning of the notebook, the pages numbered I through
XXIV (with the o:mmission of page-number XXII). Thus after
Marx had completed his writing process on separate
double-folios, he had one pile of pages which were only
half used and another pile where all four sides had been
filled in. He put these two piles together, with the
corn.pletely written-in folios laid out flat on the top and
the half-used folios likewise laid out flat on the bottom.
He then added his four unnumbered pages of reference
notes (the Fourth Manuscript) on top of the pile and
sewed the lot together down the centre. The sewn-up pile
of double-folio sheets could then be folded together to


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
81
form the notebook that exists today. Hence we find that
in the resulting notebook, only the five centermost
double folios {i.e. the Fourth Manuscript and the pages
of the Third Manuscript numbered XXVII to XLII) have all
four sides written on, while the eleven outermost double
folios have only 2 sides written on, forming pages I
through XXIII (with number XXII skipped over). The double
folio between these four center folios and eleven outer
folios has three sides written on, the third side being
the last numbered page of the whole notebook (XLIII).
Thus the Third Manuscript consists of a total of sixteen
double folios making up 64 pages, 41 containing a written
text (numbered I through XLIII, omitting XXII and XXV)
and 23 at the end of the notebook that have been left
blank.
The physical result of Marx 1 s compilation of these
sixteen double folios, which had already been written in
and numbered before Marx sewed them together, is a note-
book (the Third Manuscript) whose pagination is what you
would more or less expect in any book with numbered pages.
In other words,
we begin with page I and as we turn the
pages, the nu...1'11bers rise in consecutive sequence (skipping
numbers XXII and XXV) until the last page of writing,
which is numbered XLIII, and the remaining pages at the
end of the notebook are blank.

3. 2
The Divergence between the Numbered and the Physical
Sequence of the Pages of Marx's Srnithian Notebook
As can be seen from the diagram of Marx's First
Manuscript presented at the end of Appendix II, the un-
numbered and blank pages of this notebook come not at
the end, but at the beginning. This diagram also repro-
duces the eccentric pagination of this notebook. Thus
the first numbered page after the nine unnumbered pages,
is not page I but page XVII. Page I is the next page
and it begins a new double folio. The reverse side of
page I is not page II, but page XVI. Then the pages con-
tinue in descending order from XV to II. Pages III and
II form the other half of the double folio, on which
pages I and XVI have been written. Thus the four centre
double folios buried in the middle of Marx's first note-
book form the first sixteen numbered pages. The onl:l
way it would be physically possible to read these pages
consecutively according to the continuity indicated by
the pagination (starting with I and ending with XVI)
would be to detach the four centre folios from the rest
of the notebook. We could then read page I as the outer-
·most first page. But in o·rder to "Ifiove on i:o pa9°E!; .II·, in-
stead of turning page I to its reverse side, we would
have to flip the whole of the "core" notebook {i.e. the
four centre folios of Marx's existing First Manuscript)
over, for page II is the outermost last page of the

83
four
doubl.e-folios. We can then turn over page II, as
we do when we read any normal book, and find page III.
We can now keep turning the pages and find that they do
follow continuously and consecutively right up to page
XVI, and on turning this page to its reverse side, we
find ourselves back where we began with page I.
The remainder of the notebook consists of nine un-
numbered pages at the beginning and eleven numbered
pages, XVII-XXVII. T~is makes a total of 2o pages formed
out of five outer folios, half of which appear at the

and page XVII, and the other half appear at the end of

Thus if we were to detach the four centre-folios, we

However, in order to read page XVII, the lowest number
in this sequence, as the first page,· we would have to

have to flip over our pile of folded sheets to the last

84
outermost page, which carries the number XVIII. We can
now proceed to turn the pages as we would when reading
any other book, ~nd we find the numbers rising normally

page XXVII is itself mostly blank, we are not surprised
to find the nex 9 pages unnumbered and blank { if we ig-
nore the title page and the list of bibliographic

cript). If we turn over all these nine unnumbered pages,
we come back to the page where we started, namely page
XVII - in just the same way as we came back in the core
notebook to page I •
3.3
The Significance of the Division of Marx's
Smithian Notebook into a Corenotebook and
Outer Sheets
As we pointed out in the first section of this chapter,
when Marx carried out his investigation, he wrote on
loose folios that had not yet been compiled into a note-
book. The numbering of these loose folios indicates that
Marx, at the time of writing, regarded his work as con-
sisting of two self-contained parts: pages I-XVI, or
what we have called the core-notebook, enclosed by a
double-folio containing the first and last pages (I, II,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
85
III, '1.nd XVI); and pages XVII-XXVII, the outer five
folios, which were likewise enclosed by a double folio
containing the first and last pages of that project
(XVII, XVIII, and XIX, with the reverse side of XVII
forming the last page). The second project however was
only partially completed, for on the last page of the
outer sheets, Marx sets up an agenda for continuing
his analysis, but does not in fact carry it out. In-
stead, page XXVII breaks off with the words: "Let us
examine three relationships more closely" {I1arx 134)
and the pages that follow are blank. The fact that Marx
gave the second project page numbers that followed on
from the first project, together with the fact that he
later compiled both projects into one and the same note-
book , indicates that Marx himself regarded tb.e t;.;o pro-
jects as intimately connected and following on from one
another.
The distinction and the continuity of the two parts
of Marx I s notebook, that we have uncovered by sorting out
the eccentric pagination of this notebook as a whole, is
also borne out by the written content. In the core note-
book, the majority of the written content consists of
excerpts taken from the tradition of classical liberal
economics, primarily from Adan Smith's founding work,
The Wealth of Nations.
In many cases, Marx does not even bother to comment
on these excerpts. Where he inserts comments or argu-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
86
ments of his own, these all refer directly to the ex-
cerpted material he is dealing with. Thus Marx I s initial
investigation into the nature of commodity-production
rests heavily on the empirical documentation and theore-
tical framework provided by political economy itself.
Although Marx in his core notebook takes over "the Pre-
suppositions of political economy ... its terminology and
its laws", as he him.self states on one of the pages of
the outer sheets (page XXII, Bottomore 120), he does so
in order to reveal the limitations of this traditional
framework. His own comments :in the core-notebook, and in-
deed the very arrangement of the quotations that he in-
cludes, reveal the contradictory ways in which political
economists, including Adam Smith himself, have used
Smith's concepts and theoretical framework. By letting
the political economists speak for themselves, Marx
calls into question the validity of the traditional ap-
proach for understanding the nature of commodity-pro-
ducing society, an approach that takes as unproblematic
its basic assumption that the value of a commodity con-
sists of three analogous components, wages of labour,
profit of capital, and rent of land.
In the outer pages Marx's approach is quite different.
Although he continues to structure his pages in accord-
ance with the traditional tripartite analysis of the
commodity, he no longer uses excerpts to document the
inter:i.al contradictions and the self-evident inadequacy,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
87
of the analytical framework developed by Smith. In con-
trast to the many quotations that fill .the, pages of ·the
cOre"""J:!.otebook, there are no quotations to be found in
the outer sheets. On these pages, Marx is involved in
the task of developing his own analytical concepts and
interpretative framework within which he can reorder
the contents of political economy. The topic of investi-
gation is still the same as it was in the core notebook:
the nature of commodity production. But the specific
task has changed. Having revealed (in the core-notebook)
the inability of the political economy's scientific con-
cepts to grasp the empirical reality that it sought to
study, Marx attempts (in the outer sheets) to develop
a more appropriate and more comprehensive concept,
alienation.
Although the MEGA editors must have been aware of the
irregular pagination of Marx's notebook, they regarded
it, like the commodity structure of the pages, as an
insignificant datum. Thus in their description of the
physical features of Marx I s original notebook, they did
not even bother to mention the divergence between the
numbered sequence of the pages and the sequence in
which the pages followed one another.
If the MEGA editors had followed through the impli-
cations of this irregular pagination, they would have
been alerted to the fact that the difference between


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
88
the first and last pages of Marx I s notebook was not a
change of theme but a change of approach and that more-
over this change of approach occurred not on page XXII,
where Marx introduces his analysis of alienated labour,
but five pages earlier, on page XVII, where Marx intro-
duces his analysis of alienated land. As a consequence
of their failure to appreciate the division of Marx's
original notebook into a core notebook and a set of
outer sheets, the MEGA editors imposed their own struc-
ture on the written contents, without being aware that
this structure obscured the structure and continuity
that Marx had intended.
Thus, on the one hand, the MEGA editors separated
off the contents of the last six pages of the outer
sheets as a fourth chapter on "Alienated Labour", re-
cognizing - correctly - that they were in some way dif-
ferent from Marx• s earlier documentation of the tradi-
tional analysis of the commodity. But they hypothesized
- incorrectly - that this difference was to be interpreted
as a difference in topic. On the other hand, they pub-
lished the first five pages of the outer sheets, pages
XVII-WI, as a continuation of the third chapter with
the title "Rent of Land", thereby obscuring the metho-
dological transition that occurs between pages XVI and
XVII. In other words, the four-chapter sequence im-
posed by the MEGA edition unwittingly obliterated the
original continuity and structure of the argument that

led Marx from his critique of political economy to his

3.4
The Commodity Structure of the Pages of the Core
Notebook and the Commodity Structure of the Outer
Sheets
The division of Marx• s notebook into two related,
but distinct projects gives us a better understanding of
tie.commodity structure of the individual pages. In the
core-notebook, where Marx self-consciously accepts the
theoretical framework of political economy, most of the
pages are divided into the straightforward commodity
structure, with each column carrying a separate text
corresponding to the column heading: "Wages of Labour",
11Profit of Capital 11 , and "Rent of Land•. None of the
pages of the outer sheets repeat the straightforward
cOillinOdi.ty structure. All of the pages, however, of the
outer sheets carry one, and only one, text, despite the
division of the pages into differently headed collJl[IIls.
All of the pages of the core notebook (with one excep-
tion, page VII) carry more than one text. Thus in the
core notebook, Marx adopts the approach of political
economy and treats the different manifestations of com-
modity production as separate and independent phenomena.
In the outer pages, he attempts to trace these differ-
ent manifestations to one and the same underlying cause,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
9o
and he therefore presents his analysis of cornmodi ty pro-
duction as a single, continuous text.
3.4.1
The Straightforward Commodity Structure Pre-
dominant in the Core Notebook (Pages I-VI,
VIII-XII)
The straightforward commodity-structure is to be
found on eleven of the 16 pages of the core-notebook.
These are pages I-VI and VIII-XII. All these pages are
divided into three columns and the content of each
column bears a readily-understandable relationship to
the column heading. Thus the "Wages" column contains
passages that describe the conditions of the labourers
both at home and at work, and the fluctuations of their
wage-packet; the "Profit" column carries excerpts ex-
plaining the source of profit and the ways in which
capital is accumulated and deployed; the
11Rent 11 column
examines the relationship between tenant and landlord
and the conditions that determine the size of the rent.

The order of Marx's columns on these pages is the same
order in which Smith himself originally introduced and
analysed the three components of the COrtlI;I.Odity. Smith,
after explaining the tripartite division of the ex-
changeable value of a cornmodi ty in chapter VI of the
first volume of the Wealth of Nations, "Of the Component
Parts of the Price of Commodities", goes on to devote
chapter 8 to "Wages of Labour", chapter 9 to "P=ofit of
Stocks", and chapter 1o to "Rent of Land" (the inter-
vening chapter 1o is a discussion of "Wages and Profit
in the Different Employments of Labour and Stock") •
The material that Marx presents in the columns of his
first six pages is drawn almost exclusively from the
Wealth of Nations, particularly from the above mentioned
chapters. In many cases, Marx does not reference his
source, but in his excerpt notes on Smith's work. we find
most of the passages that he again quotes, paraphrases,
or summarizes on these first six pages. Thus the commodity
structure of the first part of the core-notebook serves
the pu::-pose of bri:-iging together on one and the same
page Smith's own observations and propositions about the
three different aspects of commodity-production, three
aspects that Smith had regarded as separate phenomena
and analysed in separate chapters.
The contents of the columns on pages VIII-XII, which
are also characterized by the straightfor,.,rard corn."!lodit::{
structure, no longer rely to the same extent on Smith 1 s

work. The contents of the "Rent of Land" column are
still drawn exclusively from Smith; the "Profit of
Capital" column contains extensive excerpts from Smith,
but also from other writers; the "Wages of Labour"
column contains nothing from Smith. To understand why,
we must look at the form and content of page VII.
3.4.2 Variation I: The Identity between the Commodity
Structure of Page VII and the Commodity Structure
of the "Alienated Labour" pages XXII-XXVII
Page VII is similar to the pages so far described in
that it is divided into three columns with the same
column headings in the same order. It differs from the
straightforward commodity structure in that the contents
of the columns do not correspond to the three column
headings. The contents of the "Wages" column is indeed
a continuation of the "Wages" text on the preceding page,
but the contents of the other two columns do not continue
the
11Profit 11 and "Rent" texts. Instead the discussion of
wages fills all three columns. The structure of page VII
is thus identical to the structure of the last six pages
of the outer sheets, where, as the MEGA editors noted in
their prefatory description of Marx's original notebook,
11 the text is written continuously across the three
columns 11 , although these columns retain their three
different headings.

The divergence of page VII fran the straightforward

and "Rent of Land". In Marx's original notebook, the

in the corresponding columns of page VIII, where Marx
returns to the straightforward commodity structure. Only
in the chapter on 11Wages of Labour" do we find the Roman
numeral VII. The disproportionate length of the text bet-

We ha~noted that the three separate texts that fill

Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.

Wealth of Nations. The one continuous text that fills all
three columns of page VII summarises the points that have
been made in the preceding pages and presents them as
a series of contradictions. It is this series of contra-
dictions that reveal the inability of Smith's theoretical
framework to provide a satisfactory explanation 1) of the
role of the labouring class in the creation of the wealth
of nations and2) of the relationship of the labouring
class to the property-owning classes (the capitalists
and the landlords). Thus on page VII: the project that
Marx had undertaken in his core notebook to reveal the
inadequacies and contradictions of political eccnomy 1 s
theoretical framework is partially fulfilled. This is
made explicit in Marx's comments at the end of page VII:
Let us now rise above the level of political
economy and seek from the foregoing argument,
which was presented almost in the word:; of the
economists, answers to two questions: (Harx 76-7)
In fact, Marx does not "rise above the level of political
economy" until he undertakes the project of presenting
an alternative theoretical framework in the outer sheets.
In the rest of the core notebook, Marx continues his
project of exploring the theoretical inadequacies of
Smith's categories, "Profit of Capital" and "Rent of
Land", but in the case of "Wages of Labour" this is no
longer necessary. Thus what we find in the "Wages"colum.n
of the remaining pages of the notebook is a selection of
quotations from post-Smith writers, that provide empirical
documentation of the worsening working and living condi-

tions of the labouring class which is brought about
by the advance of manufacture and the corresponding de-

factual confirmation of the theoretical contradictions
that Marx had already revealed in Adam Smith I s account
of the labouring class, serves as an illustrative appen-
dix to Marx's theoretical project, but it does not yield
any new theoretical insights.
The problematic implications that Marx has revealed in
Smith's account of the "Wages of Labour" on page VII,
are not dealt with again until the last six pages of the
outer sheets, where the discussion of "Wages of Labour"
again fills all three columns of the page. Moreover, as
we shall see in chapter IV, the two questions that Marx
poses on page VII and promises to answer by rising above
the level of political economy, are indeed answered 0:1
pages XXII-XXVII by his explanation of wage-labour as
alienated labour

and continued with a discussion, introduced in the

Although the MEGA editors did not explicitly mention
the two-column format of these four pages, this is
probably what
they were thinking of when they stated
that the "planned parallelism" (i.e. three equally long
expositions of the three components of the commodity)
"appears to have collapsed and gone astray later in
many respects". If, however, as we have argued, the
purpose of the comrr.odity structure of Marx's pages was
not to compare the parts, but rather to use what had
been written about the parts in order to penetrate the

that task.
The category that disappears on these pages is the
"Rent of Land", and according to Ada!!'. Smith, this is a
tendency inherent in the progress
and spread of manu-
facturing:
As any particular commodity comes to be mere manu-
factured, that part of the price which resolves
itself into wages and profit, comes to be greater
in proportion to that which resolves itself into
rent. In the progress of manufacture, not only
the number of profits increase, but every sub-
sequent profit is greater than
the foregoing;
because the capital fror., which it is derived
must always be greater. (Smith 51)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
99
In another passage, Adam Smith recognized that already
in this time, commodities were being produced and sold
without any part of their value going to the owners of
land, in other words, without any part ot their value
taking the form of rent:
The highest ordinary rate of profit may be such
as, in the price of the greater part of commodi-
ties, eats up the whole of what should go to the
rent of the land, and leaves only what is suffi-
cient to pay the labour of preparing and bring-
ing them to market, according to the lowest rate
at which labour can any-where be paid, the bare
subsistence of the labourer. The workman must
always have been fed in some way or other while
he was about his work; but the landlord may not
always have been paid. (Smith 97)
Thus although pages XIII, XIV, and XV diverge from
the straightforward commodity structure, unlike the
pages of the outer sheets, they do not step outside
Smith's own theoretical framework. But they do point
to an inconsistency in this framework: narr.ely th.J.t there
is nothing inherent in the concept of the exchangeable
value of the commodity nor in the social reality of
commodity exchange that demands that the rent of land
be an essential or integral component of value in ex-
change. The question then arises: does it make sense to
continue treating the rent of land as a component of
the commodity? The first five pages of .:-iarx' s
outer
sheets are devoted to answering that question.
The last two-column page, which is also the last page
of the core-notebook, page XVI, does step outside Smith's
theoretical framework, in that, in its final form, this


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
100
page excludes the "Wages of Labour". For, according to
Smith, no commodity can be reduced and brought to market
to be exchanged without labour. One could speculate that
Marx's exclusion of the labouring class on this page
was intentional in order to bring out a hidden aspect
of the nature of connnodity-production. This aspect be-
comes explicit in Marx's explanation in his outer sheets:
namely that the distinguishing characteristic of
commodity-production is •the fact of private property"
(Marx 120) and that the development of commodity pro-
duction necessarily renders the labouring class property-
less.
3.4.4 Variation III: The Unique Status of "Rent of
Land" in the Outer Sheets (pages XVII-XXI)
Whereas the commodity-structure of the last six pages
of the outer sheets repeats a format that has already
been introduced in the core notebook on page VII, the
commodity structure of the other pages of the outer
sheets, pages XVII-XXI, is entirely new. On these pages,
the two side columns have been left blank, apart from
their column headings, and only the center column
carries a written text. A further difference between
these pages and the other three-column pages, is that
the center column no longer carries the heading "Profit
of Capital", but has now switched to 11Rent of Land."

All editions of the ~, following the example of

tinued existence of a landowning class, distinct from
and independent of the capitalist class, was incompatible
with the continued development of comi.-nodity-production.
This implication is made explicit on the first page of
the outer sheets. But there is also an important dis-
continuity, a discontinuity indicated in the change of
page format. Marx switches from investigating the form
that the rent of land takes in a fully-developed com-
modity-producing society to examining the origins of
the rent of land in feudal society.

3.4.5 The commodity Structure of the "Alienated

The only vari.ation in the straightforward commodity

It has led some of the most sensitive interpreters of

3. 5
Summary
In this chapter we explored a little-k:10·,-1n datum of
Marx 1 s original manuscript, namely its irregular
pagination, and were led to the conclusion that this
pagination indicated a two-fold project. The first pro-
ject extended from page I to page XVI, which ·11e called
the core-notebook, in accordance with the position of
these pages in the centre of Marx's notebook as a whole.
We noted that this project, both in the straightfon1ard
commodity structure of most of its pages and in the
written content that filled the columns of these pages,
revealed a self-conscious irnr.tersion on the part of ~·larx
in the traditional analysis of corr.modi ty production as
it had been carried out by Adam Smith and his successors.
The pages of the core notebook that diverge from the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
108
strai.ghtforward commodity structure, namely page VII and
the two-column pages at the end of the core notebook,
indicate both in their form and written content the
inadequacies of Adam Smith's analytical framework.
We may therefore infer that these pages represent the
crucial link between the project that Marx carried out
in his core-notebook -
namely a demonstration of the
inability of Smith's analytical framework to cope with
the contradictory phenomena presented by the empirical
reality of cornrnodi ty production -
and the project that
he introduced in his outer sheets - namely a new way
of looking at commodity production that took into ac-
count these contradictions. On the first page of the
outer sheets, page XVII, Marx introduces a variation on
the commodity structure that is to be found nowhere in
his core notebook. Moreover, the straightforward com-
modity structure which characterized most of the pages of
the core notebook does not reappear in any of the outer
sheets.
Similarly, there are no quotations or paraphrases
from other works of political economy. This is in sharp
contrast to the core notebook, where most of the written
content consists of citations from other writings. Thus
page XVII marks the change in direction in Marx' s think-
ing that the MEGA editors attributed tc page XXII by
publishing the contents of pages XXII-XXVII as a separate
fourth chapter with the title "Alienated Labour". The


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
109
change from page XVI to page XVII is not a change of
topic, but a different way of approaching one and the
same topic, the nature of commodity production. From
page XVII (and not just from page XXII where the con-
cept of alienated labour is introduced) to the end of
the manuscript, Marx is engaged in the task of developing
his own concept and interpretative frar.:.ework within
which he can reorder the contents and subject matter
of political economy. The basic difference between the
traditional framework of Adam Smith's Wealth of Nations
which Marx used in his core-notebodc and the new frame-
work which Marx introduces in his outer pages is that
Marx treats commodity production as a historical de-
velopment, a process with a beginning, a middle, and
an end. Marx seeks for the historical laws governing
this development. Adam Smith, in contrast, had regarded
commodity production as the realization of the "natural
liberty of man" and had treated the laws governing this
mode of production as eternal verities, as hidden forces
that owed their origins not to the specific constella-
tion of historical circumstances, but to the natural
condition of humankind.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
CHAPTER IV
Marx's Application of the Dialectical
Method to Adam Smith's Weal th of Nations
110
We have presented evidence on two features of .Marx I s
first manuscript that have so far been overlooked in all
published versions of the manuscript aY1d therefore in
the secondary literature as well. The first is the di-
vision of all of the pages of r,!arx' s notebook into
col urons representing the components of the comroodi ty as
analyzed by Adam Smith. The second is the di vis ion of
the notebook as a whole into two parts: the core-note-
book and the outer sheets. In chis chapter we shall argue
that these physical features are a reflection of the
methodological principle that guided Marx from his im-
mersion in Adam Smith's Wealth of l~ations to his own
concept of alienated labour. ·That is, while we are pri-
marily interested in revealing the so-far unrecognized
influence of Adam Smith on Marx's development of the con-
cept of alienated labour, we do not want to deny that
Marx's philosophical background, particularly his train-
ing in Hegel's method of dialectical critique, was equal-
ly essential for Marx's arrival at the concept of alien-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
111
ated labour. Our argument is that Hegel supplied the
method and Smith supplied the content of Marx's concept.
However, since Hegel viewed the dia·lectic in the form
that he developed it as the demonstration of the
inseparable unity of method and content, what we have
called the content appears in Hegel 1 s presentation of
the dialectic as the first stage of the method itself.
My task in this chapter will be to outline the three
stages of Hegel's dialectical method and to interpret
the content and overall structure of Marx's Smi thian
notebook in terms of these three stages. I will thus ar-
gue that Adam Smith I s Wealth of Nations corresponds to
the first stage; that the core notebook, which Marx de-
votes to uncovering contradictions in Smith's interpre-
tive framework, corresponds to the second stage, and
that the outer sheets, in which Marx attempts to bring
these contradictions together within the concept of
alienation - both the alienation of land and the alien-
ation of labour -
corresponds to the third stage.
The attempt to interpret Marx's First Manuscript in
terms of Hegel's dialectical method has caused me great
ambivalence and frustration. On the one hand, the con-
viction that the dialectical method is the key to under-
standing the overall structure of ~1arx' s first notebook
and the intellectual process tnat moved Narx from his
study of Adam Smith to his own concept of alienated
labour is one that I cannot rid myself of. The origins


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
112
of this conviction lie not in Marx's First Man'..!script,
but in his Third Manuscript. In Marx's Preface to all
four manuscripts, which appears on the last pages of
the Third Manuscript, he asserts that his results "are
the outcome of an analysis grounded in a careful study
of political economy" and further states "I consider
the final chapter of the present work, the exposition
of the Hegelian dialectic and of philosophy in general
essential". Why should Marx have insisted that an expos-
ition of Hegel's dialectic was an essential part of his
stud;r· of political economy - unless his study of politi-
cal economy was itself an exercise in the dialectical
method? Furthermore, Marx continued to insist through-
out his life-long study of the capitalist mode of pro-
duction, that the only way to make sense of political
economy was by applying Hegel I s dialectical method. In
his Foreword to Capital Volume I, he stated that he
was proud to call himself a disciple of Hegel 1 s, and
that the only difference between Hegel's dialectical
method and his own was that "with him (Hegel) it is standing
on its head". Moreover, Marx explicitly acknowledged
his debt to Hegel I s Logic in the presentation and de-
velopment of the most difficult chapter of Capital
Volume I. This was his first chapter -
a chapter devoted
to an explanation of the commodity. Thus there is stror1g
evidence that Marx's study of political economy, and in
particular his efforts to understand the secret of the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
113
cornrnodi ty, were guided by Hegel's methodological prin-
ciples.
On the other hand, I find myself unable to grasp or
present in simple terms precisely what Hegel meant by
the dialectical method. My own understanding of that
method has been heavily influenced by my conviction
that Marx's First Manuscript is an illustration of the
dialectical method. Hence my presentation of tnat method
presupposeswhat should be demonstrated. However, I
have found no way of presenting the dialectical method
that does not plunge the reader into the obscure depths
of Hegel's philosophical system, depths that I myself
cannot claim to fully understand or to be able to justi-
fy. In short, I am well aware that my account of the
dialectial method is open to objection. But since no
one else has yet attempted to argue that there is an
underlying structure that links 1-iarx' s concept of alien-
ated labour at the end of his notebook to his citations
from the Wealth of Nations at the beginning, I shall
present this method in the way that I have come to under-
stand it.
4.1 Hegel's Dialectical Method
The word dialectic originally stems from the Greek
word "to converse or to dialogue". It was Socrates who
made such effective use of dialoguing as a method for


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
114
advancing knowledge. All of Socrates' dialogues recorded
by Plato represent Socrates querying representatives of
conventional wisdom about what they really mean by the
concepts they use. The most famous is Socrates 1 probing
of the meaning of njustice" in the first book of the
Republic, in which he demonstrates that any attempt to
give justice a universally valid standard and definition
will confront certain concrete situations in which the
application of this standard will result in injustice -
the opposite of justice. This same principle, of probing
the categories that conventional wisdom takes to be uni-
versally valid, in order to reveal that they turn into
their opposite, is likewise the principle of Hegel's
dialectical method.
In Part I of his Encyclopaedia of the Philosophical
Sciences, Hegel formalizes his dialectical method as
three aspects of a logical reality. I interpret these
three aspects of logical reality as the steps by which a
conventional body of knowledge is to be transformed into
what Hegel regarded as true and absolute knowledge. He-
gel states:
In accordance with its form, the logical
doctrine has three stages or aspects:
(i)
the Abstract stage, or the level of the
Understanding
(ii)
the Dialectical, or that of negative
reason (the immanent critique) and
(iii) the Speculative, or that of positive
reason.
Hegel I s characterization of the stages is at a deliber-
ately high level of abstraction because he is attempting


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
115
to outline the process by which ~
conventional body
of knowledge at the Level of Understanding can be trans-
formed into an adequate body of knowledge, i.e. know-
ledge that not only makes certain propositions about
the world, but that is able to demonstrate that these
propositions are necessarily true.
4. 1. 1
The Level of Understanding
This is the stage in which the knowing subject goes
to work on empirical reality. The knowing subject re-
produces in thought an exhaustive inventory of the
characteristics of the object under investigatioP.. These
characteristics are then presented as universal cate-
gories. Hegel describes this action of the knowing
subject towards the object it investigates in the :Eol-
lowing words:
The action of Understanding may De in general
described as investing its subject matter with
the form of universality.
The limitation of the Level of Understanding is that
once it sets up its inventory of characteristics and
turns them into categories, it does not pause to re-
examine the validity of the categorization of the
characteristics. Consequently, the body of knowledge
produced at the Level of Understanding must be trans-
formed into a more adequate comprehension of the ob-
ject under investigation.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
116
4. 1.2
The Dialectical Stage of Negative Reasoning
Hegel I s characterization of the ''true and proper
character" of the dialectic makes the relationship bet-
ween this second stage and the categories of the Under-
standing explicit.
In its true and proper character, Dialectic
is the very nature and essence of the catego-
ries (formulated by the understanding) of
things, and of the finite as a whole. (Logic
para. 81)
Thus the object under scrutiny at this stage is no
longer the empirical reality, but the categories of the
Level of Understanding. In the course of this scrutiny,
Hegel argues, the categories will be forced to reveal
contradictions and the eventual outcome is that they
will turn into their opposites.
In the Dialectical stage these finite character-
izations or formulae /of the level of Under-
standing/ supersede themselves, and pass into
their opposites. (Logic, para. 81)
What is important to note about this stage is that these
contradictions are not produced by introducing any ex-
ternal criterion to judge the categories. ?..ather the
contradictions already exist, though, implicitly, at
the Level of Understanding and all that the dialectical
stage does is to make them explicit. Thus Hegel speaks
of the dialectic as "an imrnanen t tendency, forcing it-
self outward and beyond", in other words, the dialec-
tical stage is a process of immanent critique.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
117
4 .1. 3
The Speculative Stage of Positive Reasoning
Just as the dialectical stage of negative reasoning
took as its point of departure the categories of the
Understanding and transformed these categories into
their opposites, so too the speculative stage takes as
its point of departure the resulting oppositions pro-
duced by the action of the dialectic. Hegel character-
izes the activity of thought at this stage as follows:
The Speculative stage, or stage of positive
reason, apprehends the unity of terms (pro-
positions) in their opposition - the affirma-
tive which is involved in their disintegration
and in their transition. (para 82)
This third stage is thus a demonstration that the contra-
dictions revealed by the second stage do not necessarily
invalidate the knowledge contained in the categories of
the Understanding, but affirms this knowledge at a
higher level, and allows its re-interpretation on the
basis of new categories.
4. 2
Smith's Wealth of Nations as the Level of Under-
On page XXII of his Smithian notebook - just before
he introduces his concept of alienated labour - Marx
states in no uncertain terms what his point of departure
was:
We have begun from the presuppositions of
political economy. We have accepted its term-
