118
inology and its laws. We presupposed private
property; the separation of labour, capital
and land, as also of wages, profit and rent;
the division of labour; competition; the con-
cept of exchange value, etc. {Marx p. 120)
Although ~arx does not anywhere in his notebook mention
the Level of Understanding, there are several clues that
allow us to infer that Marx viewed nis starting-point -
Adam Smith I s wealth of Nations -
as the Level of Under-
standing. In the first place, what Marx lists in the
above quotation as his presuppositions are all categories
of political economy. Secondly, when Marx goes on to
criticize the limitations of the categories of political
economy, he does so in terms that are strongly :::-eminis-
cent of Hegel I s Characterization of the Level of Under-
standing as "a separating and abstracting attitude"
which "lives in a world where every term or product of
thought preserves a stereotyped distinction from every
other. Each of these limited abstractions the Under-
standing believes to be and exist on its own account".
Thus Marx describes the laws of political economy as
"general and abstract formi.:las" (Earx p. 120), and points out that
that thouc:rh political e:a:;onomy assumes the separatior, c::
labour from capital and of capital from land, it pro-
vides no explanation for the basis of this distinction.
He also states ";>olitical economy fails to understand
the interconnexions ... " (~-!arx, p. 1 21 l
Admittedly these hints can scarcely be treated as
conclusive evidence, but they are supported by the fact


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
119
that Marx, before he undertook his study of political
economy, immersed himself in a thorough and detailed
critique of Hegel's Philosophy of Right. In this work
Hegel expressly identified political economy - as re-
presented by Smith, Say and Ricardo, all of whom Marx
refers to and cites from in his Smithian notebook -
as
the Level of Understanding, which takes as its object
of study
11civil society". By civil society, 3egel meant
the social institutions that provide for the satisfactior.
of individual needs by the labour of others:
Political economy is the science which starts
from this view of needs and labour but has the
task of explaining mass-relationships and mass-
movements in their complexity and their quali-
tative and quantitative character. This is one
of the sciences which have arisen out of the
conditions of the modern world. Its develop-
ment affords the interesting spectacle (as in
Smith, Say, a."ld Ricardo) of thought working
upon the endless mass of details which confront
it at the outset and extracting therefror:t the
simple principles of the thing, the Understand-
ing effective in the thing and directing it.
(P.of R., para. 189 addition).
In a later study on political economy {"A Contribution
to the Critique of Political Economy" written in 1857),
Marx appended an .autobiographical account cf his own
intellectual development, which reveals the extent to
which his first studies of political economy were in-
fluenced by Hegel's Philosophy of Right: Having "found
myself in the embarrassing position of having to dis-
cuss what is known as material interests" and having
been forced "to turn my attention to economic questions",
Harx goes on to state:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
The first work which I undertook to dispel
the doubts assailing me was a critical re-
examination of the Hegelian philosophy of
120
law •••• My inquiry led me to the conclusion
that neither legal relations nor political
forms could be comprehended whether by them-
selves or on the basis of a so-called general
development of the human mind, but that on
the contrary they originate in the material
conditions of life, the totality of which
Hegel, following the example of English and
French thinkers of the eighteenth century,
embraces within the term "civil society";
that the anatomy of this civil society, how-
ever, has to be sought in political economy.
The study of this, which I began in Paris, I
continued in Brussels .... (Marx 1970, p.2o)
The fact that Marx here links Hegel 1 s discussion of
civil society to his own earliest study of political
economy is yet another indication that Marx, at that
time, like Hegel, viewed political economy as the Level
of Understanding. If Marx identified political econony
as the Level of Understanding, then we would anticipate
that he would subject it to the two further stages of
reasoning that Hegel outlined in his logical doctrine.
Marx's division of his manuscript into two self-con-
tained, but related parts -
the core notebook and the
outer sheets - suggests that he did so. Thus we shall
pursue the hypothesis that Adam Smith's Wealth of Nations,
the founding work of political economy, is treated in
Marx's first notebook as the Level cf Understanding.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
4. 3
The Core-Notebook as the Dialectical Stage of
Negative Reasoning
Given Hegel's schernatization of the three levels
121
of his logical doctrine, it is clear that the object
addressed by the dialectical stage of negative reason-
ing is never the directly-observable social reality it-
self {in this case the production of a nation's wealth),
but the way in which that reality has been understood
and interpreted (in this case, the Wealth of Nations,
authored by Adam Smith). Thus the "raw materials"
which the dialectical critic will work over are the pro-
ducts of the human mind at the "Level of Understanding",
or in other words, the conceptual apparatus which con-
ventional wisdom has developed to organize and make
sense of the world. The task underta.k:.en by the dialec-
tical critic is the total immersion in the categories
and concepts of the "Level of Understanding" in order
to detect any ambiguities, inconsistencies, and contra-
dictions internal to (or immanent in) this conceptual
framework. The fact that the contradictions that the
critic is interested in are internal, means that they
can be demonstrated simply by bringing together and
quoting, word-for-word, the contradictory passages. This
is precisely what Marx did in his core-notebook - at
least in his own eyes, as evidenced by several incidental
comments that he inserts. Consequently, the contents


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
122
of !:his part of his first botebook, which have been dis-
missed by unwary edit:>rs as random worknotes, consist
mainly of quotations and accurate paraphrases from the
Wealth of Nations, selected and arranged in such a way
that the contradictions implicit in Smith I s work become
explicit and sharpened.
The categories that Marx's immanent critique focuses
on are indicated in the column headings of his pages. In
documenting and juxtaposing Smith's propositions con-
cerning the wages of labour and concerning the way wages
are determined by other forces i.n the economy, Marx
shows that labour, as the active property of the wage-
earning class, exhibits characteristics that are in
complete contradiction to the concept of labour on
which Smith based his labour theory of value and his
fundamental argument that labour is the sole source of
wealth. This ambiguity in Smith's concept of labour,
which remains implicit in the Wealth of Nations , is
made explicit in Marx's core-notebook as a list of contra-
dictions between the •practical" and the theoretical
claims of the labourers. This list begins in the lower
half of the "Wages" column of page VI and continues in
all three columns of the page.
The other two categories that Marx subjects to
scrutiny in his core-notebook are the "Profit of Capital"
and the "Rent of Land". The basic contradiction that
Marx uncovers in Smith's treatment of these two cate-
gories is that the "Rent of Land" is not an independent


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
123
and universal category, as Smith had assumed, but that
the very forces that Smith had argued were necessary for
the continued creation of wealth, would inevitably
transform landed property into capital, and the rent of
land into the profit of capital.
According to Hegel, the reason why the dialectical
method of immanent critique is so successful in forcing
the categories at the Level of the Understanding to re-
veal self-contradictions, is because of a fundamentally
false premise in the attitude which the Level of Under-
standing adopts toward the object it investigates: name-
ly that it is possible to compartmentalize and isolate
the different aspects of the object from one another.
Thus the ultimate goal of the dialectical stage of
negative reasoning is to lay bare the problematic nature
of a fundamental premise which structures the conceptual
products of the "Level of Understanding". The next task
- the speculative stage of positive reasoning - is to
reintegrate and reconstruct the contents of the account
given by the "Level of Understanding" in such a way as
to yield an explanation of the unexplained premise.
4.4
The Outer Sheets as the Speculative Stage of Posi-
tive Reasoning
The problematic premise structuring the conceptual
apparatus of political eco:r..omy is identified Oy Marx in


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
124
his outer sheets as the taken-for granted existence of
private property:
Political economy begins with the fact of
private property; it does not explain it.
It conceives the material process of private
property, as this ~in reality, in general
and abstract formulas, which then serve it as
laws. It does not comprehend these laws; that
is, it does not show how they arise out of
the nature of private property. (Marx 120 )
Smith's acceptance of the fact of private property lies
at the root of his division of the exchangeable value
of the commodity into three components. In introducing
these three components, Smith had asserted that the rent
demanded by the landowning class derived from its pri-
vate ownership of the land, and the profit demanded by
the capitalist class derived from its private accumula-
tion of stock. Thus what is common to these two classes,
which Smith had regarded as independent of one another,
and which Marx has demonstrated in his core-notebook
must eventually merge, is their private ownership of
the objective conditions of production. Furthermore, it
is precisely this private ownership of the objective con-
ditions of production by the non-labouring classes that
transforms labour into wage-labour, a transformation that
in Smith's account is not explored, but which, as Harx's
examinatio~ has revealed, introduces profound contradic-
tions for Smith's labour theory of value. Thus in taking
private property as the fundanental problematic premise
of political economy, !·Iarx is identifying both the basis
of Smith's seperation of the col.Tlr.lodity into three com-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
125
ponents and at the same time the source of the contra-
dictions which Marx has revealed in his core-notebook.
Thus private property serves as Marx's central concept
in his outer sheets, where he tries to constr..:.ct a new in-
terpretative framework for understanding the contents
and contradictions inherent in the production of wealth
that Smith had described. Unlike Smith, Marx does not
treat private property as an unchanging
given, nor
does he treat the pursuit of private interests as the
universal principle of human society. Instead he treats
private property as a historically-specific phenomenon,
whose origins must be located in Europe's feudal past,
and whose movements must be recognized in Europe's
capitalist present.
In the first half of his outer sheets, Marx focuses
on the relationship between Smith's two categories of
land and capital and reinterprets this relationship as
the developmental continuity between the feudal organ-
ization of production and the capitalist organization
of production. The evolution of capitalism out of
feudalism is interpreted as the transition from private
property in its embryonic, immature form as landed pro-
perty to its fully-developed and universal form as the
purchasing power of capital. However, Marx does not mean
to imply that private property is an autonomous object
whose development occurs independently of human action.
Quite the oppos_ite; by demonstrating the historical de-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
126
velopment of private property, Marx demonstrates that
it can only be understood as the outcome of historical
action, the product of human agency. However, in its
developed form, private property confronts the human
agents of capitalist society as a force controlling
their every action. For this reason, Adam Smith and
other political economists had assumed that private
property was an innate and necessary urge in the human
condition.
In the latter half of his outer 1,>ages, t,"iarx analyses
the nature of the human agency that is presupposed by
the existence of fully-developed private property. This
agency corresponds to Smith's description of wage-labour,
but Marx reconceptualizes it as the alienation of
labour, a process in which the very activity of produc-
tion is at the same time the alienation (or transfer)
of the product out of the hands of the labouring class.
In this way, Marx is able to reconcile the contradictions
between Smith's concept of labour as the source of all
wealth and Smith's concept of labour as wage-earning
activity, in which the labourers have no claim on that
wealth. For the production of a nation's wealth is at
the same time the transformation of that weal th into
the private property of the non-labouring class. t•iarx
states the outcome of his analysis of alienated labour
as follows:
The relation of the worker to work also pro-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
127
duces the relation of the capitalist {or what-
ever one likes to call the lord of labour) to
work. Private property is, therefore, the pro-
duct, the necessary result, of alienated
labour, of the external relation of the worker
tc>n'ature and himself. (Marx 131-2)
Marx himself regarded his analysis of the nature of
private property as the result and outward manifestation
of the alienation of labour as an attempt to identify
the hidden unifying principle that underlies both the
categories of political economy and the contradictions
in these categories. This is made clear in the following
statement:
As we have .discovered the concept of private
property by an analysis of the concept of
alienated labour, so with the aid of these two
factors we can evolve all the categories of
political economy, and in every category, e.g.
trade, competition, capital, money, we shall
discover only a particular and developed ex-
pression of these fundamental elements. (Marx
132).
Thus the task that Marx undertook in his outer sheets
corresponds to the task that Hegel regarded as the out-
come of the third stage of the dialectical method, name-
ly the discovery of the hidden unity of opposites (pri-
vate property and alienated labour), which lies at the
root of the categories of the Level of the Understand-
ing, but which is obliterated by the very act of separ-
ating the empirical reality into independent categories.

CHAPTER V
Labour and Property in Adam Smith 's
Wealth of Nations.
128
We have so far considered two features of Marx's
original notebook that have not been taken into account
in the interpretations of Marx's concept of alienated
labour in the secondary literature. These were the di-
vision of all 27 pages of Marx's notebook into columns
representing Smith's component parts of the commodity,
and the division of the notebook as a whole into a
core-notebook and outer sheets. In the last chapter we
hypothesized these two hitherto ignored features of
Marx's original notebook as a reflection of a met:l.1odo-
logical principle that guided Marx's thought-processes
while he attempted to appropriate for himself Smith's
account of the Wealth of Rations. This method is the
dialectic, which Hegel developed in order to call
attention to the limitations of the body of knowledge
created by the inductive methods employed by the empiri-
cal sciences. In order to understand why Adam Smith's
weal th of Nations should lend itself to serving as the
level of unterstanding in the dialectical method of
investigation, we shall in this chapter take a brief
look at the contents of the Wealth of Nations.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
129
The task that Adam Srni th had undertaken was to in-
quire into the nature and causes of the weal th of
nations, as the title of his work states, and into the
distribution of this weal th "among the different ranks
and conditions of men in the society (Smith lviii) ."
His basic premise, briefly argued in the Introduction
to his whole work, was that the poverty or wealth of
a nation depends on the degree to which the productive
powers of its labouring propulation have been improved
and developed. Srni th identifies two principal causes of
the improvement in the productivity of labour (and
hence of the wealth of nations): the division of labour
(the subject of Volume I) and the accumulation of capi-
tal (the subject of Volume II).
Volume III traces the
history of Europe from the fall of the Roman Empire up
to Smith's own times, to show how the historical con-
ditions of different countries have either encouraged
or discouraged the application of these two principles.
Volume IV is a critique of the false analysis of wealth
offered by two systems of political economy current in
his time, the mercantile system, and the Physiocrats'
system. In the fifth and last volume, Smith discusses
the limited role of the "Sovereign" (i.e. the State)
within his own system of political economy: on the one
hand, civil government is absolutely indispensable for
the security of private property (Smith 669-671); on
the other hand, "the natural progress of opulence" (i.e.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
130
the improvement in the productive powers of labour, and
the increase in the nation's weal th) lies beyond the
competence of "any statesman or lawgiver" and is best
promoted by the self-interested activity of every
private individual (Smith 421-424).
Adam Smith was the first to argue that labour and
labour alone is the source not only of the weal th of
nations
but also of the exchangeable value of every
commodity, bought and sold by private individuals on
the market. In his chapter entitled "Of the Real and
Nominal Price of Comrnodi ties, or of their Price in
Labour and their Price in Money" (Volume I, chapter 5),
Srni th formulates his labour theory of value, a theory
which radically distinguished his analysis of wealth
from those of his predecessors:
The value of any commodity ... to the
person who possesses it, and who means not
to use or consume it himself, but to
exchange it for other commodities, is
equal to the quantity of labour which it
enables him to purchase or command. Labour
therefore is the real measure of the
exchangeable value of all commodities.
The real price of everything, what every-
thing really costs to the man who wants
to acquire it, is the toil and trouble of
acquiring it ••• What is bought with money
or with goods is purchased by labour I as
much as what we acquire by the toil . of our
own body. Money or . . . goods . . . contain
the value of a certain quantity of labour
... It was not by gold or silver but by
labour, that all the wealth of the world
was originally purchased; and its value,
to those who possess it, and who want to
exchange it for some new productions is
precisely equal to the quantity of labour
which it can enable them to purchase or
command. (Smith 30-31)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
131
However, despite his insistence that labour is the
original source of wealth and the real measure of value,
Adam Smith's concept of value suffers a fundamental am-
biguity.
He introduces the premises of his argument {the
di vision of labour, the labour theory of value, and the
market exchange of the products of labour) and explains
their interconnexions in terms of "the independent
work.rr1an" (i.e. a self-employed producer). He then goes
on to apply his theory to a historical reality which is
dominated by wage-labour:
It sometimes happens, indeed, that a single
independent workman has stock sufficient
both to purchase the materials of his work,
and to maintain himself till it be complea_-.
ted ••.
Such cases, however, are not very fre-
quent, and in every part of Europe, twenty
workmen serve under a master for one that is
independent. (Smith 65-66)
The property rights of labour implied by the model of
the independent workman are diametrically opposed to
those of the wage-labourer. This opposition is not ex-
plicitly recognized in Smith, and creates inconsisten-
cies in the way he applies his theory to an analysis
of historical development.
Independent workmen purchase (or make) their own
tools and materials and engage in a specialized line
of production, the results of which belong to them.
They can dispose of these products at their will: what-
ever they have immediate need for, they will consume
thernsel ves and the rest will be taken to market to be


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
132
exchanged for such produce of other men's labour as they
have occasion for.
Everyman thus lives by exchanging, or be-
comes in some measure a merchant (Smith 22).
This model of "everyman" allows Smith to argue that
the free competition of the market, regulated by the
exchange of labour represented by each commodity, will
guarantee that the needs of all will be satisfied and
that the atomistic pursuit of private interests is an
adequate mechanism to ensure the welfare of all. It pre-
supposes however that exchange is confined to the ex-
change of the products of labour, that every worker has
a product to sell and that every buyer is him/herself
a worker. This means that the means and materials of
production must be freely available to those who produce,
and that the right to private property is based solely
on the expenditure of labour (what I spend my labour
on, belongs to me).
Smith 's analysis of the internal structure of the
exchangeable value of a commodity presupposes an entirely
different relationship between labour and property. In
his chapter "Of the Component Parts of the Price of
the Commodity," he explains how the division of the ex-
changeable value of the comroodi ty into three components
(wages, profit, and rent) came about as the result of
two forms of private property, capital and land, which
have nothing to do with the labouring activity of their
owners. "As soon as stock has accumulated in the hands


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
133
of particular persons" and "as soon as the land of any
country has all become private property" (Smith 48-49),
two property-owning classes come into existence whose
revenues and property do not depend on "the toil of
(their) own body." (Smith 30, see quotation on page 17).
Smith does not offer any justification of the property-
claims of the landlords or the profit-taking capitalists,
nor does he explain how the appropriation of land and
the accumulation of stock occurred. He simply asserts
that they were two simultaneous, independent and irre-
versible events in the prehistory of civilized society,
after which the produce of labour was henceforth to be
divided among the "three great original and constituent
orders of every civilized society" (Smith 248}.
He
implies that the whole product in principle still belongs
to the labourers, who after marketing it, have to divide
the proceeds with the other two classes.
Srni th' s opening statements in his chapter "of the
Wages of Labour" assert that in principle the property-
rights of the wage-labourer are identical to those of
the independent workman:
The produce of labour constitutes the natural
recompence or wages of labour.
In that original state of things, which
precedes both the appropriation of land and
the accumulation of stock, the whole produce
of labour belongs to the labourer. (Smith
64-65)
But he immediately goes on to repeat and elaborate his
division of the product of labour into three components.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
134
But this original state of things, in
which the labourer enj eyed the whole produce
of his own labour could not last beyond the
first introduction of the appropriation of
land and the accumulation of stock. It was
at an end, therefore, long before the most
considerable improvements were made in the
productive powers of labour •..
As soon as land becomes private property,
the landlord demands a share of almost all
the produce which the labourer can either
raise, or collec_t from it. His rent makes
the first deduction from the produce of
the labour which is employed upon land ...
profit makes a second deduction. (Smith 65)
Here labour is reduced to a private fund that yields a
revenue Q!l1Y. to the labourers. The private fund of the
landlord class is all the land: "the wood of the forest,
the grass of the field, and all the natural fruits of
the earth" (Smith 49). The private fund of the capitalist
class is "a stock of goods •.. stored up somewhere
sufficient to maintain (the labourer), and to supply
him with the materials and tools of his work until
such time as the production process has been completed
and the products taken to market (Smith 259). Hence
according to Smith what is left in the labourers '
private fund is not even the means of their own subsis-
tence, let alone the materials and means of production. 4)
Moreover the persons who market the products of labour
are no longer the labourers themselves, but the owners
of stock (cf. Smith 59). Unlike the independent workmen,
what the labourers sell on the market is not the product
of their labour, hlt the use of their labour; the buyers
are not fellow-workers, but the owr..ers of stock, who in


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
135
buying the labour of others, acquire the rights to the
results of that labour.
My aim in this highly selective review of the contents
of Smith's Weal th of Nations has been to demonstrate
firstly that the basic proposition that Smith introduced
into the study of political economy was the identity of
wealth and labour; secondly, that Smith established
this identity in a conceptual framework that does not
correspond to the h:istorical reality that is the topic
of Smith's empirical investigation; and thirdly -
a
consequence of the second point - Smith's concepts of
labour and wealth contain ambiguities that give rise to
contradictory propositions.

l'-".a.rx 1 s selection of passages in the "Wages" columns of
his core-notebook illustrates the living and working
conditions of Smith's class of wage-labourers. This
selection of passages falls into two parts, devided by
page VII. The first seven pages rely almost exclusive-
ly on smith's statements and propositions. The last
eight pages (VIII-XV) contain lengthy quotations from
post-Smith writers, both political economists and so-
cialist historians. These latter pages we interpret as
empirical evidence from the 1800 's that the economic
forces which Smith had analysed in 1776 were still very
much in existence. Since our primary interest is in the
influence of Adam Smith, we will confine our reexami-
nation of Marx's commentary on wages in the core-note-
book to the text of the "Wages" column on the first
six pages and to the text on wages which covers all
columns of page VII. In other words, we will limit our-

138
selves to the pages where Marx deals directly with Smith.
OUr reexamination will
a} emphasize the extent of Marx's debt to Adam Smith in
particular, rather than to political economy in general,
the assumption behind all of the commentators on the
economic sources of Marx's theory of alienation;
b) bring to the surface the underlying logic - the
exercise of immanent critique - that connects the
passages that ~.arx selects from Smith's work, thereby
challenging the superficial impression shared by many
contemporary scholars that these passages are merely
random notes with no overall structure or connection
logic: and
c) demonstrate the validity of Marx's critique of the
founding work of classical political economy, :r'}le
Weal th of Nations.
Adam Smith devotes chapter 8 of his first book of
the Wealth of Nations to a discussion of the Wages
of Labour. He explains the purpose of this chapter as
follows;
I shall endeavour to explain what are the
circumstances which naturally determine
the rate of wages, and in what manner
those circumstances are affected by the
riches or poverty, by the advancing,
stationary, or declining state of the
society. (Smith 63)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
139
These are likewise the two topics that Marx deals with
in his "Wages" column; on pages I and II he addresses
himself to the circumstances that determine the rate
of wages, and on pages III-V he examines the condition
of the labourer in the three conditions of society
characterized by Smith as the advancing, stationary,
and declining state of the wealth of a nation. How-
ever, Marx does not confine himself to what Smith has
to say about these two topics in his chapter on Wages
of Labour. He also draws together propositions and com-
ments from elsewhere in the Weal th of Nations that un-
dermine Smith's optimistic assumption that wages are
the liberal reward of labour. This allows Marx to re-
veal the hidden discrepancies between Smith's descrip-
tion of the empirical conditions of labour and his
theoretical proposition that labour is the source of
value. Marx makes these discrepancies explicit in the
wages column of page VI and in the column of page VII
where the text to wages fills all three columns. We
will therefore divide our examination of Marx's text
into three parts: "Pages I and II: Labour as a Commodi-
ty: Wages as the Price of that Commodity", (ii) "Pages
III-V: The Wealth of Nations and the Labouring Poor"
and (iii) "Pages VI-VII: Contradictions in Smith's
Concept of Labour" •

Adam Smith opens his chapter on the Wages of Labour
with a recapitulation of his labour theory of value
and identifies wages with the whole produce of labour
which belongs to the labourer. However he recognizes
that this concept of wages belongs only to a classless
society of independent workmen
where the objective
conditions of production have not yet been transformed
into the private property of non-labouring classes:
The produce of labour constitutes the
natural recompense or wages of labour.
In that original state of things, which
precedes both the appropriation of land
and the accumulation of stock, the whole
produce of labour belongs to the labourer.
He has neither landlord nor master to
share with him. (Smith 64)
Smith devotes a brief discussion to the determi-
nation of wages in this situation, pointing out
that "had this state continued, the wages of labour
would have augmented with all those improvements
in its productive powers,

pendent work.a.ilen, but he does not introduce a new defini-

Marx skips over this discussion to the conclusion that

It should be reiterated that Smith's purpose in inclu-
ding "men" (Le. labour) in the category of commodity


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
148
was to demonstrate that the power of the capitalist
class to drive the wages of those they employed down to
a minimum could be annulled by the more powerful forces
of the impersonal market mechanism of supply and demand.
The argument that Marx proceeds to develop is that the
subjection of the labourers to the laws of supply and de-
mand by no means cancel out their subjection to the capi-
talist class but, on the contrary, reinforce it. All the
points that Marx draws upon to build this argument have
their origins in Smith's own work.
In arguing that the market forces of supply and de-
mand would ensure that wages would rise above their mini-
mum, Smith presupposed that the demand for labour is
higher than the supply. It is this "scarcitiy of hands"
that 11 occasions a competition among the masters, who bid
against one another, in order to get workmen" (Smith 68).
Marx first comment on Smith's characterization of labour
as a commodity notes the consequences on the workers of
the reverse situation, i.e. where supply is higher than
demand:
If the supply greatly exceeds the demand, then a sec-
tion of the workers declines into beggary or starva-
tion. Thus the existence of the worker is reduced to
the same conditions as the existence of any other com-
modity. The worker has become a commodity and he is
fortunate if he can find a buyer. (Marx 69-70)
That Marx ignores Smith's argument that a wage-rise will
follow from the excess of demand over supply may be in-
terpreted as Marx 1 s bias. But Smith himself gives good
reasons for supposing that there will very rarely be an


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
150
mechanism, the market price necessarily gravitates to-
wards the natural price. The pr:ocess by which the ba-
lance of supply and demand is ensured is described by
Smith as follows:
The quantity of every commodity brought to market
naturally suits itself to the effectual demand.
It is the interest of all those who employ their
land, labour, or stock, in bringing any commodity
to market, that the qumtit~{ never should exceed the
effectul demand; and it is the interest of all other
people that it never should fall short of that de-
mand.
If at any time it exceeds the effectual demand,
some of the component parts of this price must be
paid below their natural rate. If it is rent, the
interest of the landlords will immediately prompt
them to withdraw part of their land, and if it is
wages or profit, the interest of the labourers in
the one case, and of their employers in the other,
will prompt them to withdraw a part of their labour
or stock from this employment.
(Smith 57)
Marx summarizes this proposition from Smith but goes on
to point out the difficulties confronting the labourer's
withdrawal of his labour-power: Whereas Smith presents
each of the threeclasses as being in a position to act
according to their own description of the relationship
between worker and master, this is not the case.
If the supply exceeds the demand, one of the elements
entering into price - profit, ground rent, wages -
will be paid below its rate; a part of the supply
of these factors will then be withdrawn from this
use and the market price will graviate towards the
natural price. But (1) where there is an extensive
division of labour it is exterrnely difficult for the
worker to direct his labour into other uses, and (2)
because of his subordination to the capitalist, he
is the first to suffer hardship. (Marx 70)
Thus Smith's proposition that the pricing mechanism of
the market inexorably brings the market of commodities
in line with their natural price brings about the greater

dependence of the worker on the capitalist. Marx repeats
this consequence more explicitly in his next paragraph:
The worker, therefore, loses most, and loses inevi-
tably, from the gravitation of the market price to
the natural price. At the same time, it is the abi-
lity of the capitalist to put his capital to other
uses, which eit!ler conder'lns the worker, who is limi-
ted to one employment of his labour, to starvation
or forces him to accept every demand which the capi-
talist makes. (~1arx 70)
The continuous fluctuation in the market price by which
the natural price is established are likewise interpre-
ted by Marx to be a greater disadvantage to the worker
than to the other two classes. Smith himself had stated:
The occasional and temporary fluctuations in the mar-
ket price of any commodity fall chiefly upon those
parts of its price which resolve themselves into
wages and profit. That part which resolves itself
into rent is less affected by them. (S:r.tith 59)
Smith then goes on to discuss the way in which profit and
wages are affected by a sudden increase in the demand
of a particular commodity. Marx paraphrases Smith I s in-

differential effects on wages and profit:
The adventitious and sudden variations in market
price affect ground rent less than those parts of
the price which comprise profit and wages, but they
affect profit less than wages. In most cases, for
every wage that rises there is one t-mich rer.ains station-
ary and one which falls. (Marx 70 }
The point that Marx is here drawing attention to is that
on the one hand even if we can point to certain cases
·.-,here wages do indeed rise in response to a :,articular
demand, the wage-level for the working class as a whole
remain; the same. On the other hand, even if the wage-
level of the class as a whole remains stable, the indi-

vidual workers are continually confronted with instabi-
lity and insecurity of employment as a result of the
chance fluctuations of the market. The revenues of the
individual members of the other two classes are not ex-
posed to such fluctuations.
Although Smith argued that the market price would
inevitably gravitate to the natural price, he recognizes
at the end of his chapter several countervailing tenden-
cies that enable the market price to remain above the
natural price. All of these countervailing tendencies
spring from the capacity of the capitalist class to in-
terfere in the operation of the market. Smith's discus-
sion is presented on the last pages of chapter 7 (pages
60-61) and Marx correctly summarizes this discussion;
and draws out the implications for the workers:
The worker does not necessarily gain when the capi-
talist gains, but he necessarily loses with h1.m.
Thus the worker does not gain if the capitalist suc-
ceeds in maintaininq the market price above the na-
tural price by means of a manufacturing or commercial
secret, a monopoly or the favourable situation of
his property. (Marx 70)
For his next point, Marx returns to Smith's chapter on
wages and notes what Smith had to say about the relation-
ship between the price of provisions, on which the labour-
er's life depends, and the price of labour. Marx para-
phrases the following paragraph from Smith:
The scarcity of a dear year, by diminishing the demand
for labour, tends to lower its price, as the high
price of provisions tends to raise it. The plenty
of a cheap year, on the contrary, by increasing the
demand, tends to raise the price of labour, as the
cheapness of provisions tends to lower it. In the
ordinary variations of the price of provisions those

153
two opposite causes seem to counterbalance one an-
other; which is probably in part the reason why the
wages of labour are everywhere so much more steady
and permanent than the price of provisions.
In the first situation,that of a "dear year 11 ,Smith notes
that the demand for labour is diminished. This, as Marx
correctly points out, means that the supply of labour
exceeds demand and that many workers will be unemployed:
"In any event numbers of workers are Witt.out bread."
(Marx 70) In the second situation, where the demand for
labour is high, Smith points out that wages
may not
rise {even though the general laws governing all other
commodities are that prices will rise when demand is
higher than supply) since the law governing wages as
the price of the commodity labour is counterbalanced by
the law that equates wages with the bare minimum of pro-
visions necessary to sustain the labourer.
The last observation that Marx documents from Smith
is taken from Smith's chapter 1 O which discusses the
differences in wages and profits from one branch of pro-
duction to another. In this chapter, Smith discusses five
circumstances which bring about different levels of wages
in different employments. He then goes on to say:
Of the five circumstances, therefore, which vary the
wages of labour, two only affect the profits of stock .
• • • • It should follow from all this, that in the same
society or neighbourhood, the average and ordinary
rates of profit in the different employments of stock
should be more nearly upon a level than the pecuni-
ary wages of the different sorts of labour. They are
so accordingly. (Smith 111)
Marx paraphrases:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
154
Another disvantage of the worker: The wage rates of
different kinds of workers vary much more than do
the profits in the different branches in which capi-
tal is employed. In work, all the natural, spiritual
and social differences of individual activity appear
and are differently remunerated, while dead capital
maintains an unvarying performance and is indifferent
to real individual activity. (Marx 71 )
Why Marx should regard this as a disadvantage to the wor-
kers is not immediately clear. But if we set it in the
context of the points that Marx has already made and
the perspective that Marx has already developed, then it
does make sense to regard the differential remuneration
of workers as a disadvantage. Marx has identified com-
petition as a divisive force that prevents the workers
from combining in the same way that their masters can.
Thus whatever benefits individual workers may derive
from the differences in wage-levels in the various occu-
pations, the working class as a whole suffers from a re-
inforcement of the competitive force that subjugates the
working class to the power of the capitalist class.Marx
concludes his survey of the conditions that Smith noted
determine the rate of wages - all of which Marx has set
in the context of the relationship between the capitalist
and the worker - with a reference back to his earlier
comment that the worker necessarily loses when the capi-
talist loses. Marx notes that the loss experienced by
the worker is quite different from that experienced by
his employer:
In general, it should be noted that where worker
and capitalist both suffer, the worker suffers in
his existence while the capitalist suffers in the
profit of his dead mammon. (Marx i1)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.

6. 2 The Labouring Poor and the Wealth of Nations
At the bottom of page II, Marx announces his intention
to investigate the condition of the workers in each of
Smith's three states of wealth:
Let us now take three conditions in which society
may find itself, and consider the situation of the
worker in each of them. (Marx 71)
Marx's main focus is on the allegedly "favourable" si-
tuation of the workers in a country where wealth is in-
creasing. (He curtly dismisses the situation of decli-
ning wealth with Smith I s own observation about the wor-
kers in such an unfortunate nation: "none suffers so
cruelly from its decline as the working class"{Marx 71,
emphasis Marx 1 s; cf. Smith 249). (He reserves the situ-
ation of stationary wealth for a
later attack.)
Smith, in his description of the state of increa-
sing idleness in workers (as many of Smith I s contempo-
raties held), stimulated the workers to increased acti-
vitiy, industry and diligence (Smith 81). He uses as sup-
portive evidence the observation that
morkmen ... when they are liberally paid by the price,
are very apt to overwork themselves, and to ruin
their health and constitution in a few years {Smith
81-82).
Marx seizes on this observation to satirize the "favour-
able" situation of the workers.
But, in the first place, the raising of wages leads
to overwork among the workers. The more they want
to earn the more they must sacrifice their time and
perform slave labour in which their freedom is total-
ly alienated in the service of avarice. In so doing
they shorten their lives. This shortening of the life
span is a favourable circumstance for the working-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
156
class as a whole, since it makes necessary an ever
renewed supply of workers. This class must always
sacrifice a oart of itself I in order not to be
ruined as a ;_hole. {Marx 71, emphasis Marx 1 s)
Marx second and more fundamental point is the inevitable
effect on the woi.king class of the process of capital-
accumulation. Marx has already, in his "Profit" column
on the first page, defined capital as "stored-up labour
(Marx 85, emphasis Marx's, Smith 314), a definition that
Smith himself introduced in his chapter "Of the Accumu-
lation of Capital, or of Productive and Unproductive
Labour," {Smith 314-332), but did not consistently apply
in the rest of his work. Hence the accumulation of capi-
tal rr.eans the acc",r,.ulation of labour - but in the hands
of the non-labouring capitalist class. We already get a
hint of the conceptual framework (the alienation of la-
bour in the hands of a class whose interests are always
opposed to the labourers') (Smith 66) , which )larx will
later develop to reinterpret the contents and contra-
dictions of the Wealth of Nations. "Increasing wealth,"
states )rarx,
is only possible (a) when much labour is accumula-
ted, for capital is accumulated labour; when there-
fore, more of the worker• s product is taken from
him, when his own labour becomes opposed to him as
an alien possession, and when his means of existence
and his activity are increasingly concentrated in
the haI1ds of the capitalist. (Marx 72)
The last point that ~larx draws attention to is competi-
tion -
among both the labourers and the capitalists.
Smith had noted the importance of competition in his
discussion of the forces that determine the wage-rate:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
157
the larger the population, the greater the supply of
labour and the lower the level of wages. But in his
account of increasing national wealth, he admits the
premise and ignores the conclusion:
The most decisive mark of prosperity of any country
is the increase of the number of its inhabitants
(Smith 71)
The conclusion ±s filled in by Marx:
the competition among workers, on account of the
increase in their numbers, has become greater, more
abnormal and more violent. Consequently, a part of
the working class falls ir~to a condition of beggary
or starvation (Marx 73)
Marx further notes that the severity of competition
among the workers is aggravated not only by the higher
birth-rate, but also by the influx of ruined and bank-
rupt capitalists into the working class U-iarx 73) . The
flow of bankrupt capitalists into the working class is
a phenomenon that Smith includes in his description of
a declining state of wealth (Smith 73), but it appears
only implicitly in his analysis of a thriving economy.
As capitals increase in any country, the profits
which can be made by employing them necessarily
diminish. It gradually becomes more and more dif-
ficult to find within the country a profitable me-
thod of employing any new capital. There arises in
consequence a competition between different capi-
tals, the owners of one endeavouring to get posses-
sion of that employment which is occupied by an-
other. (Smith 336, Marx 92)
What Smith does not explicitly acknowledge in this pas-
sage is that capitalists not only endeavour, but actu-
ally succeed in getting possession of other capitals
and in driving other capitalists into bankrupt9y. Marx
draws the conclusions (which he argues in greater de-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
158
tail in the
11Profit" columns of pages VI, VIII-X (Marx
90-94), using as his evidence Smith's own words), that
the bankruptcy_ of capitalists is itself a necessary
phenomenon in the state of increasing wealth, and that
the concentration of capital in the hands of the sur-
viving capitalists becomes even greater, tending to-
wards oligopoly and eventual monopoly, the higher the
rate of increase in the nation's wealth (Marx 72-73).
The ruination of the losing capitalists has a two-fold
effect on the situation of the workers: first, it in-
creases the number of workers, and hence the competition
within the working class for jobs; secondly, it decreases
the number of surviving capitalists who are now few e-
nough to make a tacit agreement not to raise wages by
outbidding one another on the labour market.
Marx draws together the points that he has taken from
Smith into a concentrated attack on Smith's optimistic
assumption that the increase in a society's productivity
and wealth necessarily improves the condition of t!"!.e w;:,rldng
class.
Thus, even in the state of society which is most fa-
vourable to the worker, the inevitable result for
t~;.e wor}~er is oven..;or1: and premc';.ture death, reC:.uc-
tion to a machine, enslavement to capital which ac-
cumulated in menacing opposition to hL-rn, renewed com-
petition and beggary or starvation for a part of the
workers. (Marx 7 3)
Marx now raises the question:
Eventuall:::7, however, this state of growth must reach
its culmination. What is then the condition of the
o;orker? (f'1arx 73)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
159
In answer, he simply repeats word for word, Smith's ac-
count of a stationary society (i.e. a stagnant econo:rri.y)
(Smith 94-5, Marx 74). In doing so, Marx does not mis-
represent Smith in the slightest, for Smith himself was
fully aware that a society which "has acquired its full
complement of riches" is a stationary society (Smith 81).
Marx concludes by summing up the condition of labour
in all three of Smith's states of national wealth as one
of misery. What then is the benefit to the labouring po-
pulation of the improvement and expansion of their pro-
ductive powers? What then is the goal of production?
Thus, in a declining state of society, increasing
misery of the workers, in a progressive state, com-
plicated misery; and in the final state, stationary
misery. ·
Since, however, according to Smith (Smith 79,81),
a society is not happy in which the majority suffers,
and since the weali:hiest state of society leads to suf-
fering for the majority, while the economic system
(in general a society of private interests) leads to
this wealthiest state, it follows that social misery
is the goal of the economy. (Marx 74, emphasis Marx's)
Marx I s analysis of the situation of the labouring poor
in a society where weal th is growing, thus provides an
excellent example of his method of immanent critique. By
selecting points of detail from Smith I s account, and by
making connexions that Smith omitted, £-1:arx has succeeded
in refuting Smith's optimistic assertion that wages must
necessarily rise as the wealth of a nation grows - with-
out resorting to anything but Smith's own observatii.ons
and propositions!

Let us now adopt entirely the view·point of the eco-

He tells us that originally, and in principle,

But he adds immediately that, in fact, the worker

bourers
stripped of their means of production. In
Smith's model of civilization these

on page VII, not only in the "Wages" cohwm, but also

According to Smith, only the ~pansion of wealth can be

that

appears on the first three of the two-column pages

of the columnar format of his pages to show the conver-

7. 1
Capital and Land as the Rule of Private Property
over the Production Process
According to Smith, the major characteristic, common
to both profit and rent that distinguishes both these
revenues from wages, is the fact that capital and land
are aspects that allow their owners to enjoy a revenue
without having to work. Smith introduces his analysis
of profits thus:
The profits of stock, it may perhaps be thought,
are only a different name for the wages of a particular
sort of labour, the labour of inspection and direction.
They are, however, altogether different, are regula-
ted by quite sufficient principles, and bear no pro-
portion to the quantity, the hardship, or the inge-
nuity of this supposed labour of inspection and direc-
tion. They are regulated altogether by the value of
the stock employed, and are greater or smaller in
proportion to the extent of this stock. {Smith 48,
Marx86).
Similarly the revenue of the "idle" (Srnit!l Is
favourite ephitet for this class) landlords
costs them neither labour nor care, but comes to
them, as it were, of their own accord. (Smith 249,
Marx 104).
In other words, rent and profit are incomes that have
nothing to do with the exercise of one I s own personal
activity of labour. The distinguishing characteristic
shared by land and capital alike is that they are essen-
tial preconditions for the production-process carried out


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
168
by others, preconditions which in Smith's original state
of affairs were freely available to every labourer. The
arrival of civilized society obliterated the labourers'
right of access to the preconditions of their productive
activity, though in Smith's account, the nature of this
obliteration is left unsaid.
What Smith left unsaid serves as Marx's point of
departure. Drawing on a footnote from J. -B. Say, half of
which is quoted in the "Profit" column and the other half
paraphrased in the "Rent" column, Marx asserts that
capital, which he parenthetically defines as "private
ownership 11 of the products of other men's labour
(Marx
85, cf. Smith 314), and landownership are the outcone
of robbery and protected by legislation (Marx 85, 103).
Marx then turns to the Wealth of Nations to show what
this robbery consists of, and who its victims are. In
the case of capital, the victims are the labouring class
capital "is, as it were, a certain quantity of labour
stocked and stored up" (Marx 85, Smith 314). This syste-
matic transfer of labour into the hands of the capitalist
class is legalized as the "power of purchasing" {Marx 85,
Smith 31) . In the case of landownership, the victims are
the class of tenant-farmers. The landlords' power to
extort a tribute in the form of rent from the tenant
farmers rests both legally and in fact on their monopoly
of the land:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
169
The rent of land, therefore, considered as the price
paid for the use of land, is naturally a monopoly
price. It is not at all proportioned to what the
landlord may have laid out upon the improvement
of the land, or to what he can afford to take; but
to what the farmer can afford to give (Marx 103,
Smith 145).
In the "Profit" column, Marx next summarizes the
salient points about the nature of capital, as described
by Smith, and outlines the argument he will develop in
the rest of his core notebook in this column:
Capital is thus the power of command over labour
and its products. The capitalist possesses this
power, not on account of his personal or human qua-
lities, but as the owner of capital. His power is
the purchasing power of his capital, which nothing
can withstand.
we shall see later how the capitalist J
by means
of capital, exercises his power of command over
labour, and further, how capital itself rules the
capitalist. (Marx 85)
In the "Rent" column, Marx explicitly addresses
Smith's theoretical justification for the categorical
distinction between landownership and capital, rent and
profit. He cites several passages {Marx 104, Smith 145,
147, 268), all of which illustrate what Ricardo was later
to call Smith I s treatment of 11rent in the strict sense 11
(Ricardo 32), namely rent as compensation for the
"powers of nature, the use of which the landlord lends
to the farmer" (Smith 344, Marx 104). This definition
allowed Smith, and later Ricardo (Ricardo 34), to main-
tain that rent and profit were independent and mutually
exclusive categories. Marx points out the absurdity in
the theoretical assumption behind Adam Smith's strict


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
sense of rent, which Ricardo insisted was the only
correct concept of rent:
170
These propositions of Smith are important, because ...
they show clearly the perversion of concepts in
political economy, which transforms the fertility
of the soil into an attribute of the landowner.
(Marx 105).
For Marx, what is more important for understanding reality
and more consistent with Smith I s basic theory of society
as an aggregate of egoistic individuals, each pursuing
their own private interests, is Smith's less stringent
use of the term, rent, which emerges in his analysis of
the empirical conditions determining the size of the
landowners' revenue.
But let us now examine the rent of land as it is
determined in real life.
The rent of land is established by the struggle
between tenant and landlord. In all political
eccnomy we find that the hostile opposition of
interests, struggle and warfare, are recognized as
the basis of social organization. (Marx 105, cf.
Smith 145-14 7) •
Thus, whereas classical political economy starts
off from the premise that capital and land are separate
and distinct "private funds", whose inherent physical
properties have the power of generating a revenue to
their respective owners, Marx's point of departure is
not land and capital as physical assets, but private
property as a legalized relationship of social domination:
capital as domination over the working-class and land-
ownership as domination over the class of tenant farmers.
However, the hierarchical class structure of a society
based on private property and the privileged position of


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
171
members of the propertied classes vis-a-vis t..1'1ose who
lack property (both of which were recognized by Srni th,
as Marx's treatment of his first issue shows) does not
mean, in Smith's account, that these classes are power-
ful enough to overthrow the "natural" laws of social
organization and of economic development. On the con-
trary, according to Smith, the most powerful factor of
social and economic development is the individual pur-
suit of private interests, and it is precisely this
factor which precludes class rule, by bringing the mem-
bers of the propertied class into conflict and competition
with one another. In the rest of the "Profit" and "Rent"
columns, Marx examines the conditions under which this
competition occurs among the propertied classes, as
each individual property owner strives to maximise his
own profit or rent. In the "Profit" column, he shows
how the very forces which Smith identified as the cause
of competition, namely the anarchic pursuit of self-
interests, must ultimately undermine the very conditions
which allow competition to take place. The parallel argu-
ment developed in the "Rent" column leads to an equally
significant conclusion: that the anarchic pursuit of
self-interests among the landowners undermines the basis
of the power and revenue of this class, their monopoly
of land.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
7 .2
Competition and the Consolidation of the Rule
of Capital
172
Under his second subheading in the "Profit" column,
The Profit of Capital, Marx cites passages where Smith
analyses the ways in which individual capitalists deploy
their capitals in order to maximise their own profits,
and the conditions which serve to maximise the overall
rate of profit. There are three important points that
Marx establishes in this discussion. Firstl½ that profit
is in proportion to the size of the capital deployed
(Marx 86, 88, cf. Srni th 48, 51) . This means that in the
competition among the capitalists, each striving to
maximise their own profits, the owners of large capital
have a built-in advantage over the smaller capitalists.
Secondly, the proportions into which the exchangeable
value of a commodity is divided as wages, profit, and
rent are by no means fixed. The fluctuations in these
proportions, according to Smith, depend on the conditions
of the market and under conditions of limited market com-
petition, the profit which the capitalist is able to
pocket is so high that it
... eats up the whole of the rent of land and reduces
the wages of labour ..• to the lowest rate, the bare
subsistence of the labourer. (Marx 87, cf. Smith 97
-- emphasis Marx') .
Marx summarizes Smith's observations in the extent to
which capitalists can manipulate the market "by quite
respectable means" {Marx 87-88, Smith 60-62); hence the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
173
highest rate of profit, which drives wages down to a
minimum and rent down to nothing, is more likely to be
the rule than the exception. More important, however,
than the benefits which the capitalists derive from
manipulating the market is the third point which Marx
establishes: the division of labour, Smith's principle
cause of the weal th of nations, necessarily operates to
expand the capitalists' assets and revenues at. the
expense of the other two classes. In practice, as Smith
noted, the principle of the division of labour can be
applied to a far greater extent in manufacturing than
in agricultural production:
The nature of agriculture, indeed, does not admit
of so many subdivisions of labour, nor of so complete
a separation of one business from another, as manu-
factures ... The occasions for those different sorts
of labour returning with the different seasons of the
year, it is impossible that one man should be con-
stantly employed in any one of them. This impossi-
bility of making so complete and entire a separation
of all the different branches of labour employed in
agriculture, is perhaps the reason why the improve-
ment of the productive powers of labour in this art,
does not always keep pace with their improvement in
manufactures. (Smith 6).
But the more that the raw produce of the land is diverted
into the manufacturing process before being brought to
market as a finished commodity, the greater is the re-
duction of the landowning class 1 s share in the ex-
changeable value of that commodity:
As any particular commodity comes to be more
manufactured, that part of the price which resolves
itself into wages and profit comes to be greater in
proportion to that which resolves itself into rent.
(Smith 51, cf. Marx 88).


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
174
Thus the increasing application of the division of
labour, Smith's principal cause of a nation's wealth, is
itself a force in reducing the landlord's share of that
weal th. Moreover, as we have already seen from Marx's
analysis in the "Wages" column, the increasing division
of labour, which improves the productive powers of
labour, does not, as Smith anticipated, necessarily
improve the wages of labour. In the contrary, concludes
Marx, drawing on another proposition from Smith concerning
the relationship between the division of labour in the
manufacturing process and the expansion of capital (Marx
88, cf. Smith 51), the expansion of capital in the manu-
facturing process -- and therefore of the profits of
capital since "profits must always bear some proportion
to capital" (Marx 88, cf. Smith 48) -- prevents the
labourers from benefitting from the improvement in their
productive powers.
Thus the increasing part which human labour,
compared with raw material, plays in the manu::actured
product does not increase the wages of labour but
increases partly the nunber of capitals and partly
the size of every subsequent capital in relation to
that which precedes it (Marx 88).
In short, what Marx establishes in this discussion
of his second issue, the profit of capital, is that the
laws governing the production of Smith's wealth of nations
automatically ensure that the capitalist class will
increasingly appropriate and control that wealth.
Marx I s third subheading, The Rule of Capital over

178
The structure of Marx's argument in the
11Profit"
column is repeated in the "Rent" column: the second and
third issues set the stage for understanding the fourth,
an analysis of the effects of competition on the land-

Competition among the landowning class is a topic
scarcely mentioned by Smith, because in his analysis, the
monopoly of land renders the landowners immune to com-
petition. For Smith, the basis characteristic common to
all landlords is "their indolence, which is the natural
effect of the ease and security of their situation"
{Smith 249). Unlike the capitalists, the landlords do
not have to seek out the most favourable productive
conditions for deploying their assets, nor do they have
to compete on the market for the sale of their wares. They
simply demand from the tenants the !naximum for the use
of the land which the tenants can afford to pay.
Nevertheless, in making rent a compo!lent part of the
exchangeable value of a comrr1.odity, Smith admits that the
landlords are indeed affected by the cornpeti tion among

paid for the use of their land, only if their tenants
succeed in marketing their produce at a price high enough

of his second issue, the rent of land as the struggle
between tenant and landlord, consists of a selection of

in some cases, the landlord I s power to extort a rent
because of his monopoly of a certain piece of land is

which determine pr ices on the market. On the other hand,
because of the permanent demand for food, "the only pro-
duce of land which always and necessarily affords a rent

original source of rent". (Smith 164, Marx 107}.
Implicit in Marx's discussion is the fact that in
Smith's analysis tenant farmers do not constitute an

Marx I s third issue, the self-interests of the land-

and {Smith 423),
is that the interests of society are best promoted by
the individualistic pursuit of self-interests under con-

Marx 108).

Marx lists the circumstances which Smith used to demonstrate

Thus, the interest of the landowner, far from
being identical with that of society, is bitterly
opposed to the interests of the tenants, the agri-
cultural labourers, the industrial workers and the
capitalists. (Marx 111) .
Thus, not only does Marx's analysis demonstrate that
the self-interests of the landlords are no less opposed

to increase their own private wealth bring them into direct
conflict with the capitalists, who are likewise engaged
in exactly the same business of maximising their own pri-
vate property.
The fourth issue in the "Rent" colunn, the competition
which arises within the landowning class as a result of

In general, large landed property and small lan-

is
the possibility of many-sided exploitation, the

For the small landowner, the overall increase in the
society's wealth puts him at an ever greater disadvan-

unable to use the economies of scale available to the
large landowners. Consequently, his costs of production
the supply of necessary capital and the hiring of

landowners, and the surplus that he receives from the

the small capitalist: the revenue which he can derive
