CHJI.PTER VIII
Marx's Outer Sheets:
The Movement of Private Property
The physical structure of Marx I s First :Manuscript
is such that with page XVI we come full circle back to
page I (see Appendix II). We have seen how Marx deve-
loped his argument by staying strictly within the pre-
mises and propositions of Smith's Wealth of Nations.
Thus the core notebook represents a closed circle,
the contents of which are primarily drawn from Smith's
Weal th of Nations -
and in this form they had the
appearance of a well-organized and harmonious whole.
But in V.arx's core notebook, the harmonious interaction
of the parts of his closed, self-contained system has
been transformed into powerful internal contradictions
which burst as under the external shell, imposed upon
it by the science of political economy. This external
shell, the structure of the commodity, must now be
examined in connexion with these internal contradic-
tions. This Marx proceeds to do in the outer sheets of
his notebook. This new procedure necessarily goes beyond
the realm of politic al economy, because it requires
that the very foundations of Adam Smith's Inquiry into
the Nature and Causes of the Wealth of Nations, namely
his assumption that contemporary society corresponds to


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
188
the "natural order of things", must be laid aside in
order to grasp the real structure and development of
the weal th of nations.
The first five outer pages, which contain Marx's
intial attempts to go beyond the categories of politi-
cal economy and to develop an entirely different frame-
work for understanding the real structure of the pro-
duction-process, appear in the published text as the
continuation of the third chapter, "The Rent of Land".
Consequently the significance of the transition in
Marx's investigation into the nature of commodity-pro-
duction has been overlooked. On the outer sheets, Marx
is no longer carrying out an immanent critique of the
conceptual apparatus of political economy, but is now
attempting to develop his own more adequate framework
for understanding the nature and development of commo-
dity-production.
Thus Marx's argument no longer takes the form of
juxtaposing contradictory passages from Adam Smith and
other writers. It now takes the form of a polemic
against "romantic" social reformers, who viewed their
contemporary society as a situation of anarchy and
wanted to turn the clock back to reestablish the tra-
ditional stability of feudal society. The fact that
Marx, in introducing his own framework for understan-
ding the contents of political economy, immediately
confronts the question of how the present state of
affairs can be changed, is an important clue to Marx's


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
189
basic divergence from the perspective of political
economy: namely that the social circumstances in which
men find themselves are the consequences of human action
and that these circumstances can therefore be radically
changed by human action. For Adam Smith, the question
was: how can we understand our present society in or-
der to predict the "natural" future development of the
existing state of affairs? For Marx, the question was:
how can we understand the existing state of affairs in
order to change its "natural" development? Adam Smith
sought to understand, ignoring the need and possibility
for radical change; the romantic social reformers souqht
radical change, ignoring the possibility and the need
for a thorough understanding of the forces at work in
present society.
Marx's immanent critique of Smith in his core notebook
has revealed that the laws governing commodity society
are not to be regarded as "natural laws", as laws in-
herent in the very nature of human production. Rather
these laws stem from the rule of capital over contem-
porary society. How this rule of capital has developed
and come to take on a self-sustaining dynamic of its
own is the topic of Marx's outer sheets. In his outer
sheets, Marx focusses on the contradictions which he has
revealed in the categories of Smith's commodity in order
to develop a new understanding of the nature and causes
of the wealth of nations. The argument that Marx
develops in his outer sheets is that the contradictions


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
190
in Smith I s account can be understood as interrelated
aspects of the historical development of private pro-
perty, a development which has its own unifying prin-
ciple, but which is diametrically opposed to the in-
terests of human society. Thus :-iarx demonstrates that
what Smith had taken to be the promotion of social de-
velopment and material well-being through the pursuit
of private interests is the total subordination of
human society to the rule of private property.
He does so by conceptualizing private property as
"an alien power ruling over men" and locates the histo-
rical origins of this alienation in feudal society. With
the expropriation of the land from those who worked the
land, the land becomes a power -- the power of private
property -- which dominates not only the expropriated
but also the expropriators.
Feudal landed property is already essentially land
which has been disposed of, alienated from men, and
now confronting them in the shape of a few great
lords.
Already in feudal landownership the ownership
of the soil appears as an alien power ruling over
men. The serf is the product of the land. In the
same way, the heir, the first-born son belongs to
the land. It inherits him. The rule of private pro-
perty begins with the ownership of land which is
its basis (Marx 114).
Marx then goes on to explain why it is that domination
exercised over the whole society by this impersonal and
alien power, private property, remains invisible. The
rule of private property appears as the personal rule
of those who own the land and the personal obligations


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
191
of those who work the land. In the view of the Romantics
-
a viewpoint that Marx is explicitly criticizing in
this passage -
these personal ties between the aristo-
cracy and the labouring serfs formed the natural basis
of human society. In a similar way, Smith had argued that
the socio-economic system that he described -
the
atomistic pursuit of self-interests -
was a manifesta-
tion of the natural liberty of man and the natural prin-
ciple of human society. Marx argues against both
assumptions and notes that one and the same unnatural
principle structures both types of society, namely the
alien rule of private property. Both types of society
are systems of private property.
In order to analyse these two types of society as
systems of private property, Marx reconceptualizes
Smith 1 s three components of the commodity as three
aspects of the movement of private property. On page
XXII of his outer sheets, Marx writes:
Political economy begins with the fact of pri-
vate property; it does not explain it. It conceives
the material process of private property, as it
occurs in reality, in general and abstract formulas
which then serve it as laws. It does not comorehend
these laws; that is, it does not show how they
arise out of the nature of private property.
{Marx 120).
Marx here indicates that all the contradictions he has
uncovered in Adam Smith's account of the laws governing
the production and distribution of wealth are rooted
in the nature of private property. Marx 1 s synthesis of


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
192
these contradictions in the outer pages is therefore an
attempt to uncover and describe the nature of private
property as a movement with its own dynamic of self-
development. In the first five pages of his outer sheets,
Marx locates the historical origins of this movement in
the expropriation of the land that established feudal
society. He then interprets the transition from Europe 1 s
feudal past to the contemporary capitalist society as
the freeing of private property from the restrictions
still imposed upon it by the conditions of its birth.
Capitalism is thus the manifestation of private property
in its fully developed form, and this is what Adam Smith
has described, without, however, recognizing the develop-
mental process itself. In this discussion, Marx argues
that the categorical distinction that Smith drew between
land and capital, rent and profit has to be understood
as a historical distinction. In the last six pages of his
outer sheets Marx argues that the movement of private
property in its fully developed form, i.e. as capital, is
the outward manifestation of the alienation of labour.
Implicit in Marx 1 s analysis of alienated labour is the
argument that the distinction that Smith drew between
wage-labour and capital is really a distinction between
labour as subject and labour as object, in other words
between the producing subject and the object produced.
Thus the alienation of labour and the movement of pri-
vate property are two sides of one and the same relation-
ship. In Adam Smith, these two sides appear on the one


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
193
hand in his description of the activity of the wage-
labouring class, in particular the sub-category of this
class that Smith designates as productive labourers in
contrast to unproductive labourers, and on the other hand,
in his description of the activity of the capital-accu-
mul.a ting class.
The germ of Marx 1 s concept, the movement of private
property, can already be found in Adam Smith.
Smith has assumed that the private property of the
capital-owning class was, like the private property of
the landowning class the result of an irreversible event
in the prehistory of civilized society 1). However it
is clear that what the capitalist owns today is not what
he may have inherited, stolen or (most unlikely) earned
by the sweat of his own labour, twenty years ago. The
original stock of material has been used up, the origi-
nal machines worn-out or out-moded, and the original
commodities all sold (or rotted). Nevertheless, the capi-
talist sti.11 owns private property, and most likely,
several times more than his original assets. How can
an event in the prehistory of civilization explain this
ncontemporary economic fact" (Marx 121)?
Smith himself was fully aware that the private stock
of any individual gets used up and consumed over time,
and in the second volume
of the Wealth cf Nations


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
194
gives the following advice to the owners of stock on
how to divide and deploy it cautiously and avoid falling
into the fate of the "labouring poor": But when (a man)
possesses stock sufficient to maintain him for months or
years, he naturally endeavours to derive a revenue from
the greater part of it; reserving only so much for his
immediate consumption as may maintain him till this
revenue
begins to come in. His whole stock, therefore,
is distinguished into two parts. That part which, he
expects, is to afford him this revenue, is called his
capital •••
There are two different ways in which a capital may
be employed so as to yield a revenue or profit to its
employer.
First, it may be employed in raising, manufacturing,
or purchasing goods, and selling them again with a pro-
fit. The capital employed in this manner yields no
revenue or profit to its employer, while it either re-
mains in his possession, or continues in the same shape.
The goods of the merchant yield him no revenue or profit
till he sells them for money, and the money yields him
as little till it is again exchanged for goods (Smith
262). From this analysis of what Smith terms "circula-
ting capital", it becomes very clear that private proper-
ty can only be conserved (i.e. replaced) by being put
in motion, moreover a continual unending motion, from
money to commodities and from commodities to money: His


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
195
capital is continually going from him in one shape, and
returning to him in another, and it is only by means of
such circulation, or successive exchanges, that it can
yield him any profit. Such capitals therefore may very
properly be called circulating capitals (Smith, 262-263).
According to Smith, circulating capital can form only
a part of the cap~ tali st I s total capital. The other part
is "fixed capital", which Smith defined as that capital
which is
employed in the improvement of land, in the purchase
of useful machines and instruments of trade, or in
suchlike things as yield a revenue or profit with-
out changing masters, or circulating any further
(Smith 263).
But Smith then goes on to assert that all fixed capital
is dependent on circulating capital for its origin and
maintenance:
Every fixed capital is both originally derived from,
and requires to be continually supported by a circu-
lating capital. All useful machines and instruments
of trade are originally derived from a circulating
capital, which furnished the materials of which
they are made, and the maintenance of the workmen
who make them. They require a capital too of the
same kind to keep them in constant repair.
No fixed capital can yield any revenue but by
means of a circulating capital. The most useful
machines and instruments of trade will oroduce
nothing without the circulating capital-which
affords the materials they are employed upon, and
the maintenance of the work.men who employ them
(Smith 266-267).
Fixed capital therefore does not yield a profit
of its own acccrd: "The intention of fixed capital is to
increase the productive powers of labour, or to enable
the same number of labourers to perform a much greater


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
196
quantity of work (Smith 271)". In other words, fixed
capital is employed on improving the means of production,
in order that the input costs of the capitalist may be
lowered and his output of goods increased. In other
words, fixed capital is only a potential source for
profit and these potential profits can only be actua-
lized {realized) in the movement of the circulating ca-
pital. Without circulating capital, fixed capital, which
serves to improve the productive powers of labour, is
of no use to the capitalist. Every capitalist must have
both.
Smith further subdivided circulating capital into
four forms: "money, provisions, materials and finished
work (Smith 272) ". Money, the first form of circulating
capital, is "the great wheel of circulation (Smith 273) ",
"by means of which all the other three are circulated and
distributed to their proper consumers (Smith 266) ". The
other three forms, if they are not excha:r..ged for money,
but remain in the possession of the capitalist can only
be employed either as reinvestment in the "fixed capi-
tal" of his enterprise (i.e. the improvement and ex-
pansion of machinery) or for his own ili'.mediate consump-
tion. Neither of these destinies will realize a orofit.
Hence the sole source of the capitalists' "neat revenue,
which must consist altogether of his profits (Smith
273)" is not circulating capital in general, but only
its first form, money. The capitalists' original capital


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
197
may be replaced by the other three forms of circulating
capital, but it can only be expanded by money.
Smith 1 s analysis of the two parts of capital, fixed
and circulating, helps us to understand Marx's concep-
tualization of private property as a movement, and his
insistence that private property is not a thing, or a
once-and-for-all event in the past, but a continual
ongoing process in the present. We can also understand
why Marx characterises money as the purest, most mobile
form of private property, the expression of its fullest
development:
In general, movement must triumph over im....1obility .••
and money over the other forms of private property
(143 -- 2nd. Ms. emphasis Marx's).
The problem with which we began this section was to
explain the capitalists' ownership of their present
private assets. Smith himself did not raise the question
in this form, but unwittingly, in his advice on how to
replace and accumulate capital, he gave the answer.
Capital is conserved and replaced if, and only if, it is
set in motion. A motionless capital, i.e. one that re-
mains in the hands of the capitalist for his own private
consumption, is necessarily depleted over time, and the
owner of motionless private property is rendered pro-
pertyless. Moreover capital is only expar..ded (i.e. re-
placed with profit) if, and only if, the outcome of
this movement is money. The faster the motion, the
higher the profit and the greater the accumulation of


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
198
capital in the hands of the capitalists.
Thus, in characterizing private property as move-
ment, Marx is laying bare the hidden kernel of capitalist
private property in the Wealth of Nations. By replacing
Smith's categories of "fixed" and
11circulating 11 capital
that artificially separates two aspects of this movement,
Marx re-establishes their essential interdependence 2> and
restores their unity as "moments" in the movement. Further
the very term private property implicitly contains the "unity
of opposites", that Hegel characterised as the outcome of
the speculative stage of his dialectical method: namely
the privation of those who produce the wealth of nation
and the appropriation of that wealth by the non-producing
classes. Marx shows that the historical development of
private property is a phenomenon which originated with
the alienation of land under feudalism., and which deve-
lops its own self-sustaining dynamic with the alienation
of labour under capitalism. The alienation of land is
the generating cause, and the alienation of labour is
the sustaining cause of the historical existence of
private property. Moreover, the alienation of labour is
thereby also the sustaining cause of alienated labour


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
itself, private property being the outward manifesta-
tion of this alienation.
199
Thus the new organizing principle which Marx intro-
duces to reorder the contents of the Wealth of Nations,
the movez::,.er..t of private property, is simultaneously
the concept of alienation. ~his concept of alienation
is one of the most fundarnen tal categories in Be gel I s
philosophy, but, as Georg Lukacs, has pointed out in
his scholarly work on Hegel's intellectual development,
this concept by no means originated with Hegel. It was
the term used to denote the distinguishing characteristic
of the commodity, namely the fact that it is not merely
a product of labour, but an exchangeable product, some-
thing belonging to a private individual which can be
sold or otherwise alienated to another private indivi-
dual:
"EntauBerung" and "Entfremdung" are in and of them-
selves nothing new. They are simply the German
translation of the word "alienation", which was
used in English economies to denote the alienabi-
li ty of the commodity (VeriiuBerung der Ware) , and
also in almost all social contract theories of na-
tural law to characterize the loss of man's ori-
ginal freedom, and the transfer and alienation of
this freedom to the society which was established
by contract. (Lukacs, 1973, pp. 828-829}.
Marx I s originality lies in the fact that he applies
this concept not to the market relations between pri-
vate individuals, not to the exchanging of the products
of labour, after they have been produced, but to the
structure of the production process itself, to the class


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
200
relationship between those who produce and those who
own the means of production. He ist thereby able to
argue that private property and alienation do not begin
with the emergence of commodity production and the con-
frontation of private buyers and sellers on the market,
but are rooted in the separation of the means of pro-
duction from the producers themselves, a separation
which had already occurred and been institutionalized
in Europe's feudal past.

CHAPTER IX
Alienation of Land as the Root of
Private Property
9.1.
The Form and Context of Pages XVII to XXI
202
Marx's immanent critique of Smith's categories, pro-
fit and rent, has shown that the logical consequence of
Smith's laws of economic development -
the improvement
of the productive powers of labour through the division
of labour and the accumulation of capital -
is the
collapse of the distinction between these two catego-
ries and the disappearance of competition. Here on the
first five pages of his outer sheets, Marx isolates
Smith 1 s category, rent of land, in order to identify
the historical development and social conditions pre-
supposed by this category and thereby show how the
logical disappearance of the rent of land i::iplied by
Smith's own analysis of economic development can be
explained by the historical transition of Europe from
a feudal economy based on agricultural production to
a capitalist economy based on industry. This is the
major theme repeated again and again on these first
five pages of the outer sheets: the necessary transfor-
mation of feudal landed property into capital.
In his core notebook, Marx had demonstrated how


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
203
the principles that Smith had uncovered as the laws
governing the creation and distribution of wealth in a
commodity-producing society necessarily undermined the
status of rent as a category independent of profit and
the status of land as a category independent of capi-
tal. Thus the category "rent of land" is revealed to be
a "transient and disintegrating" term. Why then should
Smith have regarded the rent of land as an eternal
and universal component of the commodity? Marx I s first
five pages of his outer sheets are devoted to answering
this question. His answer is that the expropriation of
the land from those who work the land is a necessary
precondition for the rise of capital. This is expressed
most clearly in the format of these five pages.
The form.at of the first five pages .of Marx's outer
sheets (XVII-XXI) differs significantly from all the
other three-column pages of Marx I s notebook. The :.Rent
of Land" -
and not the "Profit of Capital" -
occu-
pies the centre column, and only this centre column
contains a written text. The two flanking columns,
11Wages of Labour" on the left and "Profit of Capital"
on the right contain no writing except for their re-
spective underlined column headings. This format visually
illustrates the subject matter of these pages: Europe's
feudal past -· where wages and profit do not yet exist.
Moreover, the argument now takes the form of a polemic
against "romantic (p. 113)" social reformers, who, aware


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
204
of the multitude of social problems that were mounting
with the progressive industrialization of modern society
and the commercialization of social relationship1),
wanted to turn the clock back, and re-establish the
authority of the landed nobility. Their program of
social reform, argues Marx, is based on ignorance
1) of the real basis of the power of the feudal aristo-
cracy and
2) of the real course of social development.
9.2.
The Rule of Private Property: Privation and
Appropriation
The continuity between feudalism and capitalism that
Marx examines is the similarity between the serf-land-
lord and the wage-labourer-capitalist relationship. In
both relationships, the power of the dominant class
lies in their private ownership of (some or all of)
the means of production; conversely, the subordination
of the producing class lies in their alienation :Erom
the objective conditions of their producing activity.
The land on which the serf works belongs to the feudal
lord, the factory where the wage-labourer works belongs


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
205
to the capitalist; "alienus", the Latin root of "alien-
tation", literally means "belonging to another". Marx's
analysis of the rule of private property is an analysis
of the "thing" that is privately owned, but the relation-
ship between those who appropriate (alienate) and those
who are deprived (alienated).
Privat property as Rousseau had arg'l.:ed so force-
fully 2l, is not a nature-given phenomenon. It is a
human product, the outcome of human activity. The pro-
cess which creates the institution of private property
is the simultaneous activity of privation and appropri-
ation. In feudalism, the serfs are deprived of the land
by the feudal lords' act of appropriation through plunder
and arr:ted conquest. The loss inflicted on the serfs is
not a once-and-for-all event, a loss that is suffered
and forgotten, because what they r.ave been deprived of
is their access to the means of survival, the means of
satisfying their basic needs of food, clothir:g and


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
206
lodging. Thus the feudal lords' act of appropriation gives
them not only control of the land that they have appro-
priated, but also control over the landed population
that they have dispossessed. The serfs have the option
of submitting themselves to the rule of the lord, in
exchange for access to their means of production, their
means of survival, or of dying from starvation. The
institution of private property that legitimates the
private ownership of the means of production necessari-
ly creates a ruling class of property-owners and a sub-
ordinate class of propertyless producers.
In feudalism, the hierarchical class relationship
is explicitly recognized in the political prerogatives
of the aristocracy (which literally means "the rule of
the best"), in the institutions of oligarchy (rule of
the few) a:1d monarchy (rule of one) and in the poli ti-
cal unfreedorn of the majority, the labouring serfs. But
what is not explicitly recognized is that the political
subordination of the serfs and the power exercised over
them by the feudal aristocracy rests not on the fact
that nobles are born noble, but on their private proper-
ty, on the fact that they (and not their serfs) inherit
the family estate, the monopoly of land:
feudal landed property is already essentially
land which has been disposed of, alienated from
men and now confronting them in the shape of a few
great lords. O·'.:arx p. 114)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
9.3.
207
Landed Property as the Root of Private Property:
Capital as its Essence
In locating the real basis of feudal lord's power
in the private ownership of land, Marx draws attention
to the essential sirnilari ty between feudal and capita-
list society: both are modes of production where the
producers (serfs and wage-labourers, respectively) are
separated from the means of production, and are subor-
dinated to those who have appropriated them. The diffe-
rence between feudalism and capitalism lies in the
different form that social wealth takes: in feudalism,
landed property gives the owner the right to appropri-
ate the products of others; in capitalism, money -- which
as Smith himself had noted, gives to its possessor "a
certain command over all the labour, or over all the
produce of labour which is then in the market (Smith
31) ". On the first five pages of his outer sheets, (and
again in his second manuscript) Marx identifies the
similarities between these two forms of social weal th,
which have in common the fact that the existence of this
social wealth is confined to the private property of the
non-producers, in order to show that the relationship
between them is not a definitional (categorical) distinc-
tion, but a developmental sequence. In other words, the
different forms of private property correspond to
different stages in the historical development of the
wealth of nations. Land is the embryonic, undeveloped


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
208
form of private property, for private property in
this form is still restricted to a local basis and a
particular owner. Money represents private property as
the most universal form of wealth, and in this form
private property acquires its fully-developed form and
its total independence from any restrictions. Marx
summarizes this course of development by quoting two
proverbs: "so the medieval adage, nulle terre sans
seigneur, (no land without a master) is replaced with
a new adage, l' argent n I a pas de mai tre (money has no
master) 11
(Marx 115) •
What are the distinctive characteristics of these
two forms of private property that allow Marx to argue
that their relationship is one of developmental con-
tinuity? The first and most fundamental is the distinc-
tion between moveable and immoveable private property,
a distinction that Smith himself made.
When land, like moveables, is considered as the
means only of subsistence
and enjoyment, the natu-
ral law of succession divides it, like them, among
all the children of the family ••.
But when land was considered as the means, not
of subsistence, but of power and protection, it
was thought better that it should descend undivided
to one. (pp. 361-362).
Smith is here referring to the origin and motive of
the feudal law of primogeniture governing the transfer
of landed property from one generation to the next


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
209
3)
(Smith 952}
• This law ensured that each estate descen-
ded undivided to the first son. Thus land was immoveable
private property in the sense that strict political re-
strictions were imposed on the transfer of property.
It could not be brought to market and was therefore not
a cornmodity.4) Money, on the other hand, is a form of
private property that is always being transferred from
one owner (the buyer) to another (the seller). It is
the most exchangeable of cornmodi ties (Smith 28) or in
Marx's words, it is a "mobile and restless monopoly
(p. 11S)"_5>
A second distinction is the different forms of
labour employed to look after and work the private pro-
perty of the owners.
The workers on the estate are not in the con-
dition of day-labourers (Marx 1 1 4) but are partly
the property of the lord, as in the case of serfs,
and partly stand to him in relations of respect,
subordination and duty~ His relation to them is
therefore directly political.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
210
Under feudalism, the lords inherited their serfs along
with the land. There was no such thing as a labour-
market. The serfs were obliged to render their labour
to the manor on which they lived, and their relation-
ship to the manorial lord was not confined to their
work-activity, but included social and political ob-
ligations and ties. Under capitalism on the other hand,
wage-labourers are theoretically free to sell their
6)
labour to any employer.
They have no formal ties to
their masters outside the working-day and their politi-
cal status (their citizenship rights) is no different
7)
from the masters who employ them.
In capitalism there
is apparently no ruling class, and no politically dis-
possessed unfree subordinate class.
Thus under capi talisrn, not only do the means of
production become free to move to any buyer, but so too
do the producers themselves.
A third distinction that Marx notes between feudal
private property and capitalist private property is
the different function that each serves the owner.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
211
For the landed feudal aristocracy, the land that
they owned was a source of revenue which they used to
demonstrate and confirm their aristocratic status by
conspicuous consumption: 8 )
The lord does not try to extract the maximum
profit from his estate. Se rather consumes what
is there, and tranquilly leaves the care of produ-
cing it to the serfs and tenant farmers. That is
the aristocratic condition of landownership which
reflects a romantic glorv upon its lords (p. 115).
The capitalists, on the other hand, regard their pri-
vate property as a means, not of personal consumption,
but of production. Their private assets, capital, are
the source of their revenue, profit, which is to be re-
invested in further production. Money, says the pro-
verb, makes rnoney.9)
However, the fact that feudal landed property ser-
ved the function, for its lordly owners, of consumption,
the enjoyment of the fruits of the earth, does not ne-
gate the fact that this land was the means of production
for the serfs who had to labour in order to make the
fruits of the earth available to the lords.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
212
If we now compare the feudal and the capitalist
forms of private property (landed property and money,
respectively) in terms of the three differences noted
above, then we can say that under feudalism, the private
ownership of the means of production ( land) was
1) a monopoly of a few noble families, that could only
be passed on to descendants of the same family and
was hence unfree to move to any proprietor;
2) worked by unfree labour;
3) and worked for the sake of consumption.
Under Capitalism, the private ownership of the means
of production is
1} a monopoly of the capitalist class, but subject to
competition and transfer in exchange for money;
2) worked by formally free labour;
3) and worked for the sake of production.
Marx's critical analysis of Smith's category, "Rent
of Land", has already demonstrated that with the develop-
ment of a cornmodi ty-producing society, landed property
is transformed into capital and rent into profit.lo)
Marx's concept of the movement of private property is
an attempt to understand this fact, not as an isolated


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
event, but as part of a continuous development, that
had its origins in the historical past.
213
9.4.
The Movement of Private Property as the Process
of Alienation. Division and Accumulation
In identifying the transition from feudalism to
capitalism as the movement or development of private
property, Marx is assertir..g that this transition should
not be seen as a radical rupture in history, as Smith
had done, by equating feudalism with the period of bar-
barism and his own society wi.th the stage of civiliza-
tion. The principle of private property basic to both
the feudal and the capitalist system is the private
ownership of the means of production in the hands of
the non-producing class, and conversely the alienation
(exclusion, deprivation) of the producers from their
objective conditions of production. Though Smith did not
see this historical continuity, Marx uses many of his
observations on the nature of feudalism to demonstrate
the continuity. Thus Smith, like Marx, elaborated on the
political fetters on the redistribution of the means of
production, imposed by the feudal laws of primogeni-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
214
ture,11 )which hindered the development of the productive
powers of labour. According to Smith, the preoccu-
pation of the feudal proprietors with political affairs
meant that they "had no leisure to attend to the culti-
vation and improvement of land," {Smith 364) and hence
there could be little expansion of wealth.
In other words, those who owned the r.i.eans of pro-
duction were not interested in them for the sake of
promoting production, but for the sake of retaining and
promoting their own local authority and politic al im-
portance. The feudal lords failed to exploit the full
potential of their ownership of the means of production.
How was this fetter on the development of the produc-
tive powers broken? By precisely the same process which
originally gave birth to private property: the division
and accumulation of land and the alier..ation of the pro-
ducing serfs from that land. Feudalism had its origins
as Smith himself observed in the dividing up of the land
through plunder and armed conquest, and its subsequent
consolidation (accumulation} into feudal estates, whose
indivisibility was secured by the feudal laws of inheri-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
215
tance (primogeniture) 12). The landed population, dis-
possessed of their means of production, were forced
to alienate their activity of production (labour) to
the feudal lords. The second stage in the development
of private property occurred with the breakdown of the
principle of primogeniture and the division of the
large feudal estates into smaller alienable proper-
ties .13} As soon as this occurs,
no law can keep it {land) any longer in a
few predestined hands. The direct consequence
is its fragmentation in many hands, a prey
to industrial capital.
(Marx, 118)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
216
Thus the second stage in the development of private
property, the transition from feudalism to capitalism,
is, like the first, a process of division (of the for-
merly indivisible estates) and accumulation. But this
second accumulation transfers landed property out of
the hands of the feudal lords and into the hands of the
capitalists. At the same time, there is a further aliena-
tion of the producers. The serfs are not included in
the sale of the land, on which they worked. Left handless
and workless, they are forced to migrate, dispossessed
of everything, except their labour-power.
The importance of Marx's analysis is this: private
property is not a thing, but an ongoing process, a
process that develops its own dynamic of development.
Its precondition is the alienation of the producing
subjects from the objective conditions of production,
its movement is the double process of division and
accumulation. Once this process had been initiated in
the establishment of feudal society, only the enforce-
ment of political restrictions could hold back its
potential development. ':'he transition from feudalism to
capitalism was rendered possible, not by a violent up-
rooting of the feudal institution of landed property,
but by liberating the latent potential of that property:
by freeing the elements of production, both land and
labour,14) from the extraneous goals of conspicuous


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
217
consumption and political status, to the self-defining
goal, under the direction of the capitalist class, of
production for production's sake.
In sum, the serfs are forced to submit themselves
to the feudal lord,
because he has alienated their
major source of survival from them: hence the structure
of feudal society. The landlords, in turn, are forced
to yield their monopoly of the land, to the competition
of the capitalists, because their assets, land, be-
come alienable: hence the transition from feudalism to
capitalism. The alienation of land manifests itself, at
first, in the division of land and its accumulation
into the individisible estates of the feudal lords;
and secondly, in the division of these estates into
marketable comrnodi ties, which are accumulated by the
purchasing power of capital.
Thus in revealing ( 1) that the real basis of the
power of the feudal aristocracy is their monopoly of
land and not their nobility of birth and (2) that the
real course of social development once private pro-
perty has been established is the self-development
of private property itself, Marx is laying the foun-
dations of a new interpretative franework =or the history
of European development. This framework rests on an
extension of Smith I s analysis of the developmental

CHAPTER X
Alienate?, Labour as
Fully Developed Private Property
The last six pages of Marx's notebook contain his
famous theory of alienated labour. The contents of
these pages have been published as a separate chapter
and have been interpreted and discussed in the secon-
dary literature as though Marx's presentation of
alienated labour has nothing to do with the argument
which he developed in the rest of his notebook. As a
result Marx's own intention in developing this concept
of alienated labour has been thrust aside, and instead
a variety of opposing interpretations :-:ave beer. superim-
posed on the contents of these last six pages. In this
chapter we shall draw attention to the physical and
textual evidence of Marx's own intention, before ':l'Oing
on to offer an interpretation which perhaps accords with
this intention.
It is impossible to give a single consistent inter-
pretation of Marx's concept of alienated labour, for at
this point in the development of his argument, Marx
is developing a concept that is intended not only to
provide a new interpretative fraoe•.;ork for the contents of
political economy, but also to replace certain philo-
sophical notions about the nature of alienation in modern
society. In particular Marx was working to counter Feuer-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
225
bach I s argument that the religious belief in God is the
source of human self-alienation and Hegel's analysis
of the relationship between the thinking subject and the
object of thought as the basic relationship structuring
the interaction between man and his environment.
The influence of Hegel and Feuerbach on Marx's concept
of alienation has been documented elsewhere. Our own
focus will be on the continuities between Marx's con-
cept of alienated labour and the critique of Adam
Smith and other political economists that Marx had al-
ready developed in this same notebook. Thus there will
be many aspects of Marx's concept, with which we will
not deal. Although one distinguishing characteristic of
the outer pages of Marx's notebook is that, unlike
the core-notebook, they contain no excerpts or paraphra-
ses from Adam Smith, our own presentation of Marx's
argument will continue to rely on Adam Smith to illu-
minate in greater historical detail the rather com-
pressed theoretical argument which Marx develops on
these pages. Our reason for doing this is partly to
render Marx I s argument more accessible to the reader,
by making explicit what remains implicit in Marx's
analysis, and partly to show how Adam Smith's own
assemblage of historical evidence serves to confirm
the radically different interpretation which Marx
develops.

10. 1.
The Physical and Textual Evidence of Continui tv
The first five pc.ges of Marx I s outer sheets were
devoted to sketching
the structure of historical
development which led to the division of the product of
labour into three components. Having done this, Marx
now, in the remaining six pages of his outer sheets,
reexamines the fact itself. This is indicated in the
changed format of these last six pages. "Rent of Land 11
no longer occupies the center of the page; instead
"Profit of Capital" does; and the columns are arranged
in the same order as the three-column pages of ~arx I s
core-notebook, which contain Marx's immanent critique
of the Wealth of Nations. This is a clear indication
that what Marx is going to examine on these pages is
the same social phenomenon which Smith examined in
his Wealth of Nations, using as his theoretical frame-
work, the tripa:r-tite division of the exchangeable value
of the cornmodi ty. However, al though the pages are
divided into three columns, each representing a compo-
nent of Smith 1 s commodity, the contents of these columns
belong exclusively to the "Wages of Labour". Thus the
page format represents Smith's analvsis of the ex-
changeable value of the connoditv in which labour forms
only one of three separate components, while the oage
content represents Smith I s labour theory of value,
according to which labour forms the whole of the ex-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
227
changeable value of the commodity.
This contradiction in Smith's analysis of the ex-
changeable value of the commodity has already been
posed in page VII of Marx I s core-notebook, the only
other page in the whole notebook with exactly the same
format as these last six pages. On page VII, let us re-
call, Marx listed the contradictions between the theo-
retical proposition that labour is the sole source of
wealth, and the historical conditions of the labouring
class, reduced to misery and poverty.
After listing the contradictions in Smith's con-
cept of labour which Marx had revealed in the Weal th of
Nations by juxtaposing the theoretical claims of the
workers (implied in Smith's labour theory of value)
and their practical claims (illustrated by Smith's docu-
mentation of the conditions of wage-workers), Marx,
still on page VII, posed two questions:
Let us now rise above the level of political economy
and seek from the foFgoing argument, which was
presented almost in the words of the economists,
answers to two questions -
1 . What is the significance, in the development of
mankind, of this reduction of the greater part
of mankind to abstract labour?
2. What errors are cornmi tted bv the advocates of
piece-meal reform, who eith'er want to raise wages
and thereby improve the condition of the working
class, or {like Proudhon) regard equality
of wages as the aim of the social revolution?
We have already noted that these questions were
not answered in Marx's core-notebook. They are answered
for the first time on pages XXII-XXVII. This is immediate-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
228
ly obvious in the case of the second question -
the
interested reader can turn to pages 132-133 of Botto-
more (pages XXV-XXVI of Marx's original notebook) to
confirm this. Just as Marx clearly postponed the ans-
wer to the second question raised on page VII of his
notebook until the end of his notebook, so
he
postponed the answer to the first question. Our hypo-
thesis then is that Marx I s development of the concept
of alienated labour is intended to answer tr.e first
question raised on page VII, and that in developing the
concept of alienated labour, he followed the agenda out-
lined in his introduction to this question on page VII.
~lamely, that the answer to such a question about the
significance of abstract labour for the development
of mankind can only be found by laying aside the cate-
gories of political economy "Let us now rise above
the level of political economy". At the same time, once
these categories are laid aside, the argumer.t of the
economists will yield the answer.
The page format of the pages on which Marx presents
his concept of alienated labour, thus indicates an
intimate connection with the rest of his notebook. This
connection has been buried in the published versions
of Marx's text, where there is no indication that ~arx's
text was originally written on pages divided into Smith I s
three components of the exchangeable value of the comI!'.o-
di ty. If this fact is mentioned in the editor's preface


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
229
to the text, then it is dismissed as an insignif.icant
datum, one that Marx himself paid no attention to. The
contents of the last six pages, almost always published
as a separate section under the title "Alienated Labour"
are alleged to be on a "different subject", unrelated
to the contents of the rest of the notebook. But Marx
himself did not entitle these last six pages "Alienated
Labour" -
this was a later insertion by the transcri-
bers of Marx's handwritten text. Instead, he entitled
each of these last six pages "Wages of Labour", "Profit
of Capital", and "Rent of Land" • This fact, which has
not been reproduced in the published versions of
Marx's text, has likewise been ignored by the seconda=y
literature. Hence the contents of Marx's last six pages,
the concept of alienated labour, have been interpreted
and discussed in the secondary literature as though they
have nothing to do with the argument he has already
developed in the rest of his notebook, as though they
can be adequately understood without reference to Adam
Smith's Wealth of ~ations. As a result, a variety of
conflicting interpretations has been superimposed on
.'1arx's concept of alienated labour, without much atten-
tion being paid to Marx's own intentions in developi!'l.g
this concept. In cur next sections we will document the
evidence, so far ignored in the secondary literature,
of these intentions. These intentio:1.s are illustrated
by the page
and stated partly as we have seen on


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
230
page VII, partly in Marx's preliminary remarks on page
XXII, and partly on the final pages of his notebook,
where Marx explains what he has just achieved with
his analysis of alienated labour. Certainly it was not
Marx's intention that the contents of the last six
pages should be read without regard to the contents of
the rest of his notebook. In the first paragraph on
page XXII Marx wrote:
We have begun from the presuppositions of
political economy. We have accepted its terminolo-
gy and its laws. we presupposed private property;
the separation of labour, capital and land, as
also of wages, profit and rent; the division of
labour; competition; the concept of exchange value,
etc. From political economy itself, in its own
words, we have shown that the worker sinks to the
level of a commodity, and to a most miserable commo-
dity; that the misery of the worker increases with
the power and volume of his production; that the
:-iecessary result of competition is the accumulation
of capital in a few hands, and thus a restoration
of monopoly in a more terrible form; and finally
that the distinction between capitalist and land-
lord, and between agricultural labourer and industrial
worker, must disappear, and the whole of society
divide into the two classes of property owners and
and property less workers,
---
Political economy begins with the fact of pri-
vate property; it does not explain it {Marx 120).
In this paragraph Marx SUITu.ilarizes his method of
immanent critique and recapitulates the conclusion which
this method has yielded, when he applied it, in the
pages of his core notebook, to the contents of the
Weal th of Nations.
In summary, our argument is that with his concept
of alienation Marx transforms Adam Smith's analysis of


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
231
the commodity as three separate components -
the wages
of labour, the profit of capital, and the rent of land
-
into a dialectical concept. Ti1e cornmodi ty is no
longer viewed as a static structure but as a dynamic
relation between two mutually opposed but necessarily
interdependent poles. One pole is alienated labour, the
other pole is private property. But as soon as we
view each of these poles as a dynamic process, then it
immediately becomes its opposite. Thus the alienation
of labour is at the same time the movement of private
property, and the movement of private property is the
alienation of labour. This "unity of opposites" is,
as we pointed out in chapter IV and chapter VIII,
the achievement of the speculative stage of the dialec-
tical method, a stage that builds upon and incorporates
both the categories of the understanding and the self-
contradictions in these categories that have been re-
vealed in the dialectical stage of negative reasoning.

Summary of Marx I s Argument
Before beginning our interpretation of Marx I s pages
on alienated labour, we present a brief summary of the
structure of the argument which Marx makes on these
pages, a structure which can easily be confirmed by
reading the full text. (Pages XXII-XXVII in Appendix One
or Bottomore, pages 120-134).

1.
Introduction consisting of 4 paragraphs {Marx
120-121): Here Marx summarizes the argument developed

Paragraph 1 (quoted in the last section) summarizes
the method and results of the immanent critique carried
out in the core notebook. Paragraph 2 argues that
because political economy presupposes but does not
examine the basic category of private property, the
social relations (e.g. the separation of land, labour,
and capital, the relationship between wages and pro-

and the war between
the avarice, competition) appear as "external and appa-
rently accidental conditions" and not as "the expression
of a necessary development". Paragraph 3 contrasts this
failure to provide a
historical framework for the under-

mental process with a summary of Marx's own approach
towards understanding private property as a
historical
evolution. (Marx already has outlined this approach
in the first five pages of his outer sheets). Para-

a statement of
Marx's own agenda. Here Marx lists the presuppositions
of political economy, which as he has already noted
(in paragraph 2), appear in political economy as "ex-
ternal and accidental conditions" and insists that there
is a real connexion between them, a connexion which
can be understood in terms of alienation and the money
system. We will pay special attention to this fourth

paragraph, because though the sentence is grammatically

the exchange (or mutual alienation} of labour is the
governing principle of the money-system.
2.
The starting-point for Marx's ot,m explanation ( 2

condition n which served as Adam Smith's starting-point,
and compares it to the same sort of explanations which
theologians draw upon to explain how evil entered the

"a contemporary economic fact". This fact is a summary
of the contradictions in Smith I s labour theory of
value, which Marx has already listed on page VII of
his core notebook. Now, however, they are stated in

the
in contrast to theological explanation of the origins of

Marx's conceptual exploration of the implications
of this fact, which has become the "holy text" of
Marx's theory of the four aspects of alienation (Marx
122-129): If we grant that Marx 1 s starting-point is

valid {and that in turn depends on whether or not we

is a logical development of the inverse relationship which

of his core notebook, (the revenues of Smith 1 s property
owning classes) the four aspects of wage-labour (Smith's
producing class -
the third component of Smith I s commo-
dity} are steps in an overall coherent argument, each
step leading logically to the next.
(1) Alienation of the worker from the product of his
labour.
(2) Alienation of the worker from the labour-process
itself. (The connexion between these first two
aspects are explained by Marx's transitional dis-
cussion of objectification, where he demonstrates

the result of the objectification process}.
(3) Alienation of the worker from his species-specific

class is the fact that its members work; work is
their species-specific activity. Thus if they are
alienated from their w0.:-k, they are alienated from
t..ti.eir species-specific activity. In this section,
Marx treats labour, not merely as a specific
activity, but as a humanly specific activity, cf.
Capital.

4.

It is only at this point that .Marx recognizes the

10.3.
Marx's Introductory Remarks
In his second paragraph on page XXII Marx subjects the
categories of political economy, which he has listed
as his point of departure, to one and the same criticism.
These categories have been introduced into the science


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
of political economy without an adequate explanation
being offered where the phenomena which they identify
236
came from.
When
Adam Srni th, for example,
did offer any sort of explanation about the origins
of ptenomena such as exchange, he simply offered an
ad hoc argument about human nature. What Smith failed
to do, was to show how the economic system itself pro-
duces and reproduces the phenomena which he has catego-
rized as private property, competition, exchange, etc.
At the end of this second paragraph stands a crossed-
out sentence: "We now have to look around for the essence
of the depicted material movement of property". This
sentence announces Marx's own agenda for correcting the
fundamental inadequacy of political economy, namely its
failure to explain private property and consequently its
failure to explain all the phenomer.a which presuppose
that category. But Marx crossed the sentence out, in
order to make another observation about the misguided
efforts of the political economists to analyse social
conditions without first understanding the nature of
private property. This observation, Marx's third para-
graph on page XXII, consists of one long sentence which
refers back to the contents of the first five pages of
Marx I s outer sheets. There Marx had already challenged
classical political economy by offering a radically new
perspective: the roots of the present economic and
social system are to be found in Europe I s feudal past.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
237
This new perspective emphasizes
continuity and inter-
connexions between the institutions of feudalism -· mono-
poly of land, the guild-system, large feudal estates -
and those of capitalism -
competition, a free labour-
market, the splitting up of the large feudal estate into
smaller purchaseable sites.
Just because political economy fails to under-
stand the interconnexion within this movement it
was possible to oppose the doctrine of competition
to that of monopoly, the doctrine of freedom of
crafts to that of the guilds, the doctrine of the
division of landed property to that of the great
estates; for competition, freedom of crafts, and the
division of landed property were conceived only
as accidental consequences brought about by will
and force, rather than as necessary, inevitable and
natural consequences of monopoly, the guild system
and feudal property. {Marx 121 ) .
Having crossed out his earlier agenda-statement at
the end of the second paragraph in order to make room
for another observation on the shortcomings of political
economy, Marx now restates his own agenda, this time in
a more precise way. All the categories which Marx has
mentioned in his second paragraph as phenomena whose
origins are left unexplained by the science of political
economy are repeated in this agenda: "private property,
avarice (Habsucht), the separation of labour, capital
and land, exchange and competition, value and the devalu-
ation of man, monopoly and competition". Marx summarizes
these phenomena with the phrase "this whole alienation"
(dieser ganzen Entfrerndung) • What gives Marx the right
at this stage of his argument to label these categories


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
238
of political economy
"this whole alienation"? The
answer is the criticism that he has already developed
of these same categories in his second paragraph, name-
ly that they appear in political economy without any
systematic or satisfactory explanation of the origins
of the phenomena they describe. The word "alienation"
implies two things about the object or phenomenon which
is being characterized as "alienated": firstly that the
creators of this object, the cause of this phenomenon,
are the same subjects who are now trying to understand,
or in some way "come with", this object; secondly, that
the subjects have forgotten that they were the original
creator of this object. Thus the subjects no longer recog-
nize that the object confronting them is something entire-
ly of their own doing. Instead the subjects treat the
object as though it had its own causes, as though the
object originated and now sustains itself independent
of human agency. Marx's criticism in his second para-
graph was that it was precisely the historical origins
of these phenomena, which political economy had failed
to examine.
Marx's agenda promises "to grasp the real connection"
between all these categories, and to grasp it 11with the
money-system".
Marx I s choice of the money-system as the key to
uncovering the real origins of the other phenomena
described and categorized by political economy is the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
239
testimony of his debt to Adam Smith. For it was precise-
ly in his analysis of the money-system that Smith made
the clearest connextion between human agency and an in-
stitutionalized objective phenomena, which up to Smith's
time had been treated as something entirely independent
of human productive activi.ty. In his chapter "Of the
Origin and use of Money", Smith ti-aces the historical
stages through which money evolved to "become in all
civilized nations the universal instrument of commerce,
by the intervention of which goods of all kinds are
bought and sold, or exchanged for one another" (Smith 28).
In the following chapter Smith proceeds to explain that
what really governs the exchange of goods for one another
is labour. "Labour (is) the real measure of the ex-
changeable value of all commodities" (Smith 31). In other
words, according to Smith, the money-system is not an
independent system, operating according to its own laws;
it is merely an external expression of the movement of
labour. The difficulty of grasping this notion1 that
money is merely an externalized, tangible expression of
labour, is explained by Smith in the following way:
The greater part of people do understand better
what is meant by a quantity of a particular comma-
di ty, than by a guanti ty of labour. The one is a
plain palpable object; the other an abstract notion,
which1 though it can be made sufficiently intelli-
gible, is not altogether so natural and obvious.
(Smith 31-32).
Marx's elaboration of the concept of alienated
labour is an attempt to extend Smith's insight (that

Marx I s Point of Departure: "A Contemporary
Economic fact"
Marx's "contemporary economic fact", which forms the
starting-point and premise for his reinterpretation of
the social reality documented in the categories and
theories of political economy, may be separated analyti-
cally into three steps of conceptualization. First,
Marx makes explicit a distinction which in Smith I s in-
quiry into the nature and causes of the wealth of nations
remains concealed and unacknowledged. This distinction
is between labour as subject and labour as object, or
(and these are terms for the same distinction} living
labour and dead labour. Labour as subject, living labour,
is the productive activity of those who labour (the wor-
king-class}. This activity Smith explicitly identified
in the introduction to his whole work as the sole cause
of the wealth of nations. (Admittedly, in developing his
analysis, Smith did not consistently hold to this pre-
mise. Smith uses the phrase "land and labour" in later
passages, where, had he remained true to his original
premise, he should have said "Labour" only) . Moreover,
since the wealth of nations is an agglomerate of the

the sole source of that

not consistently stick to his labour theory of value) .
Labour as object, dead labour, is labour e.1!1.bodie,.::i in a

the Wealth of Nations, the outcome of these steps bears

menon is an extremely remarkable one, characterizing

that in proportion as the divine subject is in

• • • To enrich God, man

The resemblance between Marx I contemporary fact of
political economy and Feuerbach I s remarkable phenomenon
of religion is no accident. In an article published
in 1844, Marx publicly welcomed Feuerbach' s critical
analysis of religion as an important step towards the
understanding of modern man and society. In this ar-
ticle, entitled "Contribution to the Critique of Hegel's
Philosophy of Right: Introducion" , Marx wrote:
For Germany, the criticism of religion has been
largely completed; and the criticism of religion is
the premise of all criticism •.• Man, who has found
in the fantastic reality of heaven, where he sought
a supernatural being, only his own reflection, will
no longer be tempted to find only the semblance of
himself -- a non-human being -- where he seeks and
must seek his true reality.
The basis of irreligious criticism is this: man
makes religion, religion does not make man. (Marx43) .
These opening paragraphs of Marx's article accurate-
ly summarize Feuerbach I s analysis of religion: namely
that God, the object of religious devotion, is nothing


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
249
more than an illusory figure projected by the religious
subject's own imagination. The powers and attributes
which the religious worshipper endows to God -
father-
liness, omniscience, omnipotence etc. -
are nothing
more than the collective capacities and characteristics
of the human species. However, the religious worshipper
is characterized precisely by the fact that he/ she is
unaware of (or forgets) that God is nothing more than
a creation of the human mind, and instead treats God as
though He were endowed with eternal life and superhuman
powers, as though God were the creator of all, and man
was merely His helpless creation. Thus the attitude of
the religious worshipper corresponds precisely to the
attitude of political economists, which is implicit in
their taken-for-granted categories, towards such social
phenomenon as private property, the division of land,
labour and capital, exchange, etc. Both attitudes are
"alienated" attitudes.
However we should not be misled into thinking that
Marx is going to show that private property, exchange,
etc. are merely figments of the political economists 1
imagination, which have no real basis in the contempora-
ry world. Quite the opposite.
Already in his article
welcoming Feuerbach's discovery of the "truth" of reli-
gion, Marx warned that the abolition of illusions cannot
be taken as the whole truth.

The abolition of religion as the illusory

It is the task of history, therefore, once the
other-world of truth has vanished, to establish
the truth of this world. The immediate task of
philosophy, which is in the service of history,
is to unmask human self-alienation in its secular
form now that it has been unmasked in its sacred

---
"The condition which requires illusions", "the truth
of this world", "human self-alienation in its secular
form" -
all of these, Marx asserts {contrary to Feuer-
bach I s own opinion) , cannot be discovered by an analysis
which restricts itself to the study of the religions
conceptions which human beings have developed for them-
selves. Rather, one must analyze its historical, secular
form.
10.5.
The Four Aspects of Alienated Labour
We have seen how the contradictions revealed in
Smith's account of the Wealth of Nations led Marx to
focus on the category of private property, and to re-
interpret this category not as a fixed thing, perma-
nently established by two irreversible events in the
prehistory of civilized society, but as a historical
development which had its origins in the. appropriation
of land in feudal Europe and which developed its own


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
251
self-sustaining dynamic with the emergence of wage-labour
and capital. Thus, to explain the existence of private
property -
which Smith himself did not take as a problem
but simply asserted -
Marx has to uncover the hidden
processes by which private property reproduces itself.
The contemporary economic fact with which Marx begins
his explanation summarizes the contradictions which Marx
has already uncovered in Smith I s concept of labour,
namely the impoverishment and powerlessness of living
labour, labour as the personal activity of the working-
class, and the wealth 8.nd power of dead labour, of
"labour which has been embodied in an object and turned
into a physical thing'' (Marx 122). From this relation-
ship, the relationship of the laboureIS to the product
of their labour, Marx develops on pages XXII, XXIII,
and XXIV his now famous alienated labour.
In contrast to Adam Smith, who began his analysis
of the production-process by positing the relationship
of man to man (of producing subject to producing sub-
ject) , Ma:rx begins his analysis by documenting the
relationship of the producing subject to the object of
production. He does so, in order to demonstrate how the
intersubjective relation ships among the producers are
the result of their relationship to the objective con-
ditions of production. The first aspect of alienated
labour which Marx analyzes is the relationship of the
producing subject to the object of production, a relation-
ship which is at the same time the relationship to the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
252
objective conditions of production. The second aspect
is the relationship of the producing subject to the pro-
duction process. The fourth and final aspect -
the
conclusion which Marx wants to reach, namely an alter-
native explanation for the asocial pursuit of private
interests -
is the intersubjective relationships
between the producers. The third aspect is the
necessary link in the chain of reasoning which allows
Marx to show how the relationship of subject to object
necessarily determines the relationship of subject to
object.
The four different aspects of alienated labour are
Marx's attempt to offer a radically different explana-
tion of the four characteristics of capital, which he
documented and discussed in Smith I s terninology in the
Profit column of the core notebook (see chapter VII}.
The alienation of the producer from his product is
the "basis of capital". In describing the £ irst aspect
of alienation, the alienation of the producer from the
product of his labour,, Marx writes:
So much does the appropriation of the object
appear (in political economy} as alienation that
the more objects the worker produces the fewer he
can possess and the more he falls under the do-
mination of his product, of capital. {}larx 122).
Compare this with Marx's earlier characterization
of Capitc.l (the first subheading of his profit column)
as "private ownership of the products of other men I s
labour" and as "stored up labour" . .Marx goes on to


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
elaborate the implications of the alienation of the
labourer from his product in terms of the dual role
which nature plays in the labour-process.
The worker can create nothing without nature,
without the sensuous external world. The latter
is the material in which his labour is realized,
in which it is active, out of which and through
which it produces things.
But just as nature affords the means of exis-
tence of labour, in the sense that labour cannot
live without objects upon which it can be exer-
cised, so also it provides the ~eans of existence
in a narrower sense; namely the means of physical
existence for the worker himself. Thus, the more
the worker appropriates the exter:1.al world of
sensuous nature by his labour the more he deprives
himself of means of existence, in two respects ..•
(Marx 123).
These two aspects of the labourers' alienation from
the objective conditions of their production appear in
Smith as the attributes of capital: "a stock of goods
of different kinds --- stored up somewhere sufficient
to maintain { the worker) and to supply him with the
materials and tools of his work" (Smith 259).
The second aspect of alienation which Narx discus-
ses is the alienation of labour "in the process of pro-
duction, within productive activity itself."
How could the worker stand in an alien relation-
ship to the product of his activity if he did not
alienate himself in the act of production itself?
The product is indeed only the resume of activity,
of production. (Marx 124).
And it is precisely in the act of production that
the profits of capital are generated. In his second

subheading in the Profit column, "The Profit of Capital",
Marx summarizes his selection of quotations from Smith

The greater the human contribution to a commo-
dity, the greater is the profit of dead capital.
(Marx 89).
The third aspect of alienation, and the one which
has given rise to the greatest dispute is the alienation
of the human producer from the distinguishing charac-
teristic of the human species, alienation ::"ram "free,
conscious activity". The empirical result of this has
already been documented in the third subheading in the
Profit column: The rule of capital over labour and the
motives of the capitalists, where Marx quotes Smith's
observations that
"The plans and projects of the employers of
stock regulate and direct all the most important
operations of labour, and profit is the end pro-
posed by all those plans and projects. (Marx 90 l • "
Finally the fourth aspect of alienation "man is
alienated from other men" corresponds to the fourth
issue in the Profit column. The Accumulation of Capi-
tals and the Cornpeti tion among Capita lists. Under this
sub-heading Marx quotes the following description of
the social relationships which characterize capitalist
production:
Complete freedom as regards t:le nature, quan-
tity, quality, and occasion of production, and as


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
regards the use and consumption of weal th, the
disposal of the materials of labour. Everyone is
255
free to exchange what he possesses as he chooses,
without regard to anything but his own self-interest.
Competition simply expresses voluntary exchange,
which itself is the logical consequence of the
individual right to use and abuse the instruments of
production. These three economic factors, which
form a unity -- the right to use and abuse, free
exchange, and arbitrary competition -- have the
following consequences: everyone produces what he
will, how, when and where he will, produces well
or ill, too much or not enough, too soon or too
late, too dear or too cheap. (Marx 97).
The fact that Marx identified his four aspects of
alienated labour in the same way as he identified
his four subsections in the Profit column of the
core-notebook (i.e. with arabic numerals) is, we would
argue, no accident. The purpose of Marx's analysis of
alienated labour is, as he himself expressly states,
to demonstrate that "private property is therefore
the product, the necessary result, of alienated labour,
of the external relation of the worker to nature and
himself" (Marx 131) .
Smith's definitions of capital and his analysis of
the laws governing the rate of profit and the accumu-
lation of capital all take for granted that capital
is the private ownership of the means of production.
Marx in turn explicitly rejected "the fact of private
property" as an adequate starting point for understan-
ding the empirical social reality analysed by Smith.

10.5.1. Alienation of the Workers from the Obiects
of their Labour
Marx rephrases this "contemporary economic fact"
256
as a relationship between the producer and the object
of production, a relationship whose distinguishing
characteristic is alienation, so that the object be-
comes an "alien being, a power independent of the
producer". The product is "alien" and independent
because it does not belong to the labourer who produced
it; it is a "power" because it is identical with the
power, the productive activity that produced it. Marx
states:
This fact implies that the object produced by
labour, its product, now stands opposed to it as
an alien being, as a power independent of the
producer. The produce of labour is la:Oour which has
been embodied in an object and turned into a physi-
cal thing; th1.s product is an objectification of
labour, The performance of work is at the same time
its objectification. (Marx 122) •
"Objectification" is a category central to Segel I s
philosophy, but Marx 1 s identification of objectifi-
cation with the performance of work can be directly
traced to Smith's analysis of productive labour. In
his chapter "Of the Accumulation of Capital, or of
Productive and Unproductive Labour" in his second
volume, Smith writes:
the labour of the manufacturer (=productive
labour} fixes and realizes itself in some parti-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
257
cular subject or vendible commodity, which lasts
for some time at least after that labour is past.
It is, as it were, a certain quantity of labour
stocked and stored up to be employed, if necessary
upon some other occasion. That subject, or what is
the same thing, the price of that subject, can
afterwards, if necessary, put into motion a quan-
tity of labour equal to that which had originally
produced it. (Smith 314).
Thus Smith here analyses the product of labour as
"labour which has embodied in an object and turned into
a physical thing". He further recognizes that labour
that has been objectified in this way, and that no
longer exists as the living activity of the labour, but
has been divorced from the activity -
"stocked and
stored up" -
is nevertheless a "power", a power to
"put in motion a quantity of labour", in short, a power
over labour its elf.
Marx's further elaboration of objectification on
page XXIII does not contradict Smith's description of
this phenomenon, but complements it. The point which
Marx here draws attention to, and which is implicit in
Smith I s description, is that the exercise of labour
(which is necessarily identical with the creation of
a product, with the objectification of labour) presuppo-
ses more than the mere productive energy of the worker.
The necessary precondition for the workers to exercise
their creative capacity to produce and therefore an
essential element in the objectification of labour is
the existence of raw materials and instruments of
production, in short the existence of the objective

conditions of production. Marx embraces these objective
