The worker can create nothing without nature,
without the sensuous external world. The la'tteris
the material in which his labour is realized, in
which it is active, out of which and through which
it produces things. (Marx 123) •
Only when these objective conditions exist can
labour exist as an objective activity. Labouring
activity -
no matter how eager the worker may be to
put his labouring powers into practice -
cannot occur
without these objective conditions.
Marx goes on to note that even the subjective
energy of the workers necessarily presupposes such
objective conditions.
But just as nature affords the means of living

so also it provides the means of living in the

t.m.).
Marx here separates
two aspects of the aliena-
tion of the labourer from nature: firstly the aliena-
tion of the worker from the objective conditions of
production, and secondly the alienation of the worker
from the objective conditions of his own subsistence.
These are also the two aspects that Smith identified
as the functions of capital, which he defined as "a
stock of goods of different kinds • • . stored up

1 a. 5. 2. Alienation of the Workers from the Process of
The relationship of the wage-labourer to the pro-
duction process is the second aspect of alienated
labour and follows directly from the first, which as

In discussing his first aspect of alienation, the
alienation of the labourer from the product of labour,
Marx has emphasized that this is an immediate result
of the labourer's own and that it does not presuppose
the intervention of a third party whose act of expro-
priation transforms the product of labour into a pro-
duct alien to the labourer. Rather, the existence of a
product alienated from the labourer presupposes that
the process which gave rise to the existence of this
product was likewise alienated from the labourer:
How could the worker stand in an alien rela-
tionship to the product of his activity if he did
not alienate himself in the act of production itself?
The product is indeed only the r€sume' of activity,
of production .•. The alienation of the object of
labour merely swnmarizes the alienation in the work

This aspect of alienated labour, is, like the first,
confirmed by Smith's account of labour. In his develop-

10.5.3. Alienation of Human Beings from Species Life
Marx's third aspect of alienated labour, alienation
from species-life, builds on the second aspect in just
the same way as the second aspect built on the first
aspect. The totality of the relationship discussed un-
der the second aspect, a relationship which includes the
labourer both at home and at work, covers the worker's
whole life activity. The third aspect of alienated
labour examines the relationship of the workers to their
life activity. On the objective side of the relationship
now stands the whole life of the workers, but how are
we to understand what it is that stands on the subjective
side? In what capacity are workers (or any human beings
for the matter) able to distance themselves from their
whole day-to-day material existence, and develop a
relationship to it as though they themselves were some-
how separate from that material existence? 7he answer
which Marx gives is free will and "consciousness".
Consciousness of course is not an exclusive property of
the working-class; it is the coi.":'lnon characteristic of
human beings.
Consequently, 1'1arx introduces the third aspect of
alienated labour by speaking of "man" in general and
not, as in the first two aspects, of "the workers".
man makes his life activity itself an object of
his will and consciousness ... Conscious life
activity distinguishes man from the life activity


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
266
of animals. Onlv for this reason is he a species-
being. (Marx, 127).
The fact that man is an animal endowed with con-
sciousness therefore makes possible this third relation-
ship: a relationship in which the worker treats his
life-activity as an object, as something other than him-
self. This is a premise which Marx does not here discuss
further, but one that he takes for granted. It is not,
however, a premise that he owes to Feuerbach, and there-
fore it is not a premise that demonstrates any profound
influence of Feuerbach. Rather, this premise can be more
directly traced back to Hegel, if not further back in
the tradition of Enlightenment. Marx's far more impor-
tant point and one that decisively differentiates him
from all his predecessors, is that consciousness,
while it makes it possible for man as a thinking sub-
ject to reflect upon himself as an object, creates only
the possibility of this relationship; it does not, how-
ever, determine the two poles of this relationship,
i.e. man as subject and man's life activity. What de-
termines the objective life activity of mankind, and
what determines the subject reflecting upon this life
activity is the productive process. Most of Marx's
discussion of the third aspect of alienated labour,
alienation from species-being, is devoted to demonstra-
ting that species-life can have no other meaning than
productive life. Thus while Marx agrees with a long


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.

philosophical tradition that preceded him in insisting
that "free, conscious activity is the species-character

activity, is not only this conscious activity but more
fundamentally the interaction between men and their
physical environment.
But what is exclusive to the working-class is pro-
ductive activity. This is an activity in which neither
of the other two classes participate. Their relationship
to the production process, to the necessary activities
for the reproduction of the species, is simply one of
"lending" the means of production, which they own.
The beginning of labour2) as productive life is the
experience of a need that requires to be satisfied. In

They construct nests, dwellings, as in the case
of bees, beavers, ants, etc .. But they only produce
what is strictly necessary for themselveE: or their

of direct physical needs. (Marx, 128).

According to Smith, the decisions of what to produce 1 and
how much, originate in the social needs that are expressed
in the market-place. It is the experience of the market-
demand that influences the use to which the productive

The whole quantity of industry annually employed
in order to bring any commodity to market, naturally
suits itself in this :manner to the effectual demand.
It naturally aims at bringing always that precise
quantity thither which may be sufficient to supply,
and no rr.ore than supply, demand. (p. 58).
But what is the relationship between market-demand
and the needs of the society as a whole, the greater part
of which is the "labouring poor"? Smith himself gives the
answer: there is no relationship!
Smith's use of the word "effectual" is no accident,
for he goes on to explain how it differs from "absolute"
demand, which he illustrates with the following anecdote:
A very poor man may be said in some sense to
have a demand for a coach and six; he might like to
have it; but this demand is not an effectual demand,
as the cornmodi ty can never be brought to market in
order to satisfy it. (p. 56).
"Effectual" demand is "the demand of those who are
willing to pay" (p. 56); the only needs that Sr.r.ith's
system of cor.unodi ty-production can satisfy, are those
backed by money. There is nothing in the market mechanism
that ties production to the sa_tisfaction of the hunan
needs of the majority of the society 1 the human !'l.eeds of
the labourers themselves.

All that the market mechanism does is to confirm what

Every process of human production starts not with

We are now in a position to understand Marx's fun-

of the goal of production, the decision of what to pro-

h u m a n

While, therefore, alienated labour takes away the

forms free and self-directed activity into a means, so
it transforms the species-life of man into a means of
physical existence. (Marx 128).
This point is repeated in Marx I s analysis of the
labour process in Chapter VII of Capital I, where he
presents the vi v1d comparison of the spider and the
weaver, of the bee and the architect:
We pre-suppose labour in a form that stamps it
as exclusively human. A spider conducts operations

shame many an architect in the construction of her
cells. But what distinguishes the worst architect
from the best of bees is this, that the architect
raises his structure in imagination before he erects

He not only effects a change of form in the material
on which he works, but he also realises a purpose
of his own that gives the law to his modus operan-
di ... (Capital I (1967), p. 178).
10. 5. 4. The Alienation of Human Beings from One Another
Marx's fourth aspect. of alienated labour, the alie-
nation of human beings from one another, is a direct
consequence of the analysis that Marx has so far carried
out. The discussion that Marx devotes to this point is

simply a restatement of the development of his argument:
(4) A direct consequence of the alienation of

activity and from his species-life, is that man
is alienated from other men. When man confronts
himself he also confrontSOther men. What is true
of man's relationship to his work, to the product
of his work and to himself, is also true of his
relationship to other men, to their labour and to
the objects of their labour.
In general, the statement that man is alienated
from his species-life means that each man is alie-
nated from others, and that each of the others is
likewise alienated from human life. (Marx 129).
With this last aspect of alienated labour, we come
to the premise that served as Adam Smith's starting-point
for his labour theory of value, namely the confrontation
of private individuals dependent on the produce of
others to satisfy their essential needs. As tlarx had al-
ready noted in his excerpt notes on Mill:
Economic

In his excerpt notes on Mill, Marx proceeds to

of the economic system and as the starting-point of

275
from the analysis of the worker who has no control over

starts with the relationship between subject and object,
i.e. between the labourer and his product, and shows
how this relationship necessarily structures the inter-
action between subject and subject. He thereby shows
not only that the contemporary organization of society
cannot be taken as that which guarantees the natural
liberty of man, but also that the social interaction
between individuals cannot be understood without first
examining the material conditions of production.
10. 6.
Private Property as the Necessary Result of
Alienated Labour
Marx's preliminary remarks would lead us to anti-
cipate that he is going to provide the explanation that
is missing from political economy I namely an explana-
tion of the real nature of private property. Yet when
Marx announces the beginning of his own agenda and goes
on to develop this agenda as four aspects of alienated
labour the word "private property" never appears. Why
not? Because what Marx wants to demonstrate is that an
accurate analysis of the nature of labour in the society

276
described by Adam Smith -
labour which produces commo-
dities and which is itself a cornr..odi ty -
reveals all
the characteristics that the political economists attri-
buted to private property. That this was Marx 1 s deliberate
strategy -
namely to explain the manifestation of pri-
vate property which the political econor.tists had docu-
mented, without resorting to even a mention of private
property -
becomes clear from ~arx' s discussion of the
relationship between alienated labour and privat pro-
perty. The discussion begins on page XXV after Marx has
developed all four aspects of alienated labour. Here
Marx raises the question: "Let us now examine further
how this concept of alienated labour must express and
reveal itself in reality". In answer, he carefully
retraces step by step all the necessary ir:i.plications,
which logically follow from the alienated relationship
of the workers to the objects of their production, and
triumphar.tly concludes:
Private property, is therefore, the product, the
necessary result, of alienated labour. (!1-larx 131 -
emphasis Marx') .
Yet the concept and four aspects of alienated labour,
"the alienation of the worker in his objects", involve
no departure from the basic analysis of contemporary
social and economic reality, which Adam Smith and his
followers have already made. What is new in Marx's
perspective is that he does not treat the laws governing

this soc1al reality as having their origins in the fact
of private property, in the accumulation of capital, but
rather in the very nature of labour itself. The fact
that the products of labour are predestined in this
social reality to become the assets of the capitalist
class, and not the direct means of satisfying the pro-
ducers themselves, is to be explained, according to
Marx, not by the relationship of the capitalists to
the production process, namely their private ownership
of the means of production, but by the relationship of
the workers themselves. This latter relationship, one
which remains unexamined in political economy, Marx
has shown in the course of his First Manuscript to be
a
historically specific phenomenon, one that does not
require the ideological or philosophical presupposition
that private property is an inherent and eternal feature
of the human condition.
1 0. 7.
Alienated Labour, Abstract Labour, and
Philosophy
Given that a great deal of emphasis has been laid
on the continuity between Hegel I s philosophical dis-
cussion of alienated labour in the Phenomenology of Mind
(p. 228-240) and Marx's theory of alienation, we will
briefly examine Hegel's account of alienated labour in
his chapter on "Lordship and Bondage" and his di.scussion


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
278
of political economy in the Philosophv of Right in order
to compare Hegel 1 s point of view with the positions of
Smith and Marx.
For Hegel, the real course of human development is
to be found, not in the increase and improvement of the
productive powers of human labour, but in the evo-
lution of human consciousness from a naive unreflective
faith in the evidence of the five senses to the self-
reflective, self-conscious construction of the human
world (including its physical and social environment)
through reasoningi i.e. from a simple trust in the ex-
ternal appearances of the world to the confident know-
ledge that its inner laws and structure are a creation
of the human mind. Thus the first chapter of Hegel I s
Phenomenology is "Certainty at the Level of Sense-Ex-
perience" and the last is "Absolute Knowledge". The
intermediary stages include perception, understanding,
self-consciousness, reason, culture, morality and
religion.
Hegel's account of the alienated labour performed
by the bondsman in the service of his master is an
analysis of the social context that gave rise to Stoi-
cism. Stoicism was a philosophy of inner consolation for
the loss of external freedom that became popular when
Greece fell victim to the Roman empire. Hegel analyses
the desire of both the master and the bondsman that
their independence be recognized by the other, as the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
279
first (both logical and historical) stage in the evo-
lution of self-consciousness.
There are several similarities between Hegel's
discussion of the master/bondsman relationship and Marx I s
analysis of the situation of the wage-labourer, ':::iut the
choice of their respective social contexts constitutes
a fundamental difference.
The master-bondsman relationship is a unity, just
as labour and capital together form the totality of
the production process.
"Action from one side only would be useless,
because what is to happen can only be brought about
by means of both" (Hegel 230).
Both are engaged in a "life-and death struggle"
(p. 232) and the advantage lies with the master:
Since he is the power dominating existence
the master holds, par consequence, the other in
subordination (p. 235).
The bondsman "merely works"; the master "gets the
enjoyment" (p. 235). But the crucial difference between
Hegel's and Marx's accounts of alienated labour is the
indifferer.t attitude of the master to the labour-pro-
cess. This is precisely what, in Marx's analysis of the
developr.tent of private property, distinguishes the feu-
dal ownership of the serfs' means of production from the
capitalist control of the production-process. Just as
the feudal lords restricted their interest in the pro-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
duce of labour to the simple act of consuming it, so
too "The master enjoys it without qualification and
without reserve . • . the bondsman • • . labours upon it
in other words, labour shapes and fashions the thing"
(Hegel, p. 238}. Thus in Hegel 1 s account of alienated
labour it is the subjective idea of the bondsman that
280
is given an objective realization in the transformation
of the environment, and not the will of the master. What
the bondsman sees in the external world, after his many
hours, days,and years of toil in the service of his
master, carrying out his master I s desires, is a world
shaped in accordance with the bondsman's own design and
populated with the products of the bondsman's own
creation.
Thus precisely in labour where there seemed to
be merely some outsider's mind and ideas involved,
the bondsman becomes aware, through this re-dis-
covery of himself by himself, of having and being
a "mind of his own". (Hegel, 239).
We have already seen that the crux of Marx 1 s cri-
tique of the capitalist mode of production, and the
self-destructive potential of alienated labour, is
precisely the fact that it is the capitalists' mind and
ideas that control and organize the labour-process and
substitute as the goal of this process the pursuit of
profj_ t
(money-making) instead of the satisfaction of
human needs. Not only does the outer environment fail
to echo the ideas and the will of the labourers, but
the inner being of the labourers, their own work-acti-

For Marx, alienated wage labour and abstract labour
are synonymous: for Hegel they are not. Nevertheless
Hegel I s discussion of abstract labour in his Philosophy
of Right takes as its context precisely the social
system that Marx analysed in his 1844 notebook, the
system of production and needs, described by Smith, Say
and Ricardo, also known as "civil society".9) Hegel
characterizes political economy as "one of the sciences
which have arisen out of the conditions of the modern
world".
Its development affords the interesting spec-
tacle (as in Smith, Say, and Ricardo) of thought
working upon the endless mass of details which con-
front it at the outset and extracting therefrom the
simple principles of the thing (Hegel 127, para. 189).
"The simple principles" that Hegel refor.nulates as
the concept of abstract labour are Smith's principles
of the division of labour and the interdependence of
needs.
Smith's argument was straight,_forward and simple:
in a society based on the division of labour, every


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
283
individual is engaged in pursuing a single specialized
line of production, the results of which can satisfy
only one of her/his many needs for survival. Conse-
quently every individual is dependent on every other
member
of society for the all-round satisfaction of
her/his needs: Observe the accommodation of the most
common artificer or day-labourer in a civilized and thri-
ving country, and you will perceive that the number of
people of whose industry a part, though but a small
part, has been in procuring him this accommodation,
exceeds all computation ... without the assistance and
co-operation of many thousands, the very meanest person
in a civilized country could not be provided ... the
easy and simple manner in which he is com.r.:1only acco:m..ao-
dated. (Smith, 11-12).
Hegel characterizes this as the interdependence of
needs and
argues that the interdependence of needs and
the division of labour is the result of "the process
of abstraction" {going back to the Latin root of
"abstraho" which means "draw apart"):
the abstracting process ... effects the subdivision
of needs and means and thereby eo inso subdivides
producticn and brings about the~on of labour.
By this division, the work of the individual becomes
less complex, and consequently his skill at his
section of the job increases, like his output.
At the same time, this abstraction of one man's
skill and means of production from another's comple-
tes and males necessary every-where the dependence
of men on one another and their reciprocal relation
in the satisfaction of their other needs. Further,
the abstraction of one man's production from


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
284
another's makes work more and more mechanical,
until finally man is able to step aside and install
machines in his place. {Hegel 129) 10)
It is no accident that Hegel chose the word "ab-
straction", a word that also carries the meaning of
theoretical activity or mental production, to replace
the Smith's worC.s "division" and "subdivision". For it
was Hegel's intention to demonstrate that human needs,
which are typically regarded in philosophy as the par-
ticularized non-communicable, and non-generalizable ex-
periences of each individual (in contrast to human
reason) 11 ) at a certain stage in the C.evelopraent of
humankind, exhibit a "show of ratior.ality" (?.egel 126).
In other words, that it was possible1 and not only possible,
but a necessary development, for human needs, the most
particularized and sensuous ::iode of hunan experience,
the
to slough off cloak of particularity and sensuot:.sness,
and to be revealed as an irmnature stage in the growth
of human reason, a stage that once passed (superseded),
reveals the ser.suous needs of humankind to be an early
manifestation of ti:e hu."Tian mind.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
285
Thus Hegel, like Marx, treated political economy as
a science that studies human behaviour and social history
at the Leve 1 of Understanding. But Hege 1 located the
fundamental flaw in the starting-point of political
economy {which views human needs and their satisfaction
through the activity of labour} as merely "subject.i.ve
particularity" (Hegel, p. 126). Hegel asserts that this
premise prevents political economy from u...,C.erstanding
the real course of human history. For Hegel the hidden
teleological principle that unites these subjective
particularities is not the invisible hand of the market
place, but the inner develop~ent of human reason. Be-
hind the many particular, subjective needs of private
individuals is "the universal and objective element in
work ••• the abstracting process" (Hegel 129; para. 198).
Thus when Marx asks the question
11what is the sig-
nificance of this reduction of the majority of mankind
to abstract labour?" he is using the term that Hegel
introduced to characterize labour within a social
system based on the division of labour and the inter-
dependence of needs. But in contrast to Hegel, Marx
does not view the historical development of Western
philosophy as a movement of consciousness independent
of, and above, this social system. Rather, as Smith
himself observed, the development of philosophy is de-
pendent on, and a manifestation of, the progress of
the division of labour:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
286
In the progress of society, philosophy or spe-
culation becomes, like every other employment, the
principal or sole trade and occupation of a parti-
cular class of citizens. Like every other employment
too, it is subdivided into a great number of diffe-
rent branches, each of which affords occupation to
a peculiar tribe or class of philosophers; and this
subdivision of employment of philosophy, as well
as in every other business, improves dexterity and
saves t.i..-ne • Each individual becomes more expert in
his own peculiar branch, more work is done upon the
whole, and the quantity of science is considerably
increased by it. (Smith 10).
Consequently there is little difference between the
mental genius of the philosopher and the manual drudgery
of the "street porter"; both are "the effects of the
division of labour".
The difference of natural talents in different
men is, in reality, much less than we are aware of;
and ... is not upon many occasions so :r.iuch the cause
as the effect of the division of labour.
The difference between the most dissimilar cha-
racters, between a philosopher and a common street
porter, for example, seems to arise not so much from
nature, as f:::-om habit, custom, and education. When
they came into the world, and for the first six or
eight years of their existence, they were, perhaps,
very much alike, and neither their parents nor play-
fellows could perceive any remarkable difference.
About that age, or soon after, they come to be
employed in very different occupations. The diffe-
rence of talent comes then to be taken :-iotice of,
and widens by degrees, till at last the vanity of
the philosopher is willing to acknowledge scarce
any resemblance. (Smith 15-16).
What distinguishes the specialized occupation of
philosophers from other trades is its self-reflective
activity. In other words, philosophy is an attempt by
thought to understand itself, to discover the structure
of thought and the rules of thinking. For the weaver
who produces cloth, all that is required of her is the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
287
mechanical practice of weaving; but for the philosopher
who wants to produce a system of philosophy, it is
essential at every step to define her/his terms, make
explicit the logic that connects them, and demonstrate
that these terms and logic correspond to the structure
of knowledge itself. This at last was the task that
Hegel set himself.
In discovering the laws of the development of Western
philosophy, Hegel believed. that he had discovered the
evolution of the human mind and the real course of human
history. What he had really discovered, in Marx's ana-
lysis, was the developmental process of the expansion
of the productive powers of labour, as it has histori-
cally occurred under the rule of capital. The philo-
sopher reflecting on his activity of philosophizing and
on the historical development of his trade, is simply
one among many specialized activities as his subject of
study. Since all these specialized activities, philo-
sophy included, are the effects of the division of
labour, what the philosopher discovers in tracing the
development of his own profession, is the developmental
structure common to all branches of productive activity,
whose expansion and development is organized on the
basis of the division of labour.
This Hegel failed to see, and consequently fell into
the trap of believing that the present world, clearly
a human-created product and no longer the garden of Eden


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
that nature bestowed on humankind at the beginning of
evolution, was solely a creation of the specialized
activity of philosophers, the activity of thought. He
clai.med correctly (at least in Marx's eyes):
Wherever there is movement, wherever there is
life, wherever anything is carried into effect in
the actual world, there Dialectic is at work.
(Hegel, Logic, (trans. Wm. Wallace), p. 81).
288
But he confined the creative powers of the dialectic
to the development of the human mind, mental activity
divorced from its material basis and social context in
the 1.abour-process.

CHAPTER XI
Conclusion:
Towards a New Understanding of
Manuscripts II, III, and IV.
294
The physical features of Marx's First Manuscript led
us to undertake a new interpretation of Marx's concept
of alienated labour and to draw attention to the influ-
ence of Adam Smith on Marx's thought processes as he
was penning that manuscript. In this chapter we would
like to draw attention to the physical features of the
other three manuscripts and to indicate how they may
be explored to understand the interrelations of all
four manuscripts.
11.l
The Second Manuscript~ A Missing Notebook?
It has been universally assumed that the two sheets
of paper (i.e. four sides of written text}, known as
the Second Manuscript, constitute the last pages of
a missing notebook which would have formed the link bet-
ween the two surviving notebooks known as the first and
the third manuscripts. The deterioration of the corners
of these four written sides makes it impossible for us
toC.ay to read the four page numbers that Marx gave to


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
the two sheets. The one figu!."e that is still legible
appears on the third of the four pages I according to
295
the arrangement of the written text given by the editors
of the MEGA edition. This figure is a lonely "I", but
according to the MEGA editors' reconstruction of the
page numbers given by Marx to the four sides of these
two written sheets of paper, it must be part of a much
larger number, namely XLII. For according to the MEGA
editors' hypothesis, the numbers given ':Jy Marx to these
four pages were a consecutive sequence beginning with
XL and ending with XLIII. However, since the only un-
ambiguously decipherable
numeral that is still visible
is the isolated "I", there would have to have been a
very large gap in Marx's original page numbeIS between
this still visible "I" and the missing "XLI" that,
according to the MEGA editors' numbering, allegedly
preceded it. Such a gap would be most unusual to find
in Marx's notebooks, where the Roman figures, indica-
ting the number of the page, are grouped ·:ery closely
together ar.d frequently the whole number is j oir:ed up
together with a line across the top and another across
the bottom. Hence I am a little suspicious about wi":at
precisely
the editors of the MEGA edition were able
to see in 1932. From the evidence of the still visible
page numbering of the second manuscript, namely the
lonely "I" that can still be read in the least-worn
page corr.er, it is just as valid to infer that these
two sheets constitute the first four pages of an


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
296
intended notebook as it is to infer that they consti-
tute the last four of a missing notebook.
In point of fact, the page on which we can read
the number I begins with a complete sentence and is
therefore grammatically independent of the page that
preceded 1 t. Ad.mi ttedly however, there does seem to be
a continuity in the written text between this page,
which the MEGA editors numbered XLII, and the page
that they transcribed as the preceding page XLI. The
evidence of the written text is not unambiguous and
it should be noted that the written lines at the top of
the pages transcribed by the MEGA editors as XL and XL!
(i.e. the pages transcribed as the first pages of
this 4-page fragment) have also suffered deterioriation
and evidently contain more words than what the MEGA
editors have transcribed. In summary, the evidence
of the written contents of these four pages, together
with the traces that still remain of the original
page numbers. is inadequate to infer that these two
sheets of pater written on both sides were the last
pages of a now-missing notebook.
Moreover, even if these numbers were still legible,
that would still not be adequate evidence to infer
that the pages belong to a missing notebook.
Let us recall the account in Chapter II of how
Marx compiled the still-surviving notebooks in the EPM
group, namely the First and the Third Manuscripts. our


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
297
main point then
was that Marx did not write in note-
books, but on separate double-folios that were later
sewn together to form notebooks. Hence, we can immedia-
tely see that there is nothing about the survival of a
few separate sheets that leads us to infer the prior
existence of a whole missing notebook. They could just
as easily be alternative ver-sions of pages that Marx
subsequently redrafted, having made the decision that
the argument developed in the four pages of the Second
Manuscript would introduce too much of a diversion in
the overall argument that he wanted to make.
In redrafting his substituted pages, Marx would have
given them the same numbers as the originally-penned
folios which he had decided to discard as no longer
being appropriate for this stage in the overall argument
that he wanted to make. r-t.arx would then have sewn the
redrafted pages into the appropriate place of the note-
book he had been preparing and left the originally-
drafted pages loose. Given the fact that only half of
most of the double-folios in Marx's third manuscript
contain a written text, we could further hypothesize
that the two separate written half-folios, which consti-
tute Marx's Second Manuscript of 1844, were likewise
originally the halves of double folios whose other halves
were blank. In this case, it is highly likely that Marx
would have torn off the blank halves, to be used as
scratch paper, and inserted the two loose sheets into


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
298
the notebook that he finally compiled, as a reminder of
additional points that still needed to be worked out.
This hypothesis of mine finds some support in the
fact that the fourth page (according to the MEGA editors'
arrangement of the written text of these two sheets, i.e.
the page that has been given the roman number XLIII)
concludes with an abbreviated, diagrarrmatic outline of
the complementary unity of labour and capital and the
successive stages through which the rr.utual opposition
between labour and capital becomes ever rr,or-e manifest
with the historical development of the capitalist mode
of production (see Marx (Bottomore 1964} 144) .It is
clear from this outline that there is a weal th of
material to which this analysis of the development of
capital and labour could be applied and there are other
indications on these four pages suggesting that this is
what Marx intended to do. One such indication is a long
parenthetical list of authors to be examined for the
assertion that capital's greatest contribution to
civilization was "to have discovered and created human
l-3.bour as the source of Keal th" (Bottomore 143), whereas
feudal civilization had treated land ( "lifeless and worth-
less matter" ibid.) as the sole source of wealth. ,_nether
indication is the parenthesis in the following sentence.
"From the real course of development (to be inserted
here) there follows the r.ecessary victory of the capi-
talist ... over ... the lar.downer." Now, it is clear


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
299
from the context that whatever Marx intended "to be in-
serted here", it would not have been a mere sentence,
paragraph or page. In order to insert "the real course
of development" - in other words, Marx's radically new
perspective on the development of capitalist society
out of its feudal past -
Marx would have neede:1at least
a whole notebook, if not several. Hence, it is not sur-
prising that Marx decided temporarily to abandon this
line of thought and set aside these pages fer future
work, rather than include them in the project that he
wanted to complete first. This project was to apply the
concept of alienated labour, which he had just developed
in his first notebook as a result of his study of poli-
tical economy, to assessing the strengths and weaknesses
of Hegel's concept of alienation and the validity of the
dialectical method which was promised on this concept.
The sketch that appears in the Second Manuscript, out-
lining a new approach for understanding the historical
development of capitalism, is based on the assumption
that Hegel's dialectical method is a valid way of ana-
lysing the development of the productive forces and
social relations of capitalist society. It does not
make sense to undertake this sort of analysis if Hegel's
dialectical method is itself invalid. 'I'his analysis must
therefore be postponed until Marx has carefully circum-
scribed the flaws - or at least, the inadequacies - in
Hegel's concept of alienation ar.d r:iade the judgment
whether or not these flaws invalidate the dialectical


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
method. This logically prior task is carried out in
Marx's notebook knO"wn as the Third Manuscript.
300
I do not, however, want to argue at this point that
the evidence unambiguously supports my hypothesis in
all its detailed elaboration. Rather my intention here
is to make it quite clear to the reader that the evi-
dence on which the MEGA hypothesized a missing notebook
is far too scanty for us to assume that this is the 2£.!.Y
plausible hypothesis which can be constructed on the
basis of the available evidence. Given the fact that
Marx compiled his notebooks ~
he had numbered and
written his text on separate, unattached sheets of paper,
there are all sorts of other plausible interpretations
about the two sheets of paper known as the Second Manu-
script that do not require us to hypothesize a lost note-
book. In short, there is nothing about the present
existence of two sheets of paper, written on both sides
and known as the Second Manuscript, that allows us to
infer a whole missing notebook.
11. 2
Can the Notes at the Beginning of the Third Manu-
script be Interpreted as Cross-References to Pages
in the Sarne Manuscript?
My assertion that there is no missing notebook also
contradicts the conventional interpretation about the
first pages of the Thir.d Manuscript. The opening pages
of the Third Manuscript contain a written text that is

introduced by the following sequence of notes: . "~ page

troduced by the notes, "ad page XXXVI'; "~ page }:XXIX"
and ".!9, ~" on the first pages of the Third Manu-

that same
~
and not to any lost notebook.
This hypothesis of mine requires a prior hypothesis:
that the first - the outermost - pages of the Third
Manuscript are not the first pages that ~:arx hir:1self
wrote~ For they must have been written after pages
XXXVI-XXXIX (which appear on the two centre-!!1.ost folios)
had been written. This prior hypothesis is by no means
an improbable reconstruction of r.!arx' s writing process,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
302
for as we have already seen in the case of the First
Manuscript, .Marx's Notebook I, there is irrefutable

theless there is a difference between the First and the

the notebook, whereas the page numbered I in the Third

maintain that the center folios of the Third Manuscript
were written before the outermost folios, then we also
have to explain why :Marx should have given these later-

stead of beginning with page number I on the first-written
center folios. In other words, in the case of the Third
:Manuscript, I need to argue that there is nothing about
the sequence of the pages in the resulting notebook
(the Third Manuscript) which allows us to assume that
the lowest-numbered pages were the first pages to be
written on. Certainly we can assume that in most cases,
the sequence of the page numbers indicates the sequence
in which Marx both wrote them and intended them to be
read. But in this particular case, where the written
text on the pages numbered I and upwards was intended
to be inserted in an entirely different place in Marx's
work, we cannot even make this assumption. My own hypo-
thesis is that the first double-folios that Marx wrote


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
303
on, to be compiled later into pages of his Third rl:anu-
script, were not the outer double-folios, even though
these contain the pages with the lowest page numbers,
but the center folios beginning with page XXIIL On page
XXIII of his Third Manuscript, Marx praises Hegel's
Phenomenology of Mind as being the first work that sys ..
tematically develops the concept of alienation ar..d
"therefore grasps the nature of ~-' ar.d conceives
objective man ••• as the result of his cwn labour"
(Bottomore 1964, 202). On this same page Marx goes on to
to say "Hegel's standpoint is that of modern political
ecor..omy". (Bottomcre 1964, 203). On the corresponding
page of his First Notebook (page XXIII). Marx exami~es
"the phenomenon of objectification" {Bottomore 1964, 123).
In this examination of "the phenomenon of objectification",
Marx builds into his own concept of alienated labour,
which he has so far derived and established solely on
the evidence provided by political economists
themselves,
the structure of Hegel's analysis of "objectification".
For in Hegel •s analysis, all objectification is neces-
sarily alienation. By doing this, t-~arx creates a tool,
the concept of alienation, which allows him not only to
analyse the real hidden objective forces propelling the
capitalist process of production, but also to reveal the
fallacies in Hegel's unqualified equation "all objectifi-
cation = alier.ation" (cf. Bottomore 1964, 201). For ?.egel 1 s
mistake is fundamentally the same as that of the political


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
304
economists, who operated on an analogous equation, namely
"all production = commodity-production".
What I am suggesting therefore, is that Marx, having
arrived at his own concept of alienated labour through
a study of political economy (page XXII of his First
Manuscript) proceeded to develop this concept into two
simultaneous critiques. On the sheets that were later to
form the remaining pages of his First :-!ar..uscript, he
systematically developed this concept into a comprehen-
sive critique of the social reality documented by poli-
tical economy. On other sheets to which he gave the same
numbers (corresponding to the last pages of the First
Manuscript), he wrote down the beginnings of his critique
of Hegel's analysis of alienation. The latter critique
was later elaborated into the full critique of Hegel's
dialectic and philosophy in general that now stands i!'l
the Third Manuscript in three differer.t places (pages
XI-XIII, pages XVII-XVIII, and pages XXIII-:XXXIV) •
Marx's editorial notes at the beginning and end of
the two-page middle section (on pages XVII and XVIII)
refer the reader backwards to page XIII, the last page
of the first section, and forwards to the first page
of the last section. The page number in the latter note
is given as XXII ("See continuation, p. XXII") a number
which Marx omitted (along with XXV) in the numbered
sequence of the written sides of his double-folios that
were late::- compiled into the Third Manuscript. This


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
305
does not, however, mean that the first page of the third
and last part of Narx' s critique of Hegel in the ~
has
been lost, for at the top of page XXIII, beside the page
number (which appears to have been written over a diffe-
rent {but no longer decipherable) number, stands a
corresponding note, referring back to the last page
of the middle section: "See p. XVIII".
What all of these details add up to is this: Marx's
critique of Hegel, that now appears in the published
versions of the ~
as the last chapter of the Third
Manuscript. was written up on three different occasions.
For if Marx had written it all at once, he would have
given the loose double folios, that he was working with,
one and the same continuous sequence of numbers~ More-
over Marx not only wrote this critique in three separate
parts with three different page-number sequences, but
he also inserted these double folios into three diffe-
rent places, in accordance with their page numbering,
when he came to compile his notebook. The two editorial
notes in the two-page middle section of the critique
(page XVII and XVIII) , which, as is clear from their
position in Marx's original script, were written at
the same time as the text to the critique itself,
strongly suggest that this section was written last, in
order to connect the other two already-written sections.
Thus the evidence of Marx's three-part critique of
Hegel in his Third Manuscript, together with the corres-
pondence in theme between the last pages of the First


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
306
Manuscript from XXIII onwards and the pages with these
same numbers in the Third Manuscript, leads me to hypo-
thesize not only:
(1) That Marx wrote the pages of his Third Manuscript
in an order different from that indicated by the num-
bered sequence and that the page · numbered I was not in
fact the first page that Marx wrote on, but also:
(2) Of all the double-folios that Marx later compiled
to form his Third Manuscript, the first to be written
on were those that now stand as the centerrnost folios,
containing pages XXIII-XXIV and XXVI-XXXVI (there is no
page number XXV) , in other words, those containing the
third and last part of Marx's critique of Hegel's dia-
lectic and philosophy in general. The strength of this
hypothesis will have to be judged by a systematic com-
parison between the contents of pages XXIII-XXVII in
the First Manuscript and the pages with the corres-
ponding numbers in the Third Manuscript.
As long as the first, and at present much stronger,
hypothesis holds, then there is no need for us to
assume that the editorial notes that Marx inserted on
pages I and III of his Third ~anuscript refer back to
the last pages of a missing notebook {of which the Second
Manuscript is the sole surviving fragment), but rather
that they are cross-references to the corresponding
pages of the same notebook, i.e. to pages XXXVI and
XXXIX of the Third Manuscript.

First let us note that it is not at all unusual for

Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.

an author to postpone writing the preface to his work
until the work itself is almost complete, for the goal
of the preface is to give the readers some idea (the

on the pages that follow. Nor is there any standard
"preface-length" -
Marx had just as much right to com-
pose a 5-page preface as a 2-page preface. What is un-
usual and highly misleading on the part of any author,
is to give a title, Preface, to the last five pages
of his manuscript and to intend it to refer to only the
first two of those last five pages - without giving any
indication whatsoever, that the last three pages are to

texts (except Landshut), was invented by the editors of

how he wanted to reorganise his work for others to read

of Marx's original manuscript - all of which points un-
ambiguously to the conclusion that all five of the last
numbered and written pages of Marx's Third Manuscript,
pages XXXIX-XLIII, - and not merely the first two of


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
310
these five pages, XXXIX-XL - belong to Marx's Preface.
What then of the written content of these pages? is
there a clear discontinuity between the contents of
pages XXXIX-XL and the contents of pages XLI-XLIII? If
we take into account the first paragraph of page XLI,
a paragraph that is totally ignored in the later-added
title "Money", then the answer must be no!
On page XXXIX, Marx begins his Preface by referring
to his promise in a recently-published article; "Contri-
bution. to the Critique of Hegel's Philosophy of Right:
Introduction", to carry out - as the very title of this
article suggests -
a complete and thorough critique of
Hegel 's political philosophy, as it is presented in
Hegel •s Rechtsphilosophie (=Philosophy of Right). Marx
goes on to explain that his readers will not find his
intended critique in the present work, which deals with
political topics such as "the state, law, morals, civil
life, etc .... only to the extent that political economy
its elf expressly deals with these subjects." {Bottomore
63) He claims that his own work is "based upon a careful
critical study of political economy" and goes on to
mention by name the most important Gennan socialist
writings on this topic. These writings, like all other
similar critiques of contemporary German society, owe
their inspiration ..,. or so Marx asserts - to Feuerbach' s
writings, "the only writings since Hegel's Phenomenology
and Logi.c which contain a real theoretical revolution".


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
311
(Bottomore 64} Feuerbach himself was fully aware that his
own theoretical contribution -
"the new philosophy" as
he characterized it in contrast to the Hegelian or "old"
philosophy - took as its point of departure Hegel's
theoretical contribution. In his Principles of the Phi-
losophy of the Future, Feuerbach stated: "The historical
necessity and justification of the new philosophy must
therefore be derived mainly from a critique of Hegel's"
and again, "The new philosophy is the realization of
the Hegelian philosophy" (Feuerbach 1972 (1842), 203-
204). Despite his praise for Feuerbach's theoretical
contribution, Marx makes it clear that Feuerbach's
critique of Hegel was in some ways inadequate, for he
goes on to announce that the last chapter of his own
work will be devoted to "a critical exposition of the
Hegelian dialectic and general philosophy which . . • is
absolutely essential, for the task has not yet been
accomplished." (Bottomore 64) . Marx's second page of
his Preface (and all published versions of this Preface)
ends by elaborating on the fallacies of Hegel's followers
and critics, who have failed to carry out such a
critique.
On the third page of his preface, however (i.e. the
page published as the first page of the chapter on "Money"),
Marx restates the ontological principle, which Feuerbach
wanted to substitute for Hegel's principle of "Spirit"
( = the collective and individual capacity of human beings

to think self-consciously about themselves and their

they have, rather, an ontological and meta-

proof of being except love or feeling in
general. Only that whose being brings you
J.2l, and whose notbeing, ~, has exis-
tence ••• (Feuerbach 1972 (1842), 225-226)
And Feuerbach 's next thesis, 34, reads in its entirety
as follows:
The new philosophy bases itself on the
truth of love, on the truth of feeling. In

the essence of feeling raised to conscious-

"t1i'ro'ugh the medium of reason: what every man
- every real man -
admits in his heart. It
is the heart made aware of itself as reason.
The heart dema.11.ds real and sensuous objects,
real~sensuous Ee'Ings. (Feuerbach 1972,
( 1842), 226)
Marx 1 s first sentence on page XLI is a summary of these
two theses from Feuerbach 1 s Principles of the Philosophy
of the Future, where Feuerbach introduced his new ontolo-
gical principle contrasting it with the old transcenden-

principle of the capacity of self-conscious thought to
attain absolute knowledge:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
313
If man 1 s feelings, passions, etc. are
not merely anthropological characteristics
in meaning, but are true ontological
affirmations of being (nature), and if they
are only really affirmed in so far as their
object exists as an object of sense, then
it is evident -
(Bottomore 189}.
It should be noted. that Marx introduces this distil-
lation of Feuerbach' s fundamental ontological principle
for a philosophy of the future with an "if"-clause. In
other words, Marx is treating this principle as not yet
proven. He is introducing it as a hypothesis, from which
certain deductions follow (Marx goes on to list and
enumerate five); and we can only accept the fundamental
principle, if these five consequences, which necessarily
and logically follow from this principle, do not them-
selves contradict one another nor the fundamental prin-
ciple itself. What Marx shows on the rest of page XLII
and on the next two pages (XLII-XLIII), which conclude
the written text of Marx's notebook, is that, under the
present social conditions imposed by the· capitalist
mode of produ·cti·on, Feuerbach'·s ·tundamenta1 ontological
principle is indeed contradicted by the c·onsequences
that logically and necessarily follow from it. (See my
"Overview of Contents of Marx's Four Manuscripts" pages
(xii}-(xiii)}. For what is clearly documented in politi-
cal economy is that the only thing capable of satisfying
one's heart's desires - or in Feuerbach Ts words "that
whose being brings you joy, and whose not-being, pain" -
is not love but money. And it is precisely because money


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
314
has this capacity to act as the link between "need and
object, between human life and the means of subsistencea
(Bottomore 190), it also has the power to break those
links - to leave the needy human-being without having
his/her needs satisfied, to leave the moneyless beggar
without the means of subsistence. In this case, money
also has the power to break the natural bonds of human
love, and to render them invalid as an ontological
principle:
If money is the bond which binds me to
human life, and society to me, and which
links 'Ille with nature and man, is it not
the bond of all bonds? Is it not, therefore,
also the universal agent of separation?
It is the real means of Doth separation
and u.."l.ion, the galvano-chemical power of
society. {Bottomore 191~
Wnat then happens to Feuerbach' s ul ti.mate ontological
principle, the power of love to confer existence on its
objects? In the last sentence of page XLIII, Marx gives
the answer:
If you love without evoking love in return,
i.e. if you are not able, by the man.i·fes·ta-
tion of yourself as a loving person, to
make yourself a b"el"ove·d ·pers·on , then your

1nore 194)
If we are right in assuming that all of the last five
pages of Marx's written text in the Third Manuscript
were intended to stand as the Preface to his whole work
(and not just the two pages that presently stand as
this Preface in all the published versions of Marx's
EPM) , then we can understand the overall structure and
purpose of Marx's introduction to his readers as follows:


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
315
1) A survey of the contents of the present work: i.e.
a)
11a careful critical study of political economy", b)
a discussion based on the writings of English, French
and German socialists, and finally c} a thorough and
critical exposition of Hegel's dialectic: 2) a critique
and dismissal of all current German theories which claim
to have done the last, except for the writings of Feuer-
bach; and 3) a vivid account of the role of money in
contemporary society in order to show that even Feuer-
bach' s theoretical revolution can offer no practical
solutions, even though Feuerbach himself claimed that
it did.
The third and last section of Marx's introduction,
which has been transferred by twentieth-century editors
to the middle of the Third Manuscript and made into a
separate chapter on "Money", is absolutely essential
for Marx to accomplish what he needed to accomplish
in his Preface, namely to explain to his readers why
it is worth their while to read the pages that follow
and to prepare them for what they are going to find
in the first chapter. These last three pages of Marx I s
Preface, pages LXI-LXIII, make it clear that the latest
and greatest ideas of Germany• s philosophical tradition
-
a tradition that at that time was far more advanced
than in any other country - cannot accomplish what they
claim to accomplish, namely to enable human beings to
understand themselves, to control and enjoy their phy-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
316
sical environment, and to find joy in their fellow
human-beings. And the reason that this cannot be accom-
plished is because the real world is populated not
merely by physical objects and fellow-human beings, but
also by money. On these last three pages, Marx vividly
portrays the impotence of Germany's highest a..~d most
attractive principles for redesigning the best of all
possible worlds: for what has been ignored in all of
philosophy is the all-pervasive power of money in the
capitalist mode of production, the power to transform
all physical objects that populate the real world
into commodities that can only be acquired for personal
enjoyment through the payment of money, and the power
to force all human beings, on pain of starvation, to
put their best talents and virtues on the market for
sale to the highest bidder, i.e. to the one who possesses
the most money. If Marx can successfully make this
point - and there is universal agreement that these
three pages (XLI-XLIII) form one of the most dramatic,
moving and well-written sections of the EPM - then the
readers will know precisely why they now have to follow
Marx in his undertaking of a "careful critical study
of political economy" and why it is worth their while
to read what Marx has to say, if they themselves want
to come to terms with their own hopes and ideals for
reforming their society. Furthermore, the point that
Marx leaves his readers with in the Preface, exposing


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
317
them to the full implications of a society where every-
one is dependent on access to money for the satisfaction
of his/her needs, is the point where .Marx begins his very
first chapter, namely an analysis of the three basic
ways in which individuals to-day ensure their access
to money -
by selling their labour for wages, by in-
vesting their capital for profit, or by leasing their
land for a rent. In short, once the Preface to Marx's
EPM comes together again, the overall structure of
the work begins to emerge.
11.4
The Little-known Fourth Manuscript is a Sianifi-
cant Datum for Understanding the Overall Structure
of Marx's Economic and Philosophic Manuscriots
of 1844
The Fourth Manuscript, which, like the Second Manu-
script, consists of only four pages forrni:ig a continuous
written text, has not given rise to any supposition of
a 1nissing fourth notebook. This is partly because of
its physical existence -
sewn into the second notebook,
to form the center page of that notebook -
, partly
because its pages are unnumbered, and partly because
the written contents form a self-contained text.
For these reasons, the Fourth Manuscript has not
been treated as the tip of an exciting, missing iceberg.
Quite the opposite -
the Fourth Manuscript has genera-
ted no excitement whatsoever. These four pages contain


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
318
Marx's extensive excerpts from the chapter in which
Hegel claimed to have discovered the possibility of
overcoming the alienated condition of humankind, namely
the last chapter of Hegel's Phenomenology of Mind, en-
titled "Absolute Knowledge 11 • The differences between
Marx's excerpts and Hegel's chapter are restricted to
a few comments and many underlinings that :-Iarx himself
added, and on the other hand, the passages, some indi-
vidual minor words and phrases, and many of Hegel's
original underlinings that Marx himself omitted (Com-
pare Appendix I to Part III, which gives an English
translation of Marx's Fourth Manuscript, with any
English translation of the last chapter from Hegel's
Phenon:.enoloqy, which I will reproduce in an additional
appendix if I get copyright permission).
Nevertheless the additions that Marx made and the
places where he made them, together with his omissions,
are all important clues to figuring out the critique
of Hegel that Marx constructs in his second notebook.
This is the most important contribution of the Fourth
Manuscript, for though Marx's critique of Hegel in the
EPM is very well known and has been frequently cited,
it is recognised to be one of the most difficult
philosophical texts that still is not fully understood.
The Fourt.'1-t Manuscript, containing as it does, a record
of Marx I s careful study of a crucial chapter in Hegel I s
philosophy, indicates why Marx chose to rest his whole
critique in the Third Manuscript on a demolition of

319
a single four-sentence passage from this chapter.
We have already mentioned that the Fourth Manuscript

physically detached, it would make no difference to

Secondly, tt . ..: fact that Marx attached his four-page

suggest, is to clarify the relationship between the

account of alienation. It is this relationship which
makes the methodological perspective developed by

EPILOGUE:
The Continuity Between the
1844 Manuscripts and Capital (1867)
Twenty-three years separate Marx I s first attempts
321
to introduce a new perspective to the science of poli-
tical economy and the completion of his most mature and
systematic presentation of that perspective - Capital,
subtitled, A Critique of Political Economy. The first
volume - and the only one that Marx himself completedl)
-
opens with an analysis of the commodity. The reason
for this, he explains in his Preface, is that
The first chapter of Capital, which lays the
foundation of Marxiari as opposed to classical "bourgeois"
political economy, is an attempt to decode the inner
structure of the cornmodi ty in order to identify the
hidden structure of bourgeois society. This was pre-
cisely the task that Marx had already attempted in his
1844 manuscript, when he carefully examined the rela-
tionships described by Adam Smith among the three
different revenues that formed the whole price of the
commodity. 3 ) The analysis presented in- Capital intro-
duces conceptual tools not yet found in the 1844 manu-
scripts -
(the resolution of the commodity into use-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
322
value and exchange-value ( terms that Smith himself used
to introduce his labour theory of value (Smith 29-30),
the distinction between necessary labour-time and sur-
plus labour-time between variable capital and constant
capital, etc. - but the basic unit of analysis and the
starting-point of tile whole critique is precisely the
same as we have uncovered in the structure of Marx's
first 1844 manuscript, the commodity. Moreover, he
claimed that his original contribution to the clarifi-
cation of the commodity, and hence to the understanding
of political economy as a whole, was the two-fold
character of labour, concrete labour, the specific
result of which is use-values, and abstract labour
(Marx's synonym in 1844 for alienated labour) , which
results in exchange-value. 4)
I was the first to point out and to examnine
critically this two-fold nature of labour
contained in commodities. As this point is
the pivot on which a clear comprehension of
!~!! t~~a~e!~~~~wr turns, we must go into it
Our hypothesis ilTplies that the current controversy
over the status of the 1844 manuscripts as the link
between the Hegelian Marx and Marx, the founder of
the Marxist tradition, restricts and belittles the
significance of these manuscripts. Rather they should
be interpreted in a broader historical context as the
link between the Weal th of Nations, the foundation of
classical liberal economics, and ~, the founda-
tion of the most sustained critique of the liberal


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
323
tradition. As such they provide a valuable insight
into the continuity (and the precise points of dis-
continuity) between "bourgeois 11 and "Marxist" social
science.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
324

1) Only the first volume was completed and published
in his own life-time. Engels prepared the second and
third volumes for publication after Marx's death in

theories of surplus-value, which was then published
as a fourth volume of Capital.
2) Karl Marx, Capital Vol. I: A Critical Analysis of
Capitalist Production (International Publishers, New
York, 1967), p. 8.
3) Those who have argued for the continuity between
the 1844 manuscripts and Capital have noted that the
fourth section of chapter 1 in Capital, entitled
"The Fetishism of Commodities" echoes the social ana-
lysis of the inversion of the world of men and the
world of things first expressed in Marx's theory of
alienation; but they have failed to see that the more
basic link is the commodity its elf.
4) Cf. Marx's letter to Engels, 24 August 1867:
The best things in my book as 1. (on which all
understanding of the facts is based) the
twofold character of labour, emphasized in the
very first chapter, expressed, as it is, in
use-value or exchange value .•.

APPENDIX II
HOW TO UNDERSTAND
THE PAGINATION OF MARX'S 1844 ~OTE300K I
It is almost irn?ossible. to explain the pagination of
Marx's notebook without having a three-dimensional
facsimile to display. The following steps offer the
quickest way of making up such a facsimile cf ½arx I s
notebook I (the First Manuscript). These steps, however,
~
Marx 1 s own procedure in compiling the original
notebook, in as far as Marx wrote and numbered l:is pages
before he sewed them into a notebook.
A. 2. 1 •
The Core-Notebook
Step 1.
Take four sheets cf paper, lay them on top
of one another, and fold the whole lot in two. Staple
them together through the centre fold.
Step 2.
Turn your blank "notebook" sideways so
that the folded edge is at the top. l•Trite I in the top
left-hand corner of the first page.
Step 3.
Turn the whole pad over so that the page
at the bottom of your pad is the one you have just
written I on, and the uppermost page is now blank with
the folded edge at the top. Write II in the top left-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
369
hand corner of this page.
Step 4.
Flip over this page (II) ilnd fold it back.
Turn the whole pad over so that the page you have just
folded over is uppermost (this page will be blank, but
its reverse side has II written on it) and so that the
folded edge is at the top. Write III on the top left-
hand corner of this page.
Step 5.
Turn the whole pad over so that the bottom
page is now III, and the uppermost page is blank with
the folded edge at the top. Write IV in the top left-
hand corner of this page.
Step 6.
Flip over this page (IV) and fold it back.
Turn the whole pad over so that the bottom page and the
uppermost page are both blank (the folded edge should
still be at the top). Write V in the top left-hand cor-
ner of this page.
Step 7.
Continue numbering the pages, repeating
step 5 for the even numbers and step 6 for the odd num-
bers. When you have written the last even number (XVI)
you will find you cannot repeat step 6. The reverse side
of XVI has already been numbered I, and all 16 pages
of the pad have been numbered.
A. 2. 2.
The Outer Sheets
Step 8.
Take five sheets of paper and repeat
step 1.


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
370
Step 9.
Repeat step 2, but write XVII instead of I.
Step 10.
Repeat step 3, but write XVIII instead of
II.
Step .11.
Repeat step 4, but write XIX instead of
III.
Step 12.
Repeat step 5 for even numbers XX through
XXVI, and step 6 for odd numbers XXI through XXVII.
Now play around with each notebook and see if it is
possible to turn the pages of each in such a way that
the numbers read consecutively.
A.2.3.
The Notebook as a Whole
Step 13.
Fit the two pads together by laying the
five-page pad (the outer sheets) flat so that the pages
numbered XVII and XVIII are uppermost, and by placing
the four-pages (the core notebook), also opened out flat,
so that the uppermost pages are the centre pages numbered
X and XI, and the bottom pages lying on the outer sheets
are those numbered I and II. Staple the whole notebook
together through the centre fold, and fold it in two
again.
Step 1 4.
Turn the notebook so that the folded edge
is now the left-hand side. On the outermost page which
is still blank, write Notebook L
Step 15.
Still with the folded edge on the left,


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
turn the page, leave the reverse side of Notebook I
blank, and write Bibliography.
371

A. 3. 2.
Transcription of Marx's List
Most editions of Marx 1 s EPM do carry a bibliography,
but these usually contain additional items inserted by
the editors, and omit some of Marx's own items. I have
never seen any published transcription of Marx 1 s own
list. I have therefore with the assistance of German
friends made my own transcription of the list as it
stands in Marx's notebook.
It is usually extremely difficult to decipher Marx's
script, but since most of the information that Marx gives
in this list of bibliographic items can be found in his
other works on political economy (particularly his
excerpt-notebooks of 1844-1846 and his Theories of Sur-
plus Value), the page here transcribed is the easiest
page of the whole of the EPM to decipher. I have indi-
cated with a double asterisk (**) the words where I am
not 1 00% sure that the transcription is accurate.
I have marked with a single asterisk (*) Marx's own mis-
spellings of the authors or titles that he cites. Marx
does not appear to have distinguished between the French
grave ( ') and acute ('') accents and consistently uses
a grave accent ( ") for both. I have therefore reproduced
Marx's grave accents wherever they occur, including the
words where we would expect an acute accent.
Thus we have tried to reproduce as accurately as
possible ::1arx' s list of bibliographic i terns, just as it


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
375
stands in his own Notebook I. The only additions are the
asterisks {mentioned above) and letters in square
brackets, which were inserted to make the common German
and French abbreviations in Marx's list intelligible to
English readers.
