APPENDIX IV
PROBLEMS IN THE
CONVENTIONAL DATING OF THE EPM
(ca. April -
August 1844)
377
It has always been taken for granted in the secon-
dary literature that Marx's EPM were penned in Paris in
the spring and summer of 1844, i.e. ca. April - August
1844.
Since Marx did not address himself to the field of
political economy until March 1844 (evidence of the ex-
cerpt-notebooks of 1844), it is clear that he cannot
have undertaken his critique of this field until he had
time to immerse himself in the literature of political
economy. The conventional date given for the beginning
of Marx's writing process presupposes that within a
month Marx was able to master his literature. I myself
-- though I have a great admiration for Marx's talent
for devouring vast quantities of literature and coming
up with a comprehensive critique wniie still in the pro-
cess of digesting what he has read -- refuse to attri-
bute to the 26-year old Marx a capacity that I have
never seen in any other fellow human-being. I would cer-
tainly agree that Marx cannot possibly have begun the
critique of Adam Smith that he carries out in his first
EPM ~
April 1844, but I would be very surprised if
he was able to make any sort of beginning as early as


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
378
April 1844. If, however, Marx began his first EPM at some
later date, how could he possibly have finished all four
EPM by August 1844? The answer is simple: he didn't.
The most important "primary" source for the dating
of Marx" s four manuscripts is (once again) the comments
appended by the MEGA editors to the first publication
of their written contents in 1932. In their general
introduction to Manuscripts I, II, and III (see page
21a of my Introduction), t:'J.ey point out that the "Pre-
face" that Marx himself appended to these r::anuscripts,
and which appears on pages IXL-XL of his Third ;;..1anus-
cript, cannot have been written before August 1844.
(They probably give their reasons in the footnotes
at the end of the volume, and unfortunately at present
I do not have a copy of these before me. However, I ex-
pect that these reasons all have to do with the publi-
cation dates of thE. works which are cited by Marx in
this Preface. In any case I do not call the MEGA editors'
assertion on this particular point into question) .
Since the "Preface" makes obvious reference to the
critique of political economy which Marx has carried out
in his first notebook and also to the critique of Hegel
in his other notebook, it is clear that these parts of
the EPM must also have been written before the Preface.
Similarly, Marx mentions in his Preface the French,
English, and German socialist writings that he has re-
lied on, and two of Feuerbach's publications (these in


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
379
passages that Marx then crossed out, so they do not
appear in most published versions of Marx's "Preface"),
so that the parts of the Third Manuscript, which deal
with socialism and with the strengths and weaknesses
of Feuerbach' s critique of Hegel, must also have been
written before the Preface was. All of this adds up to
the fact that the written text of all four manuscripts,
with the sole exception of Marx's analysis of money,
which appears on the very last pages of his Third Manus-
cript (XLI-XLIII), must have been written before the Pre-
face. The dating of the EPM therefore hangs on the dating
of the Preface.
While I certainly accept that the Preface cannot have
been written before August 1844, I reject the assumption
made in the secondary literature, that it cannot have
been written after August 1844. In point of fact, there
are no convincing attempts offered in the secondary
literature to establish August 1844 as the terminal date.
Rather, this is an assumption that has simply passed
into the conventional wisdom, because the MEGA editors
conveniently gave August 1844 as the earliest possible
date for the completion of the EPM -- but the MEGA
editors did not argue that it was also the latest
possible date.
The evidence that I present in the following sub-
sections calls into question only the terminal date
taken-for-granted in the secondary literature for the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
380
completion of Marx's EPM, namely August 1844, but in so
doing, I shall at the same time be opening the possibi-
lity -- indeed the certainty -- that the date given for
Marx I s beginning cannot be as early as April 1844.
A.4 .1.
The Chronological Evidence for the Dating of
Marx's EPM
The 1934 Chronik of Marx's life -- a year-by-year
chronology of biographical events and writings, compiled
by the Marx-Engels-Lenin Institute in Moscow -- gives
the following month-by-month account of the writing of
these manuscripts, together with the sources for this
account.
ca.
Marx writes an economics study on "Wages,
April-May
Profit of Capital, Rent of Land, and Alie-
nation of Labour", which is not completely
finished.
ca.
May-June
MEGA 3, 39-94.
Marx extends his economics study into a
"Critique of Political Economy''; at the
same time he writes on a "Critique of
Hegel's Dialectic and Philosophy in gene-
ral".
MEGA 3, 33.
ca.
Marx finally gives up his plan, announced
July
in the Deutsch-Franz6sische Jahrblicher,
to prepare in detail a "critique of juris-
prudence and political science in the form
of a critique of the Hegelian philosophy
of right", and intends to continue his still
uncompleted work on a critique of political
economy and Hegelian philosophy, "in a series
of different independent brochures, critiquing


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
381
law, morals. politics, etc. and finally in a
special work to present the interconnexions
of the whole, the relationship of the indi-
vidual parts, as well as the critique of
the speculative treatment of that material".
MEGA 3, 33.
ca.
Engels, on his return to Germany from Eng-
28th.August land, visits Marx in Paris and spends 1 0
- ca. 6th.
days with him ... Marx postpones preparing
September
the brochure on political economy together
with an explanation of the Eegelian dialectic
and discusses with Engels a joint pamphlet
against Bruno Bauer and his circle •••
Engels to Marx ca. 8.-10.X.1844,
20. I. 1845. Handw6rterbuch der
Staatswissenschaften, Jena, Bd. IV,
1892, p. 1131 (F. Engels: Marx,
Heinrich Karl) .
F. Engels, "On the History of the
League of Com..'"nunists" in K. Marx,
Enthlillungen ... Hottingen-ZUrich
1885, p. 7.
These are all the entries that the Chronik gives for
the writing of the EPM, a:1.d only the last entry does not
rely on the 1932 MEGA edition. This edition, however,
as
we have already seen, cannot be treateC. as a reliable
source.
Bt:t in fact, the references in the !vi.EGA volume
of 1932 that the Chronik cites as its sources are all
references to the written text of Harx's EPM, partL:::u-
larly the Preface {p. 33 in the MEGA edition), and not
to any discussion of the date this text was written.
The last entry, however, gives a different set of
sources. Since it is this last entry which has apparent-
ly given rise to the supposition that August 1844 is not
merely the earliest possible date for the termination


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
382
of Marx 1 s work on the EPM, but rather the only possible
date, I shall devote the next section to discussing this
entry. Although I have not yet got hold of the sources
cited by the Chronik, my assumption is that the most
reliable of the three sources given for this entry is
the primary source, namely Engels' letter to Marx dated
October 1844.
As I am not at present able to base my discussion
on the exact details of this source, I shall (for the time
being) base it on other evidence of the relationship be-
tween Marx and Engels, a relationship which up to this
point is pr:i:rnarily a relationship between their respec-
tive publications. The most important publication for
my argument is Engels' "Outlines of a Critique of Poli-
tical Economy" (see Struik 1964, 197-226), which was
first published in the 1844 issue of the journal that
Marx edited (and himself contributed articles to),
namely the Deutsch-FranzOsische Jahrblicher.
A.4.2.
Eng~ls' "Outlines of a Criticue of Political
Economy" (1844) and Marx's EPM
I have not yet got hold o=: a copy of the letter f::::-om
Engels to Marx of October 8, 1844, which the Chronik
cites as one of its sources for the last entry cited in
(ii), but I cannot imagine that a:i.ything that Engels may


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
383
have discussed with Marx on his visit to Paris in August
1844 could ever have convinced Marx to abandon his work
on Adam Smith. Quite the opposite -- I would argue that
Marx, despite his more recent exposure to political eco-
nomy than Engels, who had already published several
articles on the topic, was moving in a more radically
critical direction than Engels, and that Engels himself
would have recognized this and encouraged Marx to con-
tinue in this direction. My argument here rests in the
similarity and differences between Engels' critique of
Adam Smith's analysis of the exchangeable value of the
commodity in his 110\ltline of a Critique of Political
Economy" published in 1844 and Marx's critique in the
First Manuscript of the EPM. Engels I argument was still
fettered by the assumption that there was something in-
herent in the physical aspects of the "!:actors of pro-
duction" that allowed one to draw a valid distinction
between the objective conditions of production, i.e.
land, and the subjective energy needed to put those con-
ditions to work, i.e. labour. These two categories, land
and labour, argued Engels, are mutually exclusive and
exhaustive so that there is no need for the third cate-
gory in Smith's analysis of the commodity, namely capi-
tal. Capital is nothing but a derivative category of the
other two more basic categories which are fundamental if
any physical process of production is to occur. Capital,
however, is its elf merely another way of referring to


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
334
these basic categories: it is, on the one hand, the
fruit of the land (including the land's mineral resources)
and on the other hand, the product of labour. Thus Engels
reduced Smith's three "factors of production" to the
opposition of only two, namely land and labour.
Marx's critique of Smith's account of the three
components of value in the EPM goes much deeper than
this, for Marx calls into question the whole notion of
looking for basic social relations in the physical as-
pects of the production process. Like Engels, Marx too
reduces Smith I s three components of the commodity to
two, but unlike Engels, he insists on the historical and
social aspects of the opposition between these two, which
led
him to choose not land and labour as his basic
categories, but labour and capital. From a historical
perspective, argues Marx, the correct opposition to
landed property is not the propertyless worker, but
rather the landless serf (a category which, unlike the
other, excludes the wage-labourer). Similarly the correct
opposite of the propertied capitalist is not the pro-
pertyless worker, but specifically the wage-labourer. Thus
in Marx's perspective, Adam Smith's division of the comma-
di ty into three components, land, labour, and capital,
reveals a truth not about the physical reality, but about
the social and historical reality, namely the evolution
historically of the opposition between capitalist and
wage-labourer out of the earlier opposition between land-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
385
lord and landed serf. Thus Marx interprets Smith not as
a hypocritical apologist of bourgeois society but as the
most correct expression of his own historical times,
when the capitalist mode of production was being born
*
in the decaying womb of European feudal society.
(This point will be elaborated in my Part I below, but
my readers can check for themselves by carefully reading
though Marx's pages XVI-XXI of his Notebook I, Botto-
more 1964, 112-119).
The difference between Marx's analysis and Engels'
analysis, both of which are addressed to one and the
same problem, namely the bourgeois concept of value, has
implications for the dating of Marx's EPM that go
far
beyond the issue of what precisely Engels wrote in Oct.
1844, giving later scholars the impression that on his
visit to Marx in August 1844, he managed to persuade
Marx to give up his critical study of political economy.
These implications go right to the heart of the "Young
Marx" debate, for the following reasons:
Those who argue that there was an "epistemological
break" (between the Marx who wrote the EPM in 1844 and
the Marx who collaborated with Engels in the German
Ideology in 1845-1846) rest their argument very heavily
on Marx's alleged uncritical acceptance of Feuerbach's
philosophical perspective in the EPM. This position
first elaborated by Louis Althusser in 1960 has recently
been restated with dogmatic enthusiasm by Wm. Leogrande


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
386
in Science and Society (1977 and 1978). The argument,
reduced to its simple essentials, is that before 1845
{i.e. in the EPM of 1844 and specifically before Marx's
Theses of Feuerbach dated at the beginning of 1845},
Marx committed the same mistake that he was later to
criticise Feuerbach for in the sixth Thesis on Feuer-
bach, namely "To abstract from the historical processn
(cf. Leogrande 1977, p. 139, who quotes this thesis VI
but argues his point very obscurely} • After seeing this
fault and making it explicit for the first time --
allegedly -- in his Theses on Feuerbach, Marx never
returned to his -- again allegedly -- pro-Feuerbach
enthusiasm of the 1844 EPM.
Now, if we take a closer look at Marx' and Engels'
respective analyses of Adam Smith's three components of
value, and their respective reductions of those three
components to a dialectical opposition { for both Marx
and Engels were well-versed in Hegel's methodology) be-
tween two basic mutually complementary and mutually
contradictory moments, we can see that Engels is the
Feuerbachian and that Marx has moved beyond that per-
spective. For Engels reduced Smith's three components
of the cornrnodi ty to the opposition between man and his
environment. This opposition, which Engels regards as
the ~
opposition, is not historically specific:
it applies to all periods of human histo~ for the
history of the human species is always a history of the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
387
human agents in interaction with their physical environ-
ment. Marx, however, does not regard this universal and
general opposition the ~
opposition. For Marx, the
basic opposition is the historically specific opposition
between capital and wage-labour -- and Marx is fully
aware that what he is treating as the basic opposition
is the historically specific one. This is shown, not
only in the fact that Marx identifies an entirely diffe-
rent opposition as the basis of feudal society, namely
the opposition between landlord and landed serf, but also
in his cri ticisFJ.s of the political economists ( such as
Adam Smith) who treated the contemporary society and
commodity-production as something that corresponded to
the universal givens of nature. ~arx 1 s theory of aliena-
ted labour in the First Manuscript is directed against
the whole concept of the "natural" liberty of mankind
as though this were a universal given, and not merely
to Adam Smith's attempt to identify this natural liberty
with a free market society.
Thus Marx's analysis of the alienation of contem-
porary human beings {i.e. the labouring producers) from
their species-being, which we find in his First Manus-
cript (page XXIV, cf. Bottomore 126-129), is not an
endorsement of the concept of a universal., ahistorical
concept of "species-essence" (as all interpreters of
this passages have tended to assume, be they pro- or
con- the "epistemological break"). Rather it is a


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
388
critique of the whole notion of nalienation from species-
being" especially if this ahistorical notion serves as
the starting-point for a critique of modern society, as
is the case with Feuerbach. Such critiques remain
essentially abstract and idealist, even if -- again
as in the case of Feuerbach -- the critics themselves
claim to be "materialist".
The precision with which Marx insists on this point,
namely that generalized and abstract oppositions cannot
serve as the basic concepts for grasping the real nature
of social reality, is well illustrated in the third page
of his Third Manuscript:
But the antithesis between proPertylessness and
property is still an indeterminate antithesis, which
is not conceived in its active reference to its
intrinsic relations, not yet conceived as a contra-
diction, so long as it is not understood as an anti-
thesis between ~
and capital. Even without the
advanced development of private property, e.g. in
ancient Rome, in Turkey, etc. , this anti thesis may
be expressed in a primitive form. (Bottomore 1964,
152).
This passage reveals not only Marx's rejection of
the generalized categories "property and propertyless-
ness" in favour of the historically specific categories
of "capital and labour", but it also reveals (in the
sentences that follow Marx's opening rejection of the
generalized formulation) Marx's concern to take into
account everything that the historical record can
offer him to specify his own basic categories.
The passage we have just quoted is prefaced by


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
389
)1arx's editorial note "ad p. XXXIX". If our hypothesis
about the cross-references of the first pages of Marx's
Third Manuscript to the corresponding pages in this ~
manuscript is correct, then this refers to the first
page of Marx's Preface (page XXXIX) , where he mentions
Feuerbach's positive contributions to the new critical
socialist research current in Germany, but does not,
on this page XXXIX, -- as he does on page III in his
note modifying his enthusiasm for this current critical
(both social and socialist) research -- mention the ne-
gative aspect, the task that still has to be fulfilled.
This task is to understand and a:ri.alyse the overt gene-
ralized opposition between propertylessness and proper-
ty as the less obvious but far more fundamental opposi-
tion between labour and capital. And this opposition
Marx regards as fundamental precisely because it is
historically specific.
Thus we see that already in his EPM, !-1:arx has
decisively rejected Feuerbach' s perspective which
"abstracts from the historical process". This rejection
becomes especially pointed when we compare two different
analyses of one and the same problem by two different
Hegelians in one and the same year. The one, Friedrich
Engels, is a convert to Feuerbach' s "naterialist" appro-
priation of the Hegelian dialectic and he "solves" the
problem of Smith's three components of value, by re-
ducing them to the basic opposition between the ahisto-


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
390
rical but "materialist" categories of "man" (labour)
and his environment (land) . The other, Karl Marx, has
advanced beyond a perspective which "abstracts from the
historical process" and solves the problem by reducing
it to its historically specific elements, the opposi-
tion between capitalist and wage-labour, an opposition
which (as Marx was to demonstrate in his three-volume
work on Capital) cannot be found in any other period of
human history.

FIRST MA..."i!USCRIPT
{= Marx's "SMITHIAN" notebook)

(Nine double-folios = 36 pages: 27 numbered irregu-

I-XVI
(= 4 center-folios or "CORE NOTEBOOK": 16 pages
numbered and written)
Excerpts, paraphrases, summaries from writings on poli-
tical economy (esp. Adam Smith's Wealth of Nations) re-
organized with Marx' s own commentary into three inde-
pendent texts on Wages of Labour, Profit of Capital and
Rent of Land. These texts appear in the columns with
the corresponding column-headings, with one major ex-
ception: on page VII, the text on wages of labour fills
all three columns of the page, even though the other
two columns retain their column headings, i.e. Profit
of Capital and Rent of Land. (cf. pages XXII-XXVII =
"Alienated Labour").
Published as three sequential chapters: "Wages of
Labour", "Profit of c.ipi tal", "Rent of Land".
XVII-XXVII
(=5 outer double-folios or "OUTER SHEETS": 9 unnumbered
pages, of which two are written on, and 11 pages
numbered and written) .


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
392
The nine unnumbered pages are the first pages of
the whole notebook. The two written pages of the 9
unnumbered pages are the outermost page with the words
"Notebook I", and a list of bibliographic items on the
page of the next double-folio. Neither of these pages
are included in the published versions of the EPM.
On the first five numbered pages (XVII-XX!), the Rent
of Land is the heading of the center column. Only this
column carries a written text, in which Marx discusses
the alienation of land as the origins of private pro-
perty and of European feudalism. The transformation of
feudalism into capitalism is treated as the necessary
development cf private property and the transition from
the alienation of land (the source of the wealth of
feudal estates} to the alienation of labour (the source
of the wealth of nations).
Published as the last section of the chapter "Rent of
Land".
The last six pages (XXII-XXVII) are identical in
format to page VII of the core notebook, i.e. the text
on wages of labour fills all three columns, even though
the other two columns retain their column headings
Profit of Capital and Rent of Land. Marx recapitulates
the analysis he has carried out in his core notebook,
arguing that: "Political economy begins with the fact
of private property; it does not explain it". Marx
proceeds to fill this lacuna in political economy by
introducing a new concept, alienation of labour, to
explain and analyse the fact of capitalist (i.e. fully
developed) private property.
Published as a separate chapter: "Alienated Labour".
Sur.unary of First Manuscriot
(~arx 1 s own characterization in his Preface).
"It is hardly necessary to assure the 1::'eader who is
familiar with political economy that my conclusions
are the fruit of an entirely empirical analysis, based
upon a careful critical study of political economy".

Published under the title: "The Relationship of

.!:.ill.
(Introduced with the reference "ad paq. XXXVI").
This note of reference comments on the appropriate-
ness of Engel.' s characterization of Adam Smith as "the
Luther of political economy 11 , an analogy that Marx
justif.ies on the grounds that Adam Smith was the first
to take labour as his principle of wealth, instead of
equating wealth with external treasures. Smith thereby
"brought man himself into the sphere of private pro-
perty"'. Summary of Smith• s predecessors to confirm that
Smith was the first to do this.
Published as "Private Property and Labour".
~
(Introduced with the references "ad p. XXXIX" and
"~").
The first note of reference (only 5 lines of Marx's
script) argues that only the determinate antithesis,
labour versus capital, -- and not the indeterminate anti-
thesis, propertylessness versus property, which is the
basis of current socialist critiques of capitalism --
is capable of grasping "private property as the developed
relation of the contradiction and thus a dynamic rela-
tion which drives towards its resolution".
The second much longer note (III-XI) criticizes the
current ideas of what a communist society would look
like and shows these ideas fail to get rid of the anti-
thesis between labour ana capital. In order to get rid
of this antithesis and thereby get rid of the capitalist
form of productive activity (= alienated labour), it is
not sufficient merely to abolish private property in
all its developed forms. It is just as important to
reappropriate the acc"umulated content of the develop-
ment of what has been achieved during the rise and de-
mise of the capitalist mode of production. This accu-
mulated content provides the material and social wealth
for satisfying the fully-developed need of the all-round

human being, so that "the natural existence of man has
here become his human existence and nature itself has
become human for him". The idea that unalienat.ed (i.e.
fully developed) human specimen -- in tune with their
world and with themselves -- can only emerge as a result
of the alienated experiences of the previous mernbersor
the human species is the basis and justification of
Hegel's dialectical method. But all those (including
Hegel himself) who have so far applied the dialectical
method to an analysis of the historical develooment of
the human species (tracing the progressive improvements
in the social organization, cultures, world-views, etc.
of successive clusters of human specimen) have so far
ignored the history of industry, a "history which has
not so far been conceived in relation to human nature"_
Marx's analysis of the history of industry (i.e~he
writings of political economy) allows him to comment on

passivity", as follows: "The resolution of the theore-

through practical
means, only through the practical energy of man".

land is symptomatic of the
disappearance of this kind
of wealth: "the complete
victory of working capital
over spendthrift wealth, i.e.
the transformation of all
private property into in-
dustrial capital. . . Thus en-
joyment is subordinated to
capital and the pleasure-
loving individual is subor-
dinated to the capital-accu-
mulating individual, whereas
formerly the contrary was the
case". Thus the joys of the
flesh and the satisfactions
of diverse natural human needs
and desires recede from the
world of capital, its produc-
tion, and are replaced by a
single abstract principle,
i.e. the universal desire to
accumulate the abstract form
of wealth, money. (In an
analogous way, the corporeal

XXIII-XXXIV
(page XXII missing, but
beside the no. XXIII is

Begins by characterizing
"the outstanding achieve-

ce"s1iis own agenda: "We
shall next show in detail
Hegel I s onesidedness and
11mitations, as revealed
402
flesh-and-blood aspects of
humankind -- the bodily needs
and sensuous contacts that
human beings have with one
another and with their physi-
cal environment -- recede

in the final chapter of the

makes 1'these preliminary

Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
Marx summarizes the main
task that Hegel himself wan-
ted to achieve in his final
chapter on "Absolute
Knowledge" and then criti-
cises Hegel 1 s basic pre-
mise: "Man is equated with
self. The Self, however,
is only man conceived ab-
stractly and produced by
abstraction •.. For Hegel,
human life, man, is equi-
valent to sell-conscious-
ness". Marx then copies
out a four-sentence passa-
ge (the concluding senten-
ces of Hegel's opening
paragraph in his chapter
on "Absolute Knowledge")
where Hegel suromarizes
the distilled essentials
of his account of the
overcoming of alienation.
Marx inserts his own num-
bers ( also to be found in
his Fourth Manuscript) di-
viding this four-sentence
passage into eight points,
and proceeds to analyse
critically the first six
of these points. After
completing his detailed
criticism of these first
six points, Marx explains
why "In this discussion
all the illusions of spe-
culation are assembled"
namely, "first that con-
sciousness {knowing as
knowing, thinking as
thinking) claims to be
directly the other of
itself, the seiisuous
world, reality, life; it
is thought over-reaching
itself in thought (Feuer-
bach)". Secondly, that
the supersession of alie-
nation is portrayed as
self-consciousness being
"at home in his other
being. . • Thus reason is
at home in unreason as
403


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
such". Since the last two
points ( 7 & 8) rest on the
validity of the first six
points (now shown by Marx
to be in7alid) , Marx does
not bother to criticise
these, but instead illus-
trates how Hegel's other
writings, the Encyclopedia
of Science and Philosophy
of Right are structured on
the assumption that points
7 and 8 are valid. Thus
Marx shows that when Hegel
applies the dialectical
method to an analysis of
real-world phenomena, what
he ends up analysing are
not these phenomena, not
"the actual religion, state
of nature, but religion it-
self as an object of know-
ledge, i.e. dogmatics; and
similarly with jurisprudence,
political science, and ~-
tural science". In short,
what Hegel takes to be
real-world phenomena are
only the products of other
thinkers, only what pre-
vious philosophers and
scholars have written about
these real-world phenomena.
Raving criticised the limi-
ted way in which Hegel used
the dialectical method and
the concept of alienation
(restricting both of the
developmental processes of
human consciousness and the
way in which thinking beings
construct concepts to under-
stand and cope with the
world around them) , Marx
goes on to the "positive
moments of Hegel's dialec-
tic". One of these Marx
has already mentioned:
namely that Hegel ncontra-
dicts the conventional
conceptions". Marx elabo-
rates this later: "Eegel I s
positive achievement in his
404


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
speculative logic is to
show that the determinate
concepts, the universal
fixed thought-forms •.•
are a necessary result of
the general alienation of
human nature and also of
human thought".
But the more important
point for Marx is Hegel I s
11 insight into the appro-
priation of the objective
being. • • by the destruction
of the alienated character
of the obJective world, by
the annulment of its alie-
nated mode of existence.
In the same way • • • commu-
nism as the annulment of
private property is the
vindication of real human
life as man I s property".
For Marx this is the crucial
si.gnificance of Hegel I s ne-
gation of the negation,
"the positive significance
of the self-referring nega-
tion. • • In short, Hegel con-
ceives labour as man's act
of self-creation (thougE""""'""
in abstract terms) " • Marx
recapitulates Hegel I s account
of this process, showing how
Hegel's fundamental equation,
"man = self-consciousness n
produces an illusory agent,
namely "man's abstract,
pure, absolute being, as
distinguished from himself".
Thus Hegel ignores the real
agent in hwnan history, the
real agent behind "man's
act of self-creation", name-
ly real, living human indi-
viduals who have laboured
and produced ever since human
history and the development
of the human species began.
Marx concludes his critique
of Hegel with a comment on
Hegel I s understanding of
nature -- the objective
world in which the human
think.er lives and moves and
405


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
has his/her being. For He-
g-f>l, this outside world of
nature is its elf a product
406
of thought, "an entity of
thought. . . The whole of
nature, therefore, !:'eiterates
to him the logical abstrac-
tions, but in a sensuous,
external form". The fact that
nature in reality is some-
thing which we cannot equate
with thought is for Hegel an
unfortunate deficiency, that
we should kindly turn a
blind eye to -- "this exter-
nali ty of nature, and its
contrast with thought,
appears as a deficiency ...
nature distinguished from
abstraction appears as
a deficient being". (Marx
indicates the end of his
critique of Hegel, by
drawing a line across the
width of his column).
XXXIV-XXXVIII
(separated from the critique
of Hegel by a line drawn
across the width of the
column).
Marx continues his er ~.tique
of politic al economy at the
point where he left off on
page XXI, commenting on the
advance in political econo-
my since the Physiocrats so
that the landowner is now
regarded as "an unproductive
rentier" (= disappearance
of rent of land as a basic
category in political eco-
nomy) . The present-day eco-
nomist now ignores land as
a factor of production and
"reduces everything to man ,
i.e. to the individual,
whom he deprives of all
characteristics in order
to classify him as a capi-
talist or worker". The only
time that the economists go
beyond this individualistic
outlook is when they talk
about the di vision of labour
and thereby recognize the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
407
social interdependence of
human individuals on one an-
other. Marx characterises
the economists I concept of
the di vision of labour as
"the economic expression of
the social character of
labour within alienation".
But because the economists'
individualistic outlook,
based on the taken-for-gran-
tedness of private property
and private interest, leaves
no room for human beings as
social beings, they "are very
confused and self-contradic-
tory about the division of
labour. Marx illustrates this
by quoting word-for-word
lenghty excerpts from Adam
Smith I s discussion of the
di vision of labour, including
( 1) Smith's circular argument
that the division of labour
presupposes "the propensity
to truck, barter and exchange
one thing for another" (a
propensity that Smith asserts
is "common to all men") while
the act of exchange presuppo-
ses the division of labour;
(2) Smith's argument that
"by nature a philosopher is
not in genius and disposi-
tion. • • so different from
a street-porter" but the re-
sult of the division of
labour which forces one pea-
in-the-pod to specialize in
street-portering. Marx supple-
ments Smith's account with
shorter word-for-word ex-
cerpts from later political
economists (J.B. Say, F.
Skarbek, and James Mill) ,
and then carefully summarizes
everything that he has quoted,
pointing out the advances made
by the later writers over
Smith I s original account of
the division of labour, such
that human labour has come
to be (and to be regarded as)


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
Published as "Critique
of Hegel 1 s Dialectic
and General Philosophy".
XXXIX-XL
408
unthinking "simple mechanical
motion". (In other words,
just as Hegel's account of
thought ( = mental labour)
excludes entirely the real
labouring-process in society,
so do the real labouring-
processes in capitalist so-
ciety exclude any thought
whatsoever, on the part of
the labourer. This mutual
exclusion of mental and ma-
nual labour is the result
of the capitalist di vision
of labour which Marx has de-
fined as "the social charac-
ter of labour within aliena-
tion").
Published as "Needs, Pro-
duction and Division of
Labour".
{Page XXXIX is the only page in the Third Manuscript
which carries a title. This underlined title at the
top of the left-hand column is "Vorrede" (= Preface
or Foreword) • The script continues to the bottom of
page XL and there is nothing in Marx I s original manus-
cript to indicate that the three pages that follow
belong to a different part of the notebook} .
This Preface is clearly not a summary of the contents
of Marx'snotebooks for his own clarification, but,
on the contrary, it is addressed to his anticipated
reading public. He explains why he has not yet carried
out his promised critique of politics and acknowledges
that "in the present work" political topics "are
touched upon only to the extent that political econo-
my itself expressly deals with these subjects". He goes
on: "It is hardly necessary to assure the reader who
is fa1Uiliar with political economy that :ny conclusions
are the fruit of an entirely empirical analysis, based
upon a careful critical study of political economy".
He mentions the work of contemporary French, English,
and German socialists, including Engels' "Outlines for
a critique of political economy;' (1844). He notes that
all of this work owes its inspiration to Feuerbach' s
writings, "the only writings since Hegel's Phenomena-
~
and Logic which contain a real theoret~-
lution". Marx explains that his "critical exposition


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
409
of the Hegelian dialectic and general philosophy, which
forms the final chapter of the present work, is abso-
lutely essential", because those who have not undertaken
this task have continued to wheel and deal in abstrac-
tions, despite their criticisms of Hegel for doing
precisely this. The crossed-out passages (reproduced
in Easton & GudC.hat 1967, 284-287) are all comments
developing this criticism of the current vogue in Ger-
many among the Young Hegelians.
Published (right at the beginning of the EPM, before
the First Manuscript) as "Preface".
XLI-XLIII
(= continuation of Preface)
Marx elaborates under five points the ontological prin-
ciple that takes "man I s feelings, passions, etc." as
"true ontological affirmations of being (nature)".
(This is the ontological principle proposed by Peuerbach
to replace the ultimate principle of Hegel I s philosophy,
a philosophy which was premised on the assumption that
the unique capacity of human beings for self-reflexive
thought (= self-consciousness) is the only valid onto-
logical affirmation of being and nature) • Marx's last
point, which follows from the other four, states that:
"5. the meaning of private property -- released from
its alienation -- is the existence of essential objects
for man, as objects of enjoyment and activity". Marx
then goes on to show that under the present mode of
production, there is only one essential object, namely
money. Money is not only the means through which each
member of this society satisfies his needs; it also
gives the possessors of money, natural qualities which
they really didn't have. "What I am and can do, is
therefore, not at all deter!'l'l~.nedby my individuality.
I am ugly, but I can buy th12 most beautiful woman for
mys°elf. Consequently I am not ~, for the effect of
ugliness, its power to repel, is annulled by money.
As an individual I am larrte, but money provides me
with twenty-four legs. 7A reference to Adam Smith 1 s
comment "A very poor man may be said in some sense to
have a demand for a coach and six; he might like to
have it; but his demand is not an effectual demand,
as the commodity can never be brought to market in or-
der to satisfy it" (Smith 56)). Therefore I am not lame".
Marx then goes on to comment on the effect that money
has on the relationships of wo/man to wo/rnan .. "Money,
then, appears as a disruptive power for the individual
and for the social bonds ••• It changes fidelity into


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
410
infidelity, love into hate, hate into· love, virtue
into vice, vice into virtue, servant into master,
stupidity into intelligence, and intelligence into stu-
pidity". In contrast (Marx concludes} "Let us assume
man to be man, and his relation to the world to be a
human one. 'rhen love can only be exchanged for love,
trust for trust, etc .... if you wish to influence
other people you must be a person who really has a
stimulating and encouraging effect upon others. Every
one of your relations to man and to nature must be a
specific expression, corresponding to the object of
your will, of your real individual life. If you love
without evoking love in return, i.e. if you are not
able, by the manifestations of yourself as a loving
person, to make yourself a beloved person, then your
love is impotent and a misfortune".
This passri.ge, which concludes the five pages that
Marx hL-nself intended to stand as the Preface to his
whole work, unambiguously reveals that Feuerbach' s
own solution to the alienat€d condition of man --
namely abolish God, so that man can bestow his love
on man and no longer waste it on a supernatural being
-- is inadequate to accomplish what Feuerbach himself
laid down as the criterion of truth: love.
In Thesis 35 of his "Principles of the Philosophy
of the Future, Feuerbach wrote: "The old philosophy
maintained that that which could not bethought of
also did not exist; the new philosophy maintains that
that which is not loved 'or cannot be loved does not
exist...
Love is not only objectively but also sub-
jectively the criterion of being, the criterion of
truth and reality. Where there is no love there is also
no truth. And only he who loves something is also some-
thing -- to be nothing and to love nothing is one and
the same thing. The more one is, the morP ~me loves,
and vice versa" .
Thus, the Preface that stands in Marx's original
notebook, five pages long, is a far more appropriate
way of introducing his readers to his work than the
two pages (XXXIX-XL) published by later editors as
the Preface. Not only does it introduce the contents
of Marx's EPM -- a critique of political economy, a
commentary ()11 current socialist ideas, and a critique
of the Hegelian dialectic (all mentioned on the first
two pages) -- but it also sets up the problematic of
the whole EPM, namely how is the theoretical revolution,
begun in Hegel's Phenon:enology and Logic and continued
in Feuerbach' s writings, to be translated into the prac-
tical transformation of contemporary society that is de-
sired by the socialist movement? The major stumbling
block to the recovery of natural human and social
relations (caring for the needs of one another) is the
total dependence of everyone, who participates in the


Reproduced with permission of the copyright owner.  Further reproduction prohibited without permission.
411
capitalist mode of production, on the possession of
money to satisfy all of his/her human needs {physical,
social., cultural). This major stumbling-block is ana-
lysed in Marx's first notebook where Marx examines
the three basic types of monetary income which enable
members of a commodity-producing society to satisfy
their survival- and other needs: wages of labour,
profit of capital, and rent of land. (See First Manus-
cript above) •
Published as the chapter separating the final (pub-
lished} chapter on "Critique of Hegel's Dialectic and
Philosophy in General" from the preceding (published)
chapter on "Needs, Production, and Division of Labour",
under the chapter heading of "Money11 •
Followed by 23 unnumbered, blank pages.

comes out of pure knowledge-both qua existence and

self-cancelling, self-transcending. The one of the two
pa_?-ts of the opposition is the disparity between exis-
tence within itself, in its individuality, and unh-ersality;
the other, disparity between its abstract uniYersality
and the self. The former dies to its self-existence, and
relinquishes itself, makes confession: the latter renounces
the rigidity of its abstract uui.-ersality, and thereby dies
to its lifeless self and its inert uni.-ersalitv; so that the
former is completed through the moment Of universality,
which is the essence, and the latter through unh-ersality,
which is self. By this process of action spirit has come
1 ~=.~~he for~ ~f /as~-:~2-:~~~!:::;htr
ia simvle unity of knowledge. It is through action that
spirit is spirit so as definitely to exist; it raises its
e~tence into the sphere of thought and hence into
absolute opposition, and. returns out of it through and
~in this very opposition.
(!Z.l'hus, then, what was in religion content, or a way
of imagining (Vorstellen) an other, is here the action
propcrofthesclf. ~:i.i.be.~~

m,:-~~-t~ .;Gl\e.-,., as we see. ~~

.nd -alt·existence,-the-:knowl~--~
flnW'tan('€' anrl i)f the·Sub~R.!!C'e aa·this knowlt"'1g,i,rof
~J,Ct.i~ \Vhat we ha,e done here. in addition, is
simply to gather together the particular moments, each
of i..hich in principle e:s:hibits the life of spirit in its
entirety, and again to secure the notion in the form of
the notion, whose content was cfuclosed in these mo-
ments, and "hich had already pre5ented itself in the
~rm of a mode or shape of consciousness.
~)!'his last embodiment of spirit-spirit which at once
gi,es its comp!rtc and true content the form of self,
and thereby rcaliz.es its notion, and in doing so remains

the mere withdrawal of self-consciousness into its pure

for and. the ri.::,ing of existence, which has the subst2.nce
for its essential ri.ature anri subsists on its own account.
But this subsisting of existence for itself is the notion
established in determinate form, and is thereby the
notion's own inherent mo>ement-that of descending
into the simple substance, which is only subject by
being this negati•ity and going through this process.
Ego has not to take its stand on the form of self-
consciousness in opposition to the form of substantia.lity
and objecti.:ity, as if it were afraid of relinquishing or
externalizing itself. The power of spirit lfos rather in
remaining one with itself when giring up itself, ·and,
because it is self·contained and self-subsistent, in
establishing as mere moments its explicit self-existence
as well as its implicit inherent nature-. Nor again is

rather in the seeming inactirity which merely wakhea
bow v.hat is distinguished. is self-moved by its very
nature and returns again into it:s c;.;·n unity.
